and~\ref{mj:sec:lj-moments} ask for the bulk profile and the exact
supercritical moment constants. Those are the starting points of Part~IV.

That distinction is essential. A boundary tail of the form $c/\sqrt k$ does
not by itself identify $\ljE D_n/\sqrt n$: events with $D_n$ proportional to
$n$ contribute at precisely the same order. Part~IV analyzes those events
directly, rather than extrapolating the boundary approximation. On the
ordinary probability scale $D_n/n\to0$; on the scale of probabilities
multiplied by $\sqrt n$, macroscopic deficits remain visible, and it is this
second scale that governs all moments of order greater than $1/2$. Part~IV
builds on the subject and elementary decompositions of Parts~I--III but
assumes none of their probabilistic or spectral theorems; this makes the
dependency on the existing research particularly narrow and auditable.

\subsection{The macroscopic profile and moving-bound enumeration}
Define, for $0<u\le1$,
\begin{equation}
 \Psi(u)=
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\frac3{\sqrt\pi}\frac{1-2u}{\sqrt{u(1-u)}},&0<u<\tfrac12,\\[6pt]
 0,&\tfrac12\le u\le1.
 \end{cases}
 \label{mj:eq:Psi}
\end{equation}
$\Psi$ is continuous on $(0,1]$. The zero at $1/2$ and beyond is part of the
definition, not a claim that the finite probability vanishes there.

\begin{theorem}[Complete first-order macroscopic profile; both manuscripts]
\label{mj:thm:profile}
For every $0<u\le1$,
\begin{equation}
 \sqrt n\,\ljP(D_n>un)\longrightarrow\Psi(u).
 \label{mj:eq:profile-limit}
\end{equation}
More generally, for every integer sequence $d_n$ with $d_n/n\to
u\in(0,1)$,
\begin{equation}
 \sqrt n\,\ljP(D_n\ge d_n)\longrightarrow\Psi(u).
 \label{mj:eq:profile-sequence}
\end{equation}
For each fixed $\delta>0$ the convergence in \eqref{mj:eq:profile-limit}
is uniform in $u\in[\delta,1]$, and so is that of
$u\mapsto\sqrt n\,\ljP(D_n\ge\lceil nu\rceil)$. Consequently, for fixed
$1/2<\theta<1$,
\begin{align}
 \frac{a_n^{(\lfloor\theta n\rfloor)}}{C_n}
 &\sim\frac{3(2\theta-1)}{\sqrt{\pi\theta(1-\theta)}}\,n^{-1/2},
 \label{mj:eq:bulk-ratio}\\
 a_n^{(\lfloor\theta n\rfloor)}
 &\sim\frac{3(2\theta-1)}{\pi\sqrt{\theta(1-\theta)}}\,
       \frac{4^n}{n^2}.
 \label{mj:eq:bulk-count}
\end{align}
For fixed $0<\theta\le1/2$ the ratio is $o(n^{-1/2})$ and the count is
$o(4^n/n^2)$; no positive asymptotic equivalent in that range is asserted.
\end{theorem}

Manuscript~10 proves \eqref{mj:eq:profile-limit} with uniformity on
$[\delta,1]$ and both consequences; manuscript~11 proves the sequence form
\eqref{mj:eq:profile-sequence}, compact uniformity and \eqref{mj:eq:bulk-ratio}.
The first proof (manuscript~10) solves a renewal equation for a marked
measure (\cref{mj:sec:renewal}); the second (manuscript~11) sums fixed outer
words of a canonical threshold decomposition (\cref{mj:sec:words}). The
zero part of $\Psi$ is a first-order \emph{asymptotic} cutoff, not a finite
combinatorial prohibition. The increasing permutation, for example, has
$M_n=1$ and $D_n=n-1$. Such permutations lie beyond the cutoff, but have
negligible mass on the $n^{-1/2}$ scale.

\subsection{The marked measure behind the coefficient three}
Put
\[
 \mathcal X=(0,1]\times[0,1],\qquad
 \Xi_n=(D_n/n,L_n/n),\qquad v(y)=\min(y,1-y).
\]
The following locally finite measure is infinite near the missing boundary
$x=0$ of the deficit coordinate; it is not a probability measure.

\begin{theorem}[Marked rare-event measure; manuscript~10, last assertion both]
\label{mj:thm:marked}
For every continuous function $f$ on $[0,1]^2$ that vanishes on some strip
$\{0\le x\le\delta\}$ of the deficit coordinate, $\delta>0$,
\begin{align}
 \sqrt n\,\ljE f(\Xi_n)&\longrightarrow\mu(f),\label{mj:eq:marked-limit}\\
 \mu(f)&=\frac1{4\sqrt\pi}\int_0^1
 \frac{f(v(y),0)+f(v(y),1-y)+f(v(y),1)}
 {[y(1-y)]^{3/2}}\,\dd y.
 \label{mj:eq:marked-measure}
\end{align}
Equivalently, $\sqrt n\,\ljLaw(\Xi_n)$ converges vaguely on $\mathcal X$ to
$\mu$. The deficit marginal of $\mu$ has density
\begin{equation}
 \rho(x)=\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}[x(1-x)]^{-3/2}\one_{(0,1/2)}(x).
 \label{mj:eq:rho}
\end{equation}
That is, on the locally compact space $(0,1]$ the measures
$\mu_{D,n}=\sqrt n\,\ljLaw(D_n/n)$ converge vaguely to
\begin{equation}
 \mu_D(\dd x)=\rho(x)\,\dd x=\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}
 \frac{\one_{(0,1/2)}(x)}{[x(1-x)]^{3/2}}\,\dd x,
 \label{mj:eq:measure}
\end{equation}
and $\mu_D([u,1])=\Psi(u)$ for $u>0$. The measure $\mu_D$ has infinite mass
near zero and is not a probability measure.
\end{theorem}

The three summands describe a last entry close to the low end, a last entry
in the interior, and a last entry close to the high end. Their equal weights
are a theorem of the renewal calculation, not an independence assumption
about endpoints. The last assertion is manuscript~11's Theorem~1.2, proved
there from the profile (\cref{mj:sec:words-measure}); manuscript~10 obtains
it as the deficit marginal (\cref{mj:sec:profile-proof}).

\subsection{Exact moment constants and the mean-carrying law}
\begin{theorem}[Every supercritical moment; both manuscripts]
\label{mj:thm:moments}
For every fixed real $p>1/2$,
\begin{equation}
 \ljE D_n^p\sim\mathfrak m_p\, n^{p-1/2},
 \label{mj:eq:moment-equivalent}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{align}
 \mathfrak m_p
 &=\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}\int_0^{1/2}
          x^{p-3/2}(1-x)^{-3/2}\,\dd x\label{mj:eq:kappa-beta}\\
 &=\frac3{\sqrt\pi}\int_0^1
       \left(\frac{z^2}{1+z^2}\right)^{p-1}\dd z.
 \label{mj:eq:kappa-t}
\end{align}
In particular,
\begin{align}
 \ljE M_n&=n-\frac3{\sqrt\pi}\sqrt n+o(\sqrt n),
 \label{mj:eq:mean-M}\\
 \mjVar(D_n)=\mjVar(M_n)
 &\sim\frac{3(4-\pi)}{4\sqrt\pi}\,n^{3/2}
  =\frac3{\sqrt\pi}\left(1-\frac\pi4\right)n^{3/2}.
 \label{mj:eq:variance}
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

The two printed forms of the variance constant are manuscript~10's and
manuscript~11's; they are equal. Both manuscripts state both integral forms
of $\mathfrak m_p$; manuscript~10 adds the size-biased law below, which at
$p=1$ carries the same information as the integral.

Define deficit-size bias by
\begin{equation}
 \ljP_n^\star(B)=\frac{\ljE[D_n\one_B]}{\ljE D_n}.
 \label{mj:eq:size-bias}
\end{equation}
This is a probability measure on the same finite permutation space.

\begin{theorem}[A uniform law under deficit-size bias; manuscript~10]
\label{mj:thm:uniform}
Under $\ljP_n^\star$,
\begin{equation}
 \sqrt{\frac{D_n}{n-D_n}}\ \Longrightarrow\ \operatorname{Uniform}[0,1].
 \label{mj:eq:uniform-law}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, the limiting distribution function of $D_n/n$ under this
bias is
\begin{equation}
 H^\star(x)=
 \begin{cases}
 0,&x\le0,\\
 \sqrt{x/(1-x)},&0<x<1/2,\\
 1,&x\ge1/2.
 \end{cases}
 \label{mj:eq:mean-cdf}
\end{equation}
Thus half the leading mean contribution comes from deficits above $n/5$:
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\ljE[D_n;D_n>n/5]}{\ljE D_n}\longrightarrow\frac12.
 \label{mj:eq:half-mean}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

Because $\mathfrak m_1=3/\sqrt\pi$, manuscript~11's moment formula at $p=1$
and manuscript~10's size-biased law carry the same information: the
size-biased density $x\rho(x)/\mathfrak m_1$ is the derivative of
$H^\star$, whose total mass one is the statement $\mathcal I(1)=1$ of
\cref{mj:sec:closed-forms} (the merge's comparison).

\subsection{What makes the constant three}
\label{mj:sec:three}
Both manuscripts explain the coefficient $3$ in $\Psi$, from different
decompositions.

\emph{Manuscript~11: outer words.} The combinatorial proof isolates one
split into two macroscopic blocks, preceded by a finite outer word of two
possible operations. One operation adds a finite block on the left of a
maximum; the other appends a new maximum. The total critical weight of all
outer words is $4$. However, words containing an append operation are
accepted only when the terminal right block has a last entry near its high
endpoint. That event has asymptotic probability $1/2$. Words without an
append operation have total weight $2$; words with one or more appends also
have total weight $2$. The accepted weight is therefore
\begin{equation}
 2+\frac12\cdot2=3.
 \label{mj:eq:three-intro}
\end{equation}
Neither ignoring append operations nor treating every append as harmless
gives the correct constant. The endpoint condition is not a secondary error
term.

\emph{Manuscript~10: a renewal with three endpoint channels.} Integrating
the Catalan root-split density alone gives only the source term $\nu$ of
\eqref{mj:eq:nu} (\cref{mj:rem:heuristic}): small outer decorations recur,
and appending a maximum can erase a rare deficit. The resulting renewal
equation has the solution \eqref{mj:eq:marked-measure}, whose three equally
weighted summands are the three endpoint channels; \cref{mj:sec:small}
separates the folding factor two from the three channels. (On tests of the
deficit alone the source is a third of the answer; \cref{mj:sec:three-match}
identifies the three channels with manuscript~11's three endpoint modes. Both
observations are the merge's.)

\subsection{Scope of the advance}
The main profile and moment results answer the two explicit repository
questions just identified. The marked endpoint limit, the size-biased
uniform law, the lower bounds of \cref{mj:sec:lower}, and the conditional
outer-word and split laws of \cref{mj:sec:conditional} are further results.
All needed finite facts are rederived. In particular, no asymptotic claim
from an unrefereed repository report is assumed in the proofs; the
finite-state recurrence is used only for computations and is attributed to
its prior source. Manuscript~10's proof is self-contained at the level of
finite Catalan decompositions, elementary generating functions, Stirling's
formula, and convergence of measures: an exact root identity eliminates the
left block's internal jump statistic, and a uniform counting bound controls
the otherwise dangerous mesoscopic remainder. Manuscript~11's proof is by
exact Catalan counting, endpoint estimates, and a tail bound at the
rare-event scale, a different asymptotic decomposition for a bound
proportional to $n$; in neither is a fixed-bound spectral asymptotic
substituted into a varying-bound problem.

Neither manuscript claims the exact total-variation constant, a constant
term in the critical half-moment, a local limit for individual macroscopic
lattice masses, or the optimal scale below the half-size cutoff. [Added 30 September
2026, batch 64: Part~V proves the constant term (\cref{cjm:thm:critical}).] The
positive bulk equivalent concerns adjacency bounds $m/n\in(1/2,1)$; the
smaller-bound bulk regime and the critical window at $m=n/2$ remain open.
Nor does this work resolve the fixed-$m$ component-ordering problem of
Mayama--Akita.

\section{Exact identities at the maximum and at the endpoints}
\label{mj:sec:root}

\subsection{The Catalan maximum decomposition}
Both manuscripts start from the maximum decomposition of Part~I
(\cref{sec:endpoints}), which Part~III also rederives
(\cref{lj:sec:preliminaries}): every $\pi\in\Av_n(132)$ is uniquely
\[
 \pi=(\sigma+b)\,n\,\tau,
 \qquad \sigma\in\Av_a(132),\quad\tau\in\Av_b(132),\quad a+b+1=n,
\]
and every such pair produces an avoider. Hence, for the left and right block
sizes of $\Pi_n$ (manuscript~10),
\begin{equation}
 \ljP(\text{left size }a,\ \text{right size }b)=\frac{C_aC_b}{C_n},
 \label{mj:eq:split-weight}
\end{equation}
and, conditional on their sizes, the standardized blocks are independent and
uniform. Summing gives $\Cat=1+x\Cat^2$. If $\pi$ is $m$-bounded and $b>0$,
then $a+1\le m$ (manuscript~11's positional restriction), because the entry
after $n$ is at most $b$: this is Part~I's \eqref{eq:max-position}. Both
manuscripts rederive the decomposition and the restriction by the argument of
Part~I; it is not repeated.

\subsection{The exact deficit recursion}
\begin{proposition}[The exact deficit recursion; manuscript~10]
\label{mj:prop:root}
Let $\pi=(\sigma+b)\,n\,\tau$ as above. If $b\ge1$, then
\begin{align}
 M(\pi)&=\max\{n-F(\tau),M(\tau)\},\label{mj:eq:M-root}\\
 D(\pi)&=\min\{F(\tau),a+1+D(\tau)\},\label{mj:eq:D-root}\\
 L(\pi)&=L(\tau).\label{mj:eq:L-root}
\end{align}
If $b=0$ and $n\ge2$, then
\begin{equation}
 D(\pi)=\min\{D(\sigma)+1,L(\sigma)\},
 \qquad L(\pi)=n.
 \label{mj:eq:append-root}
\end{equation}
For $a>0$, the first-entry deficiency satisfies
\begin{equation}
 J(\pi)=n-F(\pi)=a+1-F(\sigma);
 \label{mj:eq:first-root}
\end{equation}
for $a=0$, it is zero.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $b\ge1$. The downward boundary jump from $n$ to $F(\tau)$ is
$n-F(\tau)\ge n-b=a+1$. Every jump inside the left block is at most $a-1$,
and the jump from the left block into $n$, when present, is at most $a$.
Therefore none of these can be largest. Only the downward boundary and
jumps internal to the right block remain, proving \eqref{mj:eq:M-root}.
Subtracting the maximum from $n$ gives \eqref{mj:eq:D-root}; the last value
is unchanged. If $b=0$, appending $n$ introduces the single new jump
$n-L(\sigma)$, and subtraction gives \eqref{mj:eq:append-root}. Finally,
$F(\pi)=b+F(\sigma)$ when $a>0$, proving \eqref{mj:eq:first-root}.
\end{proof}

This is stronger than an asymptotic simplification. It explains why an
arbitrary left decoration contributes only its Catalan count in the later
analysis. The last endpoint cannot likewise be discarded: the minimum in
\eqref{mj:eq:append-root} is exactly where a putative large deficit can be
clipped away. (The recursion is the one-step form of the state
transformations \eqref{lj:eq:state-right}--\eqref{lj:eq:state-append} of
Part~III, which hold there under a first-entry hypothesis on a large hole;
\eqref{mj:eq:D-root} and \eqref{mj:eq:append-root} hold for every avoider.)

\subsection{First entries: a uniformly exponential tail}
\label{mj:sec:first}
The first-entry count
\begin{equation}
 \#\{\pi\in\Av_N(132):F(\pi)=f\}=\frac{N-f+1}{N}\binom{N+f-2}{N-1},
 \qquad1\le f\le N,
 \label{mj:eq:first-count}
\end{equation}
is Part~III's \eqref{lj:eq:first-count}, which is Part~I's \eqref{eq:h}
reindexed (\cref{lj:rem:first-entry}). Manuscripts~10 and~11 rederive it
exactly as Part~III does: the maximum decomposition gives the bivariate
first-value series \eqref{lj:eq:first-gf}, the substitution
$(x,z)\mapsto(xu,u^{-1})$ gives the deficiency form
\eqref{lj:eq:deficiency-gf}, deficiency $j$ has generating function
$(x\Cat(x))^{j+1}$, and Lagrange inversion applied to $\Cat=1+x\Cat^2$
gives $[x^k]\Cat(x)^q=\frac{q}{2k+q}\binom{2k+q}{k}$. The new content is a
pair of bounds uniform in the size, which remain useful when the event
being studied has probability only of order $n^{-1/2}$.

\begin{lemma}[A uniform first-deficiency bound; both manuscripts]
\label{mj:lem:first}
For $n\ge1$ and $0\le j\le n-1$,
\begin{equation}
 \ljP(J_n=j)\le(j+1)2^{-j-1}\le(j+1)2^{-j},
 \qquad \ljE J_n\le8,
 \label{mj:eq:first-tight}
\end{equation}
and for every integer $h\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \ljP(J_n\ge h)\le(h+2)2^{-h}.
 \label{mj:eq:first-tail}
\end{equation}
In particular $\ljP(J_n\ge h)$ is bounded by an absolute constant times
$(h+1)2^{-h}$, uniformly in $n$.
\end{lemma}
Manuscript~10 states the weaker bound $(j+1)2^{-j}$, $\ljE J_n\le8$ and the
last sentence; manuscript~11 states $(j+1)2^{-j-1}$ and
\eqref{mj:eq:first-tail}. The proofs are the same computation.
\begin{proof}
For $n\ge2$, divide the count \eqref{mj:eq:first-count} with $f=n-j$ by
$C_n$:
\[
 \ljP(J_n=j)=
 (j+1)\frac{n+1}{2(2n-1)}
 \prod_{r=0}^{j-1}\frac{n-1-r}{2n-2-r}.
\]
Indeed the ratio $\binom{2n-2}{n-1}/\binom{2n}{n}$ is $n/(2(2n-1))$, and
lowering the first upper index a further $j$ times multiplies it by the
product. The empty product is one, each product factor is at most $1/2$, and
$(n+1)/(2(2n-1))\le1/2$. Thus $\ljP(J_n=j)\le(j+1)2^{-j-1}$ (manuscript~10
bounds the prefactor $(j+1)(n+1)/(2(2n-1))$ by $j+1$ instead). Summing the
infinite geometric-arithmetic tail gives
$\sum_{j\ge h}(j+1)2^{-j-1}=(h+2)2^{-h}$. For $n=1$, the deficiency is zero
and every inequality holds directly. Finally $\sum_{j\ge0}j(j+1)2^{-j}=8$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Last entries: two endpoint modes}
\label{mj:sec:last}
The last-entry count
\begin{equation}
 \#\{\pi\in\Av_N(132):L(\pi)=\ell\}=C_{N-\ell}C_{\ell-1},
 \qquad1\le\ell\le N,
 \label{mj:eq:last-count}
\end{equation}
is Part~III's \eqref{lj:eq:last-count}, from the unique decomposition
$\pi=(\tau+\ell)\,\sigma\,\ell$ with $|\tau|=N-\ell$, $|\sigma|=\ell-1$
(\eqref{lj:eq:last-decomposition}); both manuscripts rederive it that way,
noting that triples crossing the separated blocks cannot form $132$.

\begin{lemma}[The last entry has two endpoint limits; both manuscripts]
\label{mj:lem:last}
As $n\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}
 L_n/n\Longrightarrow\tfrac12\delta_0+\tfrac12\delta_1.
 \label{mj:eq:last-limit}
\end{equation}
More precisely, for every fixed $\eta\in(0,1/2)$, uniformly for integer
$r\in[\eta N,(1-\eta)N]$,
\begin{equation}
 \ljP(L(\Pi_N)\ge r)=\frac12+O_\eta(N^{-1/2}),
 \label{mj:eq:last-half}
\end{equation}
and the probability that $L(\Pi_N)/N$ belongs to $[\eta,1-\eta]$ is
$O_\eta(N^{-1/2})$.
\end{lemma}
Manuscript~10 proves \eqref{mj:eq:last-limit}, manuscript~11 the
quantitative form; the two proofs differ.
\begin{proof}[Proof (manuscript~10)]
For fixed $j\ge0$, Stirling's formula and \eqref{mj:eq:last-count} give
\[
 \ljP(L_n=j+1)\longrightarrow C_j4^{-j-1},\qquad
 \ljP(L_n=n-j)\longrightarrow C_j4^{-j-1}.
\]
Each family of limiting masses sums to $\frac14\Cat(1/4)=1/2$. For a fixed
endpoint cutoff the two families are disjoint for large $n$. Letting the
cutoff tend to infinity leaves no asymptotic mass in the middle. This proves
\eqref{mj:eq:last-limit}.
\end{proof}
\begin{proof}[Second proof, with the uniform estimate (manuscript~11)]
The distribution \eqref{mj:eq:last-count} is symmetric under
$\ell\mapsto N+1-\ell$. For $\eta N\le\ell\le(1-\eta)N$, the Catalan bounds
\eqref{mj:eq:catalan-bounds} below bound each probability by
$K_\eta N^{-3/2}$. Summing at most $N$ terms gives the middle-mass bound.
Symmetry puts one half of the remaining mass at each end, proving
\eqref{mj:eq:last-half} uniformly in the stated range.
\end{proof}

In particular, the first deficiency has an exponentially bounded tail,
whereas the last entry converges after division by its size to an equal
mixture of the endpoints zero and one. No independence between the two
endpoints, or between $D_n$ and $L_n$, has been asserted or will be needed.
Later, the fact that $D_n/n\to0$ in probability will make its macroscopic
value deterministic inside a large unconditioned block, which is a different
argument.

\subsection{Uniform Catalan estimates}
Manuscript~11 records the following bounds. Stirling's formula and
adjustment of finitely many small indices give absolute constants
$c_-,c_+>0$ such that
\begin{equation}
 c_-(j+1)^{-3/2}\le c_j\le c_+(j+1)^{-3/2},
 \qquad
 c_j=\frac{1+O(j^{-1})}{\sqrt\pi\,j^{3/2}}\quad(j\to\infty).
 \label{mj:eq:catalan-bounds}
\end{equation}
(Manuscript~10 proves $c_h\le(h+1)^{-3/2}$ directly, in the proof of
\cref{mj:lem:Ttail}.) All constants denoted by $K$ are absolute unless a
subscript specifies otherwise; they can be enlarged from line to line. For
$N\ge n/2$, \eqref{mj:eq:catalan-bounds} implies
\begin{equation}
 \frac{C_N}{C_n}\le K4^{N-n}.
 \label{mj:eq:catalan-ratio}
\end{equation}
These inequalities are used as bounds, and the asymptotic formula only when
all involved block sizes tend to infinity uniformly. The numbers $c_j$
decrease strictly (\eqref{lj:eq:domination}), so
$C_{n-t}/C_n\ge4^{-t}$ for $0\le t<n$.

\section{Tail envelopes at the rare-event scale}
\label{mj:sec:envelope}
The proof of a rare-event limit needs an upper bound that survives
multiplication by $\sqrt n$. Ordinary weak convergence of $D_n/n$ is not
enough. Both manuscripts obtain such a bound from finite counting, without
using Part~III's algebraic boundary law, by two different decompositions.

\subsection{Stopping words and a deterministic deficit bound}
Manuscript~10 uses Part~III's three reconstruction operations $R_\beta$, $A$
and $T_\tau$ (\cref{lj:sec:skeleton}), with costs $|\beta|+1$, $1$ and
$|\tau|+1$. It calls Part~III's skeletons \emph{stopping words}: a word in
$R_\beta,A$ followed by one $T_\tau$, applied from the terminal operation
outward. It has total cost $t$ and one unexpanded hole; filling that hole
with a size-$N$ avoider produces size $N+t$.

\begin{lemma}[Disjoint families and a deterministic deficit bound]
\label{mj:lem:stopping}
Fix $n$ and $h<n/2$. Reconstructions from different stopping words of cost
$t\le h$ are disjoint, and the reconstruction map is injective in the
size-$(n-t)$ hole. Every such reconstruction satisfies
\begin{equation}
 D(\pi)\le t-1.
 \label{mj:eq:stopping-deficit}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Disjointness and injectivity are Part~III's \cref{lj:lem:injective};
manuscript~10 rederives them by the same argument (the block containing the
hole is larger than its finite sibling at every maximum decomposition, so
the word and the hole are read off from the final permutation).

For \eqref{mj:eq:stopping-deficit}: at the terminal, the jump from the
maximum to the first value of $\tau$ has size at least $N+1$. Every outer
operation preserves all existing internal differences, so the final maximum
jump is still at least $N+1$. Thus $D\le(N+t)-(N+1)=t-1$. Notably, no
first-entry condition on the hole is needed for this one-sided assertion,
unlike the exact identity of Part~III's \cref{lj:lem:reconstruction}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{An elementary bound for the exact cost tail}
The stopping words of cost $t$ are counted by Part~III's $b_t$, with
generating function $B(x)=(\Cat(x)-1)^2$ (\eqref{lj:eq:cost-gf}): the
numerator of $x(\Cat-1)/(1-x\Cat-x)$ counts terminals and the denominator an
arbitrary prefix. In particular $B(1/4)=1$, so the weights $b_t4^{-t}$ form
the probability law of Part~III's cost $T$; and $b_t=C_{t+1}-2C_t$ for $t\ge1$,
$b_0=0$ (\eqref{lj:eq:b-t}). Manuscript~10 rederives all of this.

\begin{lemma}[Exact tail and an elementary bound]
\label{mj:lem:Ttail}
For every integer $h\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \ljP(T>h)=\frac{2(h+1)(2h+1)}{h+2}\frac{C_h}{4^h}
 \le\frac4{\sqrt{h+1}}.
 \label{mj:eq:Ttail}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The equality is Part~III's \cref{lj:lem:cost-tail}, proved there, and in
manuscript~10, from $c_{j+1}/c_j=(2j+1)/(2j+4)$,
$\sum_{j=0}^hc_j=2-(2h+1)c_h$ and $b_t4^{-t}=4c_{t+1}-2c_t$ for $t\ge1$.
For the bound (manuscript~10), $c_h\le(h+1)^{-3/2}$ by induction, because
\[
 (2h+1)^2(h+2)\le4(h+1)^3.
\]
The prefactor in \eqref{mj:eq:Ttail} is at most $4(h+1)$, proving the bound.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The uniform envelope}
\begin{proposition}[Uniform finite-size deficit envelope; both manuscripts]
\label{mj:prop:envelope}
For $1\le d\le n$, put $h=\min\{d,\lfloor(n-1)/2\rfloor\}$. Then
\begin{align}
 \ljP(D_n\ge d)
 &\le\ljP(T>h)\label{mj:eq:exact-envelope}\\
 &\le\min\left\{1,\frac{4\sqrt2}{\sqrt d}\right\}.
 \label{mj:eq:uniform-envelope}
\end{align}
Consequently $D_n/n\to0$ in probability and, for every $0<u\le1$,
\begin{equation}
 \sqrt n\,\ljP(D_n>un)\le4\sqrt2\,u^{-1/2}.
 \label{mj:eq:scaled-envelope}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
Manuscript~10 proves the explicit form given here. Manuscript~11 proves
$\ljP(D_n\ge d)\le K/\sqrt d$ for $n\ge2$, $1\le d\le n-1$, with an
unspecified absolute constant (\cref{mj:cor:all-tail}), by a different
route.
\begin{proof}
By \cref{mj:lem:stopping}, the disjoint short-word families have total
probability $\sum_{t\le h}b_tC_{n-t}/C_n$ and all satisfy $D_n<d$. Since
$c_j$ decreases, $C_{n-t}/C_n=4^{-t}c_{n-t}/c_n\ge4^{-t}$. Their mass is
therefore at least $\ljP(T\le h)$, proving the first inequality. Because
$h+1\ge d/2$, \cref{mj:lem:Ttail} proves the second. Use
$d=\lfloor un\rfloor+1>un$ to obtain the scaled bound, with the zero event
handled separately when $u=1$.
\end{proof}

The same stopping construction occurs in Part~III's boundary-law proof. Its
use here is deliberately weaker and cleaner: the preserved terminal jump
supplies a tail bound directly, without reconstructing the exact limiting
deficit or controlling an exceptional set of hole endpoints.

\subsection{A canonical threshold parser}
\label{mj:sec:parser}
Manuscript~11 decomposes a bounded permutation differently. Its outer words
use only the operations $R_\beta$ (with $\beta$ an arbitrary avoider,
including $R_\varnothing$ at cost one) and $A$, with Part~III's costs; an
\emph{outer word} is a finite word in these decorated operations, including
the empty word, written outermost operation first, so that reconstruction
applies it from right to left. Its cost is the sum of operation costs, and
every operation preserves avoidance.

Fix integers $n,m$ with
\begin{equation}
 n/2\le m\le n-1,\qquad d=n-m.
 \label{mj:eq:threshold-range}
\end{equation}
Let $\mathcal E_{n,m}$ be the event $M_n\le m$. For an $m$-bounded
permutation, start at its maximum decomposition. As long as a child block
has more than $m$ entries, follow that block. A large right child records an
$R_\beta$ operation. A large left child must have an empty right sibling, by
the positional restriction, and records an $A$ operation. Stop at the first
node whose two child blocks both have size at most $m$.

\begin{lemma}[Canonical parsing and reconstruction; manuscript~11]
\label{mj:lem:parse}
Every permutation in $\mathcal E_{n,m}$ has a unique representation
\begin{equation}
 w\bigl((\sigma+b)\,N\,\tau\bigr),
 \qquad a=|\sigma|,\quad b=|\tau|,
 \label{mj:eq:parse}
\end{equation}
where $w$ is an outer word of cost $t$, and
\begin{equation}
 0\le t\le d-1,\qquad N=n-t>m,
 \qquad a+b=N-1,\qquad 0\le a,b\le m.
 \label{mj:eq:parse-sizes}
\end{equation}
The word, all its decorations, and the terminal pair are recoverable from
the permutation. For any fixed word of cost $t\le d-1$, reconstruction from
terminal pairs satisfying \eqref{mj:eq:parse-sizes} is injective. Whenever
the reconstructed permutation is $m$-bounded, its canonical word is exactly
the prescribed word.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
At every stage the current block has more than $m$ entries. Under
\eqref{mj:eq:threshold-range}, two children of more than $m$ entries are
impossible by their total size; for bounded permutations the positional
restriction also rules out a nonempty right child next to a left child of
more than $m$ entries. The stated rule therefore gives a unique next
operation whenever parsing continues. The followed size strictly decreases.
At size $m+1$ both children have at most $m$ entries, so parsing has stopped
by then. This proves $N\ge m+1$ and $t\le d-1$.

Each maximum decomposition recovers its standardized children, so it also
recovers every decoration and the terminal pair. Conversely, the core in
\eqref{mj:eq:parse} and every intermediate core have more than $m$ entries.
An $R$ decoration has size at most $t-1\le d-2<m$, while an $A$ operation has
no right sibling. Hence the same parsing rule follows the reconstructed core
at each operation and stops exactly at the specified terminal pair. This
proves injectivity and the final assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The terminal pair is not a boundary-law skeleton]
The absorbing skeleton of Part~III (manuscript~10's stopping word) stops at a
finite right block next to a giant left block. The threshold parser stops
when \emph{both} blocks lie below the adjacency threshold. Under a
macroscopic deficit, these terminal blocks are themselves macroscopic. The
two parsers serve different limits and must not be identified.
\end{remark}

Let $w_t$ count all individually decorated outer words of cost $t$, and let
$w^R_t$ count those containing no $A$ operation. The generating function of
one $R$ operation is $z\Cat(z)$, including $R_\varnothing$ at cost one.
Therefore
\begin{align}
 \mathcal W(z):=\sum_{t\ge0}w_tz^t
    &=\frac{1}{1-z\Cat(z)-z}=\Cat(z)^2,
 \label{mj:eq:W}\\
 \mathcal W_R(z):=\sum_{t\ge0}w^R_tz^t
    &=\frac{1}{1-z\Cat(z)}=\Cat(z).
 \label{mj:eq:WR}
\end{align}
Both identities follow from $z\Cat^2=\Cat-1$. In particular,
\begin{equation}
 w_t=C_{t+1},\qquad w^R_t=C_t.
 \label{mj:eq:word-counts}
\end{equation}
At $z=1/4$ these series converge, with $\mathcal W(1/4)=4$ and
$\mathcal W_R(1/4)=2$. This critical summability will allow the finite outer
word to be summed without a spectral approximation. (Part~III's cost series
factors as $B(z)=\mathcal W(z)\cdot z(\Cat(z)-1)$: an outer word followed by
a terminal $T_\tau$, so the two parsers share their
outer operations. This remark is the merge's.)

\subsection{Tightness of the canonical cost}
\label{mj:sec:tightness}
The main technical point is not merely that the outer word is usually short
under some limiting construction. Its tail must be negligible
\emph{relative to a probability of order $n^{-1/2}$}. The following exact
majorant supplies that missing uniformity. Let
\begin{equation}
 Q_{N,m}=\sum_{\substack{a+b=N-1\\0\le a,b\le m}}C_aC_b
 \label{mj:eq:Q}
\end{equation}
count terminal pairs before checking the boundary jumps.

\begin{lemma}[Terminal split bound; manuscript~11]
\label{mj:lem:Q}
For $m<N\le2m+1$, with $r=N-m\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 Q_{N,m}\le\frac{K C_N}{\sqrt r}.
 \label{mj:eq:Qbound}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For $r=1$, the full Catalan recurrence gives $Q_{N,m}\le C_N$. For $r\ge2$,
every admissible pair has $a,b\ge r-1$. By symmetry we may sum the half with
$a\le b$ and multiply by two. On that half, $b+1\ge N/2$, and the Catalan
bounds give
\[
 \frac{C_aC_b}{C_N}\le\frac{K}{(a+1)^{3/2}}.
\]
The sum over $a\ge r-1$ is at most $K/\sqrt r$, by comparison with the
integral of $u^{-3/2}$. This proves \eqref{mj:eq:Qbound}.
\end{proof}

On $\mathcal E_{n,m}$, write $T_{n,m}$ for the cost of the canonical outer
word. It need not be defined outside that event.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform tail of the canonical cost; manuscript~11]
\label{mj:lem:cost-tail}
Under \eqref{mj:eq:threshold-range}, for every integer $h\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \ljP(\mathcal E_{n,m},\ T_{n,m}>h)
 \le K\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt d\sqrt{h+1}}+\frac1d\right\}.
 \label{mj:eq:rare-tail}
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
 \ljP(\mathcal E_{n,m})\le\frac{K}{\sqrt d}.
 \label{mj:eq:global-upper-half}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Discard every boundary constraint after applying the injective parser. For
each cost $t$, there are $w_t$ words and at most $Q_{n-t,m}$ terminal pairs
per word. Since $n-t\ge m+1\ge n/2$, \cref{mj:lem:Q},
\eqref{mj:eq:catalan-ratio} and \eqref{mj:eq:word-counts} give
\begin{align}
 \ljP(\mathcal E_{n,m},T_{n,m}>h)
 &\le \frac1{C_n}\sum_{h<t\le d-1}C_{t+1}Q_{n-t,m}\notag\\
 &\le K\sum_{h<t\le d-1}
       \frac{1}{(t+1)^{3/2}\sqrt{d-t}}.
 \label{mj:eq:convolution-bound}
\end{align}
For $t\le d/2$, the sum is at most $Kd^{-1/2}\sum_{t>h}(t+1)^{-3/2}$, hence
at most $K/\sqrt{d(h+1)}$. For $t>d/2$, put $r=d-t$. This part is at most
\[
 Kd^{-3/2}\sum_{1\le r\le d/2}r^{-1/2}\le Kd^{-1}.
\]
These bounds prove \eqref{mj:eq:rare-tail}. Starting the same sum at $t=0$
proves \eqref{mj:eq:global-upper-half}, after enlarging $K$ for $d=1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[An all-deficit envelope; manuscript~11]
\label{mj:cor:all-tail}
For every $n\ge2$ and $1\le d\le n-1$,
\begin{equation}
 \ljP(D_n\ge d)\le\frac{K}{\sqrt d}.
 \label{mj:eq:all-tail}
\end{equation}
In particular, $D_n/n\to0$ in probability.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}[Proof, and second proof of \cref{mj:prop:envelope} up to the constant]
For $d\le n/2$, apply \eqref{mj:eq:global-upper-half} with $m=n-d$. For
$d>n/2$, use monotonicity and the bound at $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$. Since
$d\le n$, the resulting $K/\sqrt n$ is at most a constant multiple of
$d^{-1/2}$. Enlarge $K$ to cover the small values of $n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The order of truncations]
If $d_n/n\to u>0$, \eqref{mj:eq:rare-tail} yields
\[
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}\sqrt n\,
 \ljP(\mathcal E_{n,n-d_n},T_{n,n-d_n}>h)\le K_u(h+1)^{-1/2}.
\]
Thus one first fixes a finite cost cutoff, takes $n\to\infty$, and only then
lets the cutoff grow. Ordinary unscaled tightness would not justify this
interchange. This estimate is the reason the finite-word calculation of
\cref{mj:sec:words} determines the complete leading constant.
\end{remark}

\section{First proof of the profile: a renewal equation for the marked measure}
\label{mj:sec:renewal}
This section and the next are manuscript~10's proof of
\cref{mj:thm:marked}, and with it of \cref{mj:thm:profile}.

\subsection{Test functions and the append projection}
It is enough initially to consider a Lipschitz function $f$ on $[0,1]^2$
that is zero whenever the deficit coordinate satisfies $x\le\delta$, for
some $\delta>0$. Set
\begin{equation}
 Z_n(f)=\sqrt n\,\ljE f(D_n/n,L_n/n).
 \label{mj:eq:Zn}
\end{equation}
By \eqref{mj:eq:scaled-envelope}, this sequence is bounded, with a bound
depending only on $\delta$ and $\|f\|_\infty$. Define
\begin{equation}
 S(x,\ell)=(\min(x,\ell),1),\qquad S^\ast f=f\circ S.
 \label{mj:eq:S}
\end{equation}
Then $S^2=S$, hence $(S^\ast)^2=S^\ast$. Also $S^\ast f$ is Lipschitz and
vanishes on the same low-deficit strip.

The macroscopic source functional is
\begin{equation}
 \nu(f)=\frac1{8\sqrt\pi}\int_0^1
 \frac{f(v(y),0)+f(v(y),1-y)}{[y(1-y)]^{3/2}}\,\dd y.
 \label{mj:eq:nu}
\end{equation}
Its apparent endpoint singularities cause no problem: the numerator is zero
when $v(y)\le\delta$.

\begin{lemma}[Truncated renewal with a controlled remainder]
\label{mj:lem:renewal}
Put $r_a=C_a4^{-a-1}$. For each admissible Lipschitz $f$ there is a finite
constant $K_f$ such that, for every sufficiently large integer $H$,
\begin{multline}
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}\left|
 Z_n(f)-\sum_{a=0}^H r_aZ_{n-a-1}(f)
       -\frac14 Z_{n-1}(S^\ast f)-\nu(f)\right|\\
 \le K_f(H+1)^{-1/2}.
 \label{mj:eq:renewal-approx}
\end{multline}
\end{lemma}

\subsection{Proof of the truncated renewal}
\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{mj:lem:renewal}: small left blocks, the identity transition]
Fix $H$ and use the exact root decomposition. If $0\le a\le H$ and
$b=n-a-1$, \cref{mj:prop:root} gives
\[
 D_n=\min\{b-J_b,a+1+D_b\},\qquad L_n=L_b
\]
inside the conditional expectation. The distance between this normalized
state and $(D_b/b,L_b/b)$ is bounded by an absolute constant times
$(a+1+J_b)/n$. By \cref{mj:lem:first}, its expected value is $O_H(n^{-1})$.
After multiplying by $\sqrt n$, the Lipschitz error tends to zero. Moreover
\[
 \frac{C_aC_b}{C_n}\sqrt{\frac nb}\longrightarrow C_a4^{-a-1}=r_a.
\]
Boundedness of $Z_b(f)$ therefore gives the small-left contribution
$\sum_{a=0}^H r_aZ_{n-a-1}(f)+o(1)$.

\emph{An empty right block: the append transition.}
For $b=0$, the exact state differs from
$S(D_{n-1}/(n-1),L_{n-1}/(n-1))$ by $O(n^{-1})$. The coefficient
$(C_{n-1}/C_n)\sqrt{n/(n-1)}$ tends to $1/4$. Thus this contribution is
$\frac14 Z_{n-1}(S^\ast f)+o(1)$. If $1\le b\le H$, then $D_n\le F_b\le H$
exactly, so its contribution is zero for all sufficiently large $n$.

\emph{Two macroscopic blocks: the source term.}
Choose a fixed $0<\eta<\delta/4$ and consider $\eta n\le a,b\le(1-\eta)n$.
Set $y=a/n$. The exact recursion and the endpoint lemmas give, uniformly in
these sizes,
\[
 D_n/n-\min(a/n,b/n)\longrightarrow0
 \quad\hbox{in probability},
\]
and the last coordinate is asymptotically $0$ or $b/n$, each with
probability $1/2$. Indeed $J_b/b\to0$ and $D_b/b\to0$ uniformly for
$b\ge\eta n$, while $L_b/b$ has the two-point limit of \cref{mj:lem:last}.
Because the other coordinates have deterministic limiting values, no
endpoint independence is required. The uniformity follows directly from
convergence of the corresponding scalar sequences as their integer index
tends to infinity, and uniform continuity of $f$ on a compact square.

Stirling's formula gives, uniformly on the same interval,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{C_aC_b}{C_n}
 =\frac{1+o(1)}{4\sqrt\pi\,n^{3/2}}
   [y(1-y)]^{-3/2}.
 \label{mj:eq:split-asymptotic-10}
\end{equation}
The replacement of $b/n=1-y-1/n$ by $1-y$ has vanishing effect. A Riemann
sum therefore gives the source integral over $[\eta,1-\eta]$, which is
exactly \eqref{mj:eq:nu}, since its integrand vanishes outside
$[\delta,1-\delta]$.

\emph{Mesoscopic left blocks: the indispensable error bound.}
It remains to treat $H<a<\eta n$; the case $b<\eta n$ contributes zero
because $D_n\le b<\delta n$. On the support of $f$, the root identity forces
\[
 a+1+D_b>\delta n,
 \qquad\text{hence}\qquad D_b>\delta n/2
\]
for large $n$. By \cref{mj:prop:envelope}, its conditional probability is
$O_\delta(n^{-1/2})$. In this range $b$ is a fixed positive fraction of $n$,
so the Catalan ratio also gives
\[
 \frac{C_aC_b}{C_n}\le K r_a
\]
for an absolute constant $K$ depending at most on $\eta$. After
multiplication by $\sqrt n$, the entire mesoscopic contribution is bounded
by
\[
 K'_{f,\delta}\sum_{a>H}r_a
 =O_{f,\delta}((H+1)^{-1/2}).
\]
Here the summable tail follows either from Stirling or from
$c_a\le(a+1)^{-3/2}$ proved earlier. Combining all cases proves
\eqref{mj:eq:renewal-approx}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why a formal split heuristic is insufficient]
\label{mj:rem:heuristic}
Simply integrating the Catalan root-split density would give the source
$\nu$, not the answer $\mu$. Small outer decorations recur, and appending a
maximum can erase a rare deficit. Moreover, without the uniform tail
envelope, the range $H<a=o(n)$ cannot be discarded after multiplication by
$\sqrt n$. The proof handles both issues explicitly.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Solving the renewal equation without assuming convergence}
The Catalan identity gives
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{a\ge0}r_a=\tfrac14\Cat(1/4)=\frac12.
 \label{mj:eq:mass-r}
\end{equation}
Formally, a limit should satisfy
$\mu(f)=\frac12\mu(f)+\frac14\mu(S^\ast f)+\nu(f)$. We now prove the limit
exists rather than inserting this equation as an assumption.

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{mj:thm:marked} for Lipschitz tests]
First put $g=S^\ast f$, so that $S^\ast g=g$, and let
$\bar Z_g=4\nu(g)$. All $Z_n(g)$ are bounded. Write
$\bar r_H=\sum_{a=0}^H r_a$ and $e_g=\limsup_n|Z_n(g)-\bar Z_g|$.
Applying \cref{mj:lem:renewal} to $g$ and subtracting $\bar Z_g$ yields
\[
 e_g\le(\bar r_H+\tfrac14)e_g
       +K_g(H+1)^{-1/2}
       +(\tfrac12-\bar r_H)|\bar Z_g|.
\]
Let $H\to\infty$. By \eqref{mj:eq:mass-r}, $e_g\le\frac34e_g$. Since $e_g$
is finite, it is zero. Thus
\begin{equation}
 Z_n(S^\ast f)\longrightarrow4\nu(S^\ast f).
 \label{mj:eq:projected-limit}
\end{equation}

Next set $\bar Z_f=2\nu(f)+2\nu(S^\ast f)$ and
$e_f=\limsup_n|Z_n(f)-\bar Z_f|$. The already established convergence of the
projected sequence gives
\[
 e_f\le\bar r_He_f+K_f(H+1)^{-1/2}
             +(\tfrac12-\bar r_H)|\bar Z_f|.
\]
Letting $H\to\infty$ now gives $e_f\le\frac12e_f$, hence $e_f=0$. Therefore
\begin{equation}
 \mu(f)=2\nu(f)+2\nu(S^\ast f).
 \label{mj:eq:resolvent}
\end{equation}
Since $f$ vanishes at deficit zero and $v(y)\le1-y$,
\[
 S^\ast f(v(y),0)=f(0,1)=0,
 \qquad S^\ast f(v(y),1-y)=f(v(y),1).
\]
Substituting into \eqref{mj:eq:nu} proves precisely
\eqref{mj:eq:marked-measure}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Extension to continuous tests}
An admissible continuous $f$ can be approximated uniformly by Lipschitz
functions supported away from $x=0$, using piecewise-linear approximation on
a rectangular mesh and a Lipschitz cutoff in the first coordinate. The scaled
finite measures have uniformly bounded mass on every strip $x\ge\delta$ by
\eqref{mj:eq:scaled-envelope}, and the limiting integral has finite mass
there. Consequently the approximation error in both integrals is bounded by a
constant times the uniform approximation error. Letting that error tend to
zero proves the full theorem.

\subsection{A reusable two-state resolvent}
The calculation is an elementary renewal principle. If the total identity
weight is $w_{\mathrm{id}}$, the weight of an idempotent endpoint operator
$S^\ast$ is $w_S$, and $w_{\mathrm{id}}+w_S<1$, the analogous equation has
the solution
\begin{equation}
 \mu(f)=\frac{\nu(f)}{1-w_{\mathrm{id}}}
       +\frac{w_S}{(1-w_{\mathrm{id}})(1-w_{\mathrm{id}}-w_S)}\nu(S^\ast f).
 \label{mj:eq:general-resolvent}
\end{equation}
The same bounded-sequence limsup proof establishes convergence whenever an
analogue of \cref{mj:lem:renewal} is available. Here $w_{\mathrm{id}}=1/2$
and $w_S=1/4$, so both coefficients in \eqref{mj:eq:resolvent} equal $2$.
This algebraic principle is not itself advertised as a new general theorem;
the work is in proving the appropriate source and uniform remainder for this
statistic.

\subsection{Evaluating the profile}
\label{mj:sec:profile-proof}
For a test depending only on the deficit, all three summands in
\eqref{mj:eq:marked-measure} agree. Folding $y$ and $1-y$ onto $(0,1/2)$
gives the density \eqref{mj:eq:rho}. Thus
\begin{equation}
 \mu\{x>u\}=\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}\int_u^{1/2}
          [x(1-x)]^{-3/2}\,\dd x
 \quad(0<u<1/2).
 \label{mj:eq:tail-integral}
\end{equation}
The derivative identity
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd}{\dd x}
 \frac{1-2x}{\sqrt{x(1-x)}}
 =-\frac1{2[x(1-x)]^{3/2}}
 \label{mj:eq:derivative}
\end{equation}
evaluates the integral as \eqref{mj:eq:Psi}. The limiting measure has no
mass at any fixed positive deficit, including $1/2$. Continuous upper and
lower approximations to the tail indicator therefore prove
\eqref{mj:eq:profile-limit}.

For uniformity on $[\delta,1]$, both the finite tails and $\Psi$ are
nonincreasing, and $\Psi$ is continuous. Choose a finite mesh on which its
oscillation is small, use pointwise convergence at the mesh points, and
sandwich between adjacent points. This proves the uniform assertion in
\cref{mj:thm:profile}. Finally \eqref{lj:eq:bridge}, continuity of $\Psi$,
and $C_n\sim4^n/(\sqrt\pi n^{3/2})$ yield the moving-bound formulas
\eqref{mj:eq:bulk-ratio} and \eqref{mj:eq:bulk-count}, including their floor
conventions. (The sequence form \eqref{mj:eq:profile-sequence} follows from
\eqref{mj:eq:profile-limit} in the same way, by monotonicity and continuity
of $\Psi$; manuscript~11 proves it directly.)

\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=.9\linewidth]{figures/04-macroscopic-jump-bulk_profile.pdf}
\caption{Manuscript~10: the proved macroscopic tail profile $\Psi$ and
uniform Catalan sampling illustrations at $n=1{,}024$ and $16{,}384$, from the shipped simulation
data of \cref{mj:tab:simulation}. Error bars show two
estimated Monte Carlo standard errors; they do not bound finite-size bias and
are not rigorous confidence certificates. In particular, positive
finite-size tails beyond $u=1/2$ are compatible with the limiting zero
there.}
\label{mj:fig:profile-mc}
\end{figure}

\section{Second proof of the profile: the canonical threshold decomposition}
\label{mj:sec:words}
This section is manuscript~11's proof of \cref{mj:thm:profile} and of the
deficit part of \cref{mj:thm:marked}. It uses the parser of
\cref{mj:sec:parser} instead of the renewal equation.

\subsection{The asymptotic contribution of one outer word}
Fix a sequence $d_n/n\to u\in(0,1/2)$ and put $m_n=n-d_n$. In this subsection
$w$ is one fixed, fully decorated outer word, of fixed cost $t$. Define its
endpoint acceptance weight by
\begin{equation}
 \chi(w)=
 \begin{cases}
 1,&w\text{ contains no }A,\\
 1/2,&w\text{ contains at least one }A.
 \end{cases}
 \label{mj:eq:acceptance}
\end{equation}
Let $\mathcal E_n(w)$ be the event that $M_n\le m_n$ and the canonical word
is $w$. On this event, let $a$ be the left size at the terminal split and
define $U_n=a/n$. Write
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak k(y)=[y(1-y)]^{-3/2},\qquad
 I(u)=\int_u^{1-u}\mathfrak k(y)\,\dd y.
 \label{mj:eq:kernel}
\end{equation}

\begin{proposition}[Fixed-word contribution; manuscript~11]
\label{mj:prop:fixedword}
For every continuous function $g$ on $[0,1]$,
\begin{equation}
 \sqrt n\,\ljE\bigl[\one_{\mathcal E_n(w)}g(U_n)\bigr]
 \longrightarrow
 \frac{\chi(w)4^{-t}}{4\sqrt\pi}
       \int_u^{1-u}g(y)\mathfrak k(y)\,\dd y.
 \label{mj:eq:fixedword}
\end{equation}
The random variable inside the expectation is set to zero off
$\mathcal E_n(w)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{mj:prop:fixedword}: the endpoint step]
The terminal core has size $N=n-t$ and is $(\sigma+b)N\tau$, with
$a+b=N-1$ and $a,b\le m_n$. Fix a small $\eta>0$ such that
$[u+\eta,1-u-\eta]$ is nonempty, and first consider splits with
\begin{equation}
 u+\eta\le a/n\le1-u-\eta.
 \label{mj:eq:interior}
\end{equation}
For large $n$, both $a$ and $b$ are bounded below by a positive multiple of
$n$, and each lies below $m_n$ by a positive multiple of $n$.

All internal jumps in the two terminal blocks are at most $m_n$ simply
because their sizes are at most $m_n$. The jump from the left block into $N$
is at most $a$. The jump from $N$ into the right block equals
\begin{equation}
 N-F(\tau)=a+1+J(\tau).
 \label{mj:eq:terminal-jump}
\end{equation}
By \cref{mj:lem:first}, the fraction of terminal pairs for which $J(\sigma)$
or $J(\tau)$ exceeds a sufficiently small fixed multiple of $n$ is
exponentially small, uniformly over \eqref{mj:eq:interior}. On the
complementary set, \eqref{mj:eq:terminal-jump} is at most $m_n$.

The first deficiency of the terminal core is $J(\sigma)+1$. An append
operation increases a current first deficiency by one. An $R_\beta$ operation
either makes the first entry the new maximum, when $\beta$ is empty, or makes
its new first deficiency at most the operation cost, when $\beta$ is
nonempty. Therefore, throughout the fixed outer word, the current first
deficiency is at most $J(\sigma)+t+1$. Each new downward jump at an $R$
operation is at most $J(\sigma)+2t+1$. Each finite decoration and its jump
into the adjacent maximum have bounded size. These contributions are
harmless on the same good set for large $n$.

If $w$ contains no $A$, all constraints are now satisfied. Thus the accepted
fraction of the $C_aC_b$ terminal pairs is $1-o(1)$, uniformly on
\eqref{mj:eq:interior}.

Suppose instead that $w$ contains an $A$. Apply the word from the inside out
and let $s_w$ be the total cost of the $R$ operations encountered before the
first such $A$. Those operations preserve the terminal last entry $L(\tau)$.
The first append creates a jump bounded by $m_n$ exactly when
\begin{equation}
 L(\tau)\ge \ell_n(w):=N+s_w+1-m_n
                  =d_n-t+s_w+1.
 \label{mj:eq:append-threshold}
\end{equation}
After this append, the last entry is a maximum at least $N$. Subsequent $R$
operations preserve that value, and every later append creates a jump of
size at most $t$. Thus no further macroscopic last-entry condition is
introduced.

In the interior range, $\ell_n(w)/n\to u$, while both $\ell_n(w)$ and
$b-\ell_n(w)$ are bounded below by positive multiples of $n$. By
\cref{mj:lem:last}, the fraction satisfying \eqref{mj:eq:append-threshold}
is $1/2+O_{u,\eta,w}(n^{-1/2})$. Removing the exponentially small bad
first-deficiency set changes this by $o(1)$. We have not assumed that the
first and last entries are independent: deleting any event of probability
$o(1)$ changes the last-entry probability by at most $o(1)$.

We have proved that, uniformly over \eqref{mj:eq:interior}, the accepted
fraction of terminal pairs is
\begin{equation}
 \chi(w)+o(1).
 \label{mj:eq:accepted-fraction}
\end{equation}
By \cref{mj:lem:parse} these are exactly the pairs counted by
$\mathcal E_n(w)$, rather than merely a possibly overlapping construction.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{mj:prop:fixedword}: counting and boundary strips]
For fixed $t$ and $a+b=n-t-1$, Stirling's formula gives, uniformly when
$a/n$ stays in a compact subset of $(0,1)$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{C_aC_b}{C_n}
 =\frac{4^{-(t+1)}}{\sqrt\pi\,n^{3/2}}
       \bigl\{\mathfrak k(a/n)+o(1)\bigr\}.
 \label{mj:eq:split-asymptotic}
\end{equation}
The exponent $t+1$ is important: one unit is spent at the terminal maximum in
addition to the $t$ units of outer cost. Combining
\eqref{mj:eq:accepted-fraction} with \eqref{mj:eq:split-asymptotic},
summing the interior splits, and using the Riemann-sum mesh $1/n$, gives the
asserted limit with integral restricted to $[u+\eta,1-u-\eta]$.

All raw terminal splits have
\[
 d_n-t-1\le a\le m_n.
\]
Thus their scaled range tends to $[u,1-u]$. In the two excluded strips, the
number of terms is at most $2\eta n+o(n)$; every normalized count is at most
$K_{u,w}n^{-3/2}$ because both block sizes remain macroscopic. The
contribution of the strips after multiplication by $\sqrt n$ is at most
$K_{u,w}\|g\|_\infty\eta+o(1)$. Let $\eta\downarrow0$. This proves
\eqref{mj:eq:fixedword}, including the moving integer boundaries.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Three distinct uniformities]
The argument uses an exponentially uniform first-deficiency bound, a uniform
half-mass estimate for the last entry away from both endpoints, and a Catalan
asymptotic uniform on compact split intervals. The subsequent sum over words
uses the separate rare-scale tail estimate of \cref{mj:lem:cost-tail}. None
of these four tasks can be replaced by a pointwise ``typical block''
statement.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Summing the endpoint-weighted words}
\begin{proof}[Second proof of \cref{mj:thm:profile}, for $0<u<1/2$]
Fix a cost cutoff $h$. There are finitely many decorated words of cost at
most $h$. Apply \cref{mj:prop:fixedword} with $g=1$ and sum over them:
\[
 \sqrt n\,\ljP(\mathcal E_{n,m_n},T_{n,m_n}\le h)
 \longrightarrow\frac{I(u)}{4\sqrt\pi}
       \sum_{\ljcost(w)\le h}\chi(w)4^{-\ljcost(w)}.
\]
By \cref{mj:lem:cost-tail}, the limsup of the omitted scaled probability is
at most $K_u(h+1)^{-1/2}$. It tends to zero as $h\to\infty$. The total
weighted mass is, by \eqref{mj:eq:W}--\eqref{mj:eq:WR},
\begin{align}
 \sum_w\chi(w)4^{-\ljcost(w)}
 &=\mathcal W_R(1/4)+\frac12\{\mathcal W(1/4)-\mathcal W_R(1/4)\}\notag\\
 &=2+\frac12(4-2)=3.
 \label{mj:eq:wordmass}
\end{align}
Thus the limit is $3I(u)/(4\sqrt\pi)$. To evaluate $I(u)$, use
\eqref{mj:eq:derivative}: the expression being differentiated changes sign
under $y\mapsto1-y$. Consequently,
\begin{equation}
 I(u)=\frac{4(1-2u)}{\sqrt{u(1-u)}},
 \label{mj:eq:I}
\end{equation}
and the resulting limit is exactly \eqref{mj:eq:Psi}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The edge at one half and compact uniformity}
\begin{proof}[Completion of the second proof of \cref{mj:thm:profile}]
Let $d_n/n\to u\ge1/2$. For any fixed $y<1/2$, eventually
$d_n\ge\lceil yn\rceil$. Therefore
\[
 \limsup_{n\to\infty}\sqrt n\,\ljP(D_n\ge d_n)\le\Psi(y).
\]
Letting $y\uparrow1/2$ gives zero because $\Psi(y)\to0$. This proves the
pointwise assertion at and above one half.

For compact uniformity, fix $\delta>0$ and consider the nonincreasing
functions $f_n(u)=\sqrt n\,\ljP(D_n\ge\lceil nu\rceil)$ on $[\delta,1]$. They
converge pointwise to the continuous nonincreasing function $\Psi$. Given
$\eps>0$, choose a finite partition of $[\delta,1]$ on which the oscillation
of $\Psi$ in each interval is at most $\eps$. Pointwise convergence holds at
every partition point, simultaneously for sufficiently large $n$.
Monotonicity sandwiches $f_n$ between its endpoint values on each interval,
so the uniform error is at most $3\eps$. This proves the claim. Substitution
$d_n=n-\lfloor\theta n\rfloor$ in \eqref{lj:eq:bridge} gives
\eqref{mj:eq:bulk-ratio} and the stated smaller-bound estimate.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The deficit measure}
\label{mj:sec:words-measure}
\begin{proof}[Second proof of the last assertion of \cref{mj:thm:marked}]
For $0<x_1<x_2\le1$, the mass of $[x_1,x_2)$ is the difference of the two
tail probabilities, with the corresponding integer rounding. Hence
\cref{mj:thm:profile} gives convergence of these interval masses to
$\Psi(x_1)-\Psi(x_2)$. On every $[x_1,1]$, the total masses are uniformly bounded
by \cref{mj:cor:all-tail}. Approximating continuous functions there by
interval step functions proves weak convergence on compact subintervals
whose endpoints are not atoms of the limit. This is vague convergence on
$(0,1]$.

Differentiation, by \eqref{mj:eq:derivative}, gives
\[
 -\Psi'(x)=\frac{3}{2\sqrt\pi}[x(1-x)]^{-3/2}
 \quad(0<x<1/2).
\]
There is no atom at $1/2$, because $\Psi$ is continuous there and is zero to
its right. These facts identify \eqref{mj:eq:measure}. Its density is
asymptotic to $3x^{-3/2}/(2\sqrt\pi)$ at zero, so its total mass is
infinite, while its restriction to $[x_1,1]$ is finite for every $x_1>0$.
\end{proof}

\section{The ends of the profile and the conditional geometry}
\label{mj:sec:conditional}

\subsection{Matching the boundary, and the edge at one half}
\label{mj:sec:edges}
As $u\downarrow0$,
\begin{equation}
 \Psi(u)=\frac3{\sqrt\pi}u^{-1/2}
       \left(1-\frac32u-\frac58u^2+O(u^3)\right).
 \label{mj:eq:boundary-matching}
\end{equation}
(Manuscript~10 prints the expansion to $O(u^2)$, manuscript~11 to
$O(u^3)$.) The leading term matches Part~III's boundary equivalent
$\ljP(D_n\ge d)\sim3/\sqrt{\pi d}$ when $d\to\infty$ and $d=o(n)$
(\cref{lj:thm:tail-main}), and its limiting tail constant $3/\sqrt\pi$
(\eqref{lj:eq:tail-leading}): with $u=d/n$,
$\Psi(u)/\sqrt n\approx3/\sqrt{\pi d}$. This is a consistency check between
two proven regimes, not a proof of a uniform two-parameter expansion down to
arbitrary $u=u_n\to0$, and not an illicit substitution of a moving $u=d/n$
into a fixed-$u$ theorem. \Cref{mj:thm:profile} gives compact
\emph{absolute} uniformity away from zero; it does not by itself give
relative error estimates when $u\to0$.

At the other endpoint, if $u=1/2-\varepsilon$ with $\varepsilon\downarrow0$,
then
\begin{equation}
 \Psi(u)=\frac{12}{\sqrt\pi}\varepsilon+O(\varepsilon^3).
 \label{mj:eq:half-edge}
\end{equation}
The profile vanishes linearly at one half. This signals the need for a
smaller probability scale or a separate transition window, not an absence of
bounded permutations on the other side: a shrinking neighbourhood of $1/2$ may
interact with a smaller finite-size scale that the first-order theorem does
not determine. (Both manuscripts; the two readings are theirs.)

\subsection{Three equally weighted endpoint channels}
Fix $0<u<1/2$ and condition on $D_n>un$. The split integral $I(u)$ of
\eqref{mj:eq:I} satisfies
\begin{equation}
 I(u)=\int_u^{1-u}[y(1-y)]^{-3/2}\,\dd y
 =\frac{4(1-2u)}{\sqrt{u(1-u)}},
 \label{mj:eq:Iu}
\end{equation}
and $\Psi(u)=3I(u)/(4\sqrt\pi)$. The following is a sampling description of
the limiting conditional law, not a claim that a unique finite tree split has
already been identified (manuscript~10; but see \cref{mj:thm:conditional}).

\begin{corollary}[Three equally weighted endpoint channels; manuscript~10]
\label{mj:cor:conditional}
Let $U^{(u)}$ have density $I(u)^{-1}[y(1-y)]^{-3/2}$ on $(u,1-u)$, and
independently let $\xi$ be uniform on $\{\mathrm{low},\mathrm{interior},
\mathrm{high}\}$. Define
\[
 V=\min(U^{(u)},1-U^{(u)}),\qquad
 \zeta=\begin{cases}0,&\xi=\mathrm{low},\\1-U^{(u)},&\xi=\mathrm{interior},\\
 1,&\xi=\mathrm{high}.\end{cases}
\]
Then
\begin{equation}
 \ljLaw((D_n/n,L_n/n)\mid D_n>un)\Longrightarrow\ljLaw(V,\zeta).
 \label{mj:eq:conditional-marked}
\end{equation}
The limiting last-entry law has atoms of mass $1/3$ at each of $0$ and $1$;
its remaining mass $1/3$ has the displayed interior density. Also
$F_n/n\to1$ in probability under the same conditioning.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Restrict \cref{mj:thm:marked} to $x>u$ and divide by the positive limiting
mass $\Psi(u)=3I(u)/(4\sqrt\pi)$. The three equal summands yield the
sampling description. For the first value, \cref{mj:lem:first} makes
$\ljP(J_n>\varepsilon n)$ exponentially small, whereas the conditioning event
has probability asymptotic to $\Psi(u)n^{-1/2}$. Their ratio tends to zero.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Universal outer word and macroscopic split}
The canonical parser of \cref{mj:sec:parser} yields more than the scalar
tail. Fix $u\in(0,1/2)$, $d_n/n\to u$, and $m_n=n-d_n$. Condition on
$\mathcal E_{n,m_n}$. Let $W_n$ be the canonical outer word and $U_n$ the
terminal left size divided by $n$.

\begin{theorem}[Universal outer word and macroscopic split; manuscript~11]
\label{mj:thm:conditional}
Conditionally on $\mathcal E_{n,m_n}$, the pair $(W_n,U_n)$ converges in law
to an independent pair $(W,U^{(u)})$, where
\begin{equation}
 \ljP(W=w)=\frac{\chi(w)4^{-\ljcost(w)}}3
 \label{mj:eq:word-law}
\end{equation}
and $U^{(u)}$ has density
\begin{equation}
 f_u(y)=\frac{\mathfrak k(y)}{I(u)}\one_{[u,1-u]}(y).
 \label{mj:eq:split-law}
\end{equation}
The word space is the countable discrete space of individually decorated
finite words. Its limiting law is independent of $u$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For each fixed word, divide \eqref{mj:eq:fixedword} by
$\ljP(\mathcal E_{n,m_n})\sim3I(u)/(4\sqrt{\pi n})$. This gives the product
law on that word and every continuous test function of $U_n$. The normalized
version of \eqref{mj:eq:rare-tail}, together with the positive denominator
constant, gives tightness of the word costs. There are finitely many words at
each bounded cost, so the word variables are tight on the discrete space. The
split variable lies in a compact interval. Finite-word convergence plus this
tightness proves joint convergence. Equation \eqref{mj:eq:wordmass} verifies
that \eqref{mj:eq:word-law} is normalized.
\end{proof}

The split law $U^{(u)}$ is the same in \cref{mj:cor:conditional} and
\cref{mj:thm:conditional}: manuscript~10's split variable (its $Y$) and
manuscript~11's (its $U_x$) have the same density.

\begin{corollary}[A heavy-tailed cost but a short word; manuscript~11]
\label{mj:cor:wordstats}
Let $T_W=\ljcost(W)$. Then
\begin{align}
 \ljP(T_W=t)&=\frac{C_t+C_{t+1}}{6\,4^t}\quad(t\ge0),
 \label{mj:eq:cost-law}\\
 \ljE z^{T_W}&=\frac{\Cat(z/4)+\Cat(z/4)^2}{6},
 \label{mj:eq:cost-pgf}\\
 \ljP(\mjlen(W)=k)&=\frac{(1/2)^k+(3/4)^k}{6}\quad(k\ge0),
 \label{mj:eq:length-law}\\
 \ljE\,\mjlen(W)&=\frac73,
 \qquad
 \ljP(W\text{ has no }A)=\frac23.
 \label{mj:eq:wordfacts}
\end{align}
Furthermore,
\begin{equation}
 \ljP(T_W>h)\sim\frac{5}{3\sqrt{\pi h}},\qquad \ljE T_W=\infty.
 \label{mj:eq:cost-tail-limit}
\end{equation}
These expectation statements concern the limiting word law; convergence of
finite-$n$ word expectations is not asserted.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
At cost $t$, there are $C_t$ words without $A$ and $C_{t+1}-C_t$ with at
least one $A$. Sum \eqref{mj:eq:word-law} to obtain \eqref{mj:eq:cost-law},
and then use \eqref{mj:eq:W}--\eqref{mj:eq:WR} for its probability generating
function.

The critical total weight of one $R$ operation is $1/2$, and that of one $A$
is $1/4$. At length $k$, all words have weight $(3/4)^k$, whereas those
without $A$ have weight $(1/2)^k$. The accepted weight is half the sum of
these two quantities. Division by three gives \eqref{mj:eq:length-law}. The
geometric-series identities give
\[
 \ljE\,\mjlen(W)=\frac16\left\{\frac{1/2}{(1-1/2)^2}
                      +\frac{3/4}{(1-3/4)^2}\right\}=\frac73.
\]
The no-$A$ probability is $\mathcal W_R(1/4)/3=2/3$. Finally,
\eqref{mj:eq:catalan-bounds} and $C_{t+1}/C_t\to4$ imply
$\ljP(T_W=t)\sim5/(6\sqrt\pi\,t^{3/2})$. Summing this regularly varying
power tail gives \eqref{mj:eq:cost-tail-limit} and infinite mean cost.
\end{proof}

The word typically has only a few operations, but one decoration can be
large. This explains why summability alone was insufficient for the
rare-scale interchange: the limiting cost has no finite first moment. (The
limiting word cost $T_W$ is not Part~III's skeleton cost $T$, whose tail is
$\sim4/\sqrt{\pi h}$ by \cref{lj:lem:cost-tail}.)

\emph{Three equally weighted endpoint modes (manuscript~11).} Call the
terminal right block's last-entry mode \emph{high} when $L(\tau)>b/2$ and
\emph{low} otherwise. In the interior of the split range, the low and high
modes each have unconditioned probability tending to one half. The proof of
\cref{mj:prop:fixedword} therefore also yields the following table under
$\mathcal E_{n,m_n}$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
Outer word & Terminal last-entry mode & Limiting probability\\
\midrule
No append & Low & $1/3$\\
No append & High & $1/3$\\
At least one append & High & $1/3$\\
At least one append & Low & $0$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For a fixed no-append word, each mode has mass $4^{-t}/6$ in the conditional
law. For a fixed word containing an append, only the high mode survives, also
with mass $4^{-t}/6$. Summing over their respective critical word weights,
both equal to two, proves the table. Boundary strips are removed exactly as
in \cref{mj:prop:fixedword}. This is another interpretation of the factor
three.

\begin{proposition}[Geometric meaning of the rare-event measure; manuscript~11]
\label{mj:prop:minsplit}
Conditionally on $\mathcal E_{n,m_n}$,
\begin{equation}
 \frac{D_n}{n}-\min(U_n,1-U_n)\longrightarrow0
 \quad\text{in probability}.
 \label{mj:eq:minsplit}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for $y\ge u$,
\begin{equation}
 \ljP(D_n/n\ge y\mid \mathcal E_{n,m_n})\longrightarrow
       \frac{\Psi(y)}{\Psi(u)},
 \label{mj:eq:conditional-tail}
\end{equation}
with the right side zero for $y\ge1/2$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
First restrict to a bounded outer cost and an interior split interval. By
\cref{mj:cor:all-tail}, a uniform block of size $a$ has maximum jump
$a-o(n)$ in probability, uniformly when $a$ is proportional to $n$; the same
is true for the right block of size $b$. Deleting a set of such terminal
pairs of relative probability $o(1)$ remains harmless after imposing the
endpoint acceptance event, whose limiting fraction is either one or one half.

The terminal downward jump is $a+1+J(\tau)=a+o(n)$ by \cref{mj:lem:first},
and the upward jump into the terminal maximum is at most $a$. Every outer $R$
jump is $o(n)$ on the same good endpoint set. If an append occurs,
acceptance selects the high last-entry mode, and \cref{mj:lem:last} gives
$L(\tau)=b-o(n)$ in conditional probability. Its first append jump is
therefore $a+o(n)$; later append jumps are bounded by the fixed outer cost.
Hence the global maximum jump is $\max(a,b)+o(n)$, and the deficit is
$\min(a,b)+o(n)$. Since $(a+b)/n\to1$, this proves \eqref{mj:eq:minsplit}
under the two restrictions. Remove the boundary strips and then the cost
cutoff by \cref{mj:thm:conditional} and \cref{mj:lem:cost-tail}.

The map $y\mapsto\min(y,1-y)$ sends \eqref{mj:eq:split-law} to density
$2\mathfrak k(y)/I(u)$ on $[u,1/2]$. Its upper tail is $\Psi(y)/\Psi(u)$ by
\eqref{mj:eq:I}, proving \eqref{mj:eq:conditional-tail}. The same tail
statement also follows directly by taking the ratio in
\cref{mj:thm:profile}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{How the two descriptions of the factor three match}
\label{mj:sec:three-match}
The two manuscripts explain the coefficient $3$ by three equally weighted
alternatives, and the alternatives correspond. On the terminal split, with
$y=a/n$ the left fraction, manuscript~11's modes and manuscript~10's channels
match as follows. Without an append, the whole permutation ends with the
terminal right block's last entry, since $R$ operations preserve the last
entry; the low mode puts $L_n/n$ near $0$ (manuscript~10's \emph{low}
channel), and the high mode puts $L_n/n$ near $b/n\approx1-y$ (its
\emph{interior} channel, the mark $1-U^{(u)}$). With an append, the last entry
is a maximum at least $N=n-t$, so $L_n/n\to1$ (its \emph{high} channel). The
deficit is $\min(y,1-y)$ in all three cases (\cref{mj:prop:minsplit} and the
proof of \cref{mj:lem:renewal}). Manuscript~10's corollary does not identify
a canonical split; manuscript~11's parser supplies one, and
\cref{mj:cor:conditional}'s interior mark is then its high-mode, no-append
terminal block. This correspondence is the merge's reading of the two proofs;
neither manuscript states it, and no joint limit theorem for $(W_n,U_n,L_n/n)$
is claimed.

\section{Moment constants and size-biased limits}
\label{mj:sec:moments}

\subsection{Uniform integrability at exactly the required threshold}
\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{mj:thm:moments} (both manuscripts)]
For $p>0$, the tail formula for a nonnegative bounded random variable gives
the layer-cake identity
\begin{equation}
 n^{1/2-p}\ljE D_n^p
 =p\int_0^1x^{p-1}\sqrt n\,\ljP(D_n>nx)\,\dd x.
 \label{mj:eq:layer-cake}
\end{equation}
Using a weak rather than a strict inequality changes only the integer
threshold by at most one and has no effect on the pointwise limits. The
bound \eqref{mj:eq:scaled-envelope} dominates the integrand by
$4\sqrt2\,p x^{p-3/2}$ (manuscript~10). Equivalently (manuscript~11), by
\cref{mj:cor:all-tail} the scaled tail is at most $Kx^{-1/2}$ for $nx\ge1$,
and for $nx<1$ the trivial bound $\sqrt n\le x^{-1/2}$ gives the same
envelope, so the integrand is at most $Kpx^{p-3/2}$. This is integrable at
zero exactly when $p>1/2$. Dominated convergence and
\cref{mj:thm:profile} therefore prove
\begin{equation}
 n^{1/2-p}\ljE D_n^p\longrightarrow
 p\int_0^{1/2}x^{p-1}\Psi(x)\,\dd x.
 \label{mj:eq:moment-tail-integral}
\end{equation}
Integration by parts is legitimate, because the boundary term at zero
vanishes, $x^p\Psi(x)=O(x^{p-1/2})\to0$, and $\Psi(1/2)=0$; with
\eqref{mj:eq:derivative} it gives \eqref{mj:eq:kappa-beta}. The constant is
finite and positive. For the alternative formula, substitute
\[
 x=\frac{z^2}{1+z^2},\quad 0<z<1,
 \qquad \dd x=\frac{2z}{(1+z^2)^2}\,\dd z.
\]
The integrand $x^{p-3/2}(1-x)^{-3/2}\,\dd x$ becomes
$2(z^2/(1+z^2))^{p-1}\,\dd z$, proving \eqref{mj:eq:kappa-t}. At $p=1$ the
integral is one, so $\mathfrak m_1=3/\sqrt\pi$. At $p=2$,
\[
 \int_0^1\frac{z^2}{1+z^2}\,\dd z
       =\left[z-\arctan z\right]_0^1=1-\frac\pi4.
\]
Since $(\ljE D_n)^2\sim\mathfrak m_1^2n=o(n^{3/2})$, subtraction from the
second moment gives $\mjVar(D_n)\sim\ljE D_n^2\sim\mathfrak m_2n^{3/2}$, which
is \eqref{mj:eq:variance}. The identity $M_n=n-D_n$ gives the mean formula
\eqref{mj:eq:mean-M} and equality of the variances.
\end{proof}

The convergence proof is not a formal interchange of limits. The finite
counting envelope supplies exactly the near-zero domination needed for the
moment integral; vague convergence alone would not do so.

\subsection{Closed forms}
\label{mj:sec:closed-forms}
It is convenient to set
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal I(p)=\int_0^1\left(\frac{z^2}{1+z^2}\right)^{p-1}\dd z,
 \qquad \mathfrak m_p=\frac3{\sqrt\pi}\mathcal I(p).
 \label{mj:eq:Ip}
\end{equation}
Direct integration gives (manuscript~11; manuscript~10 prints the orders
$1$, $2$, $3$):
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cll}
\toprule
$p$ & $\mathcal I(p)$ & $\mathfrak m_p$\\
\midrule
$1$ & $1$ & $3/\sqrt\pi$\\
$3/2$ & $\sqrt2-1$ & $\frac3{\sqrt\pi}(\sqrt2-1)$\\
$2$ & $1-\pi/4$ & $\frac3{\sqrt\pi}\bigl(1-\frac\pi4\bigr)$\\
$3$ & $5/4-3\pi/8$ & $\frac3{\sqrt\pi}\bigl(\frac54-\frac{3\pi}8\bigr)$\\
$4$ & $3/2-15\pi/32$ & $\frac3{\sqrt\pi}\bigl(\frac32-\frac{15\pi}{32}\bigr)$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For example,
\[
 \mathfrak m_1=1.6925687506\ldots,
 \qquad \mathfrak m_2=0.3632283625\ldots.
\]
For $p=2$ the integral is $\int_0^1t^2/(1+t^2)\,\dd t=1-\pi/4$; for $p=3$,
expand $t^4/(1+t^2)^2=1-2/(1+t^2)+1/(1+t^2)^2$. For any positive integer
$p$, partial fractions in \eqref{mj:eq:kappa-t} reduce the constant to
rational numbers and $\pi$, followed by the common factor $3/\sqrt\pi$
(manuscript~10). For every $p>1/2$ with $p\ne1$, integration of
\[
 \frac{\dd}{\dd z}
       \frac{z^{2p-1}}{(1+z^2)^{p-1}}
 =(2p-1)\left(\frac{z^2}{1+z^2}\right)^{p-1}
 -2(p-1)\left(\frac{z^2}{1+z^2}\right)^p
\]
over $[0,1]$ yields the recurrence (manuscript~11)
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal I(p+1)=\frac{(2p-1)\mathcal I(p)-2^{1-p}}{2(p-1)}.
 \label{mj:eq:Irecurrence}
\end{equation}
At $p=1$ this identity degenerates; one must not divide by zero or use it to
obtain $\mathcal I(2)$ without an additional calculation.

The integral in \eqref{mj:eq:kappa-beta} can also be written
$\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}B_{1/2}(p-1/2,-1/2)$, an incomplete beta function. The
second parameter is negative, but the defining integral stops at $1/2$, not
at its singular endpoint $1$, so it is convergent for every $p>1/2$
(manuscript~10).

\subsection{The two limits of the constant function}
\begin{proposition}[Two limits of the constant function; manuscript~11, the first also manuscript~10]
\label{mj:prop:constantlimits}
As $p\downarrow1/2$ and as $p\to\infty$, respectively,
\begin{align}
 \mathfrak m_p&\sim\frac{3}{\sqrt\pi(2p-1)}=\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}\frac1{p-1/2},
 \label{mj:eq:p-half}\\
 \mathfrak m_p&\sim\frac{6}{\sqrt\pi\,p}\,2^{-p}.
 \label{mj:eq:p-large}
\end{align}
These are statements about the constants after taking the fixed-$p$ limit in
$n$, not joint limits with $p=p(n)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Near zero the integrand of $\mathcal I(p)$ is $z^{2p-2}(1+z^2)^{1-p}$. For
$p$ in a fixed right neighbourhood of $1/2$, subtracting $z^{2p-2}$ leaves an
integrable function with uniformly bounded integral: the factor difference is
$O(z^2)$. Hence $\mathcal I(p)=(2p-1)^{-1}+O(1)$, proving
\eqref{mj:eq:p-half}. (Manuscript~10's argument is the same: the only
divergent contribution is the integral of $t^{2p-2}$ near zero, and the
remaining factor tends regularly to one there.)

For large $p$, write $\varphi(z)=\log(z^2/(1+z^2))$. It is strictly
increasing on $(0,1]$, with $\varphi(1)=-\log2$ and $\varphi'(1)=1$. For
fixed $\eta>0$, the integral over $[0,1-\eta]$ is exponentially smaller than
$2^{-(p-1)}$. On $[1-\eta,1]$, substitute $v=(p-1)(1-z)$ and use
$\varphi(1-v/(p-1))=\varphi(1)-v/(p-1)+O(v^2/(p-1)^2)$. A one-sided
derivative bound near one supplies an integrable exponential majorant.
Dominated convergence yields $\mathcal I(p)\sim2^{-(p-1)}/(p-1)$, which is
\eqref{mj:eq:p-large}.
\end{proof}

The pole in \eqref{mj:eq:p-half} is consistent with the logarithmic critical
moment \eqref{lj:eq:critical-moment} of Part~III. It does not prove
convergence of its centered $O(1)$ remainder. The proof of
\cref{mj:thm:moments} deliberately stops at $p>1/2$: its integrable envelope
fails at the critical order, dominated convergence fails at $p=1/2$, and
vague convergence alone cannot repair that failure. That question remains
separate (\cref{mj:sec:q-centered}).
[Added 30 September 2026, batch 64: Part~V answers it (\cref{cjm:thm:critical})
and sharpens \eqref{mj:eq:p-half} to
$\mathfrak m_p=\frac{3/(2\sqrt\pi)}{p-1/2}+B_1+O(p-1/2)$, with the finite part
$B_1\approx0.382499$ of \cref{cjm:thm:crossover}
(\cref{cjm:lem:beta,cjm:rem:mp}).]

\subsection{General power bias and the uniform transformation}
For $p>1/2$, define (manuscript~10)
\[
 \ljP_{n,p}^\star(B)=\frac{\ljE[D_n^p\one_B]}{\ljE D_n^p}.
\]
The same domination argument, first away from zero and then with the cutoff
removed, proves
\begin{equation}
 \ljLaw_{\ljP_{n,p}^\star}(D_n/n)\Longrightarrow
 \frac{x^p\rho(x)}{\mathfrak m_p}\,\dd x,
 \qquad0<x<1/2.
 \label{mj:eq:power-biased}
\end{equation}
To be explicit about the cutoff removal, \eqref{mj:eq:scaled-envelope} and
layer cake bound the contribution from $D_n/n\le\varepsilon$ by a constant
times $\varepsilon^{p-1/2}$, uniformly in $n$.

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{mj:thm:uniform}]
For $p=1$, the limiting density simplifies to
\[
 \frac12 x^{-1/2}(1-x)^{-3/2}
 =\frac{\dd}{\dd x}\sqrt{\frac{x}{1-x}}.
\]
Its integral over $(0,1/2)$ is one. Thus its distribution function is
\eqref{mj:eq:mean-cdf}; applying the increasing change of variable
$t=\sqrt{x/(1-x)}$ proves \eqref{mj:eq:uniform-law}. At $x=1/5$, the value
is $1/2$, giving \eqref{mj:eq:half-mean}.
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}[tbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=.9\linewidth]{figures/04-macroscopic-jump-mean_weighted_law.pdf}
\caption{Manuscript~10: the limiting fraction $H^\star(x)$ of the mean
deficit contributed by permutations with $D_n\le xn$. This is a size-biased
distribution, not the ordinary distribution of $D_n/n$, which converges to a
point mass at zero.}
\label{mj:fig:mean-law}
\end{figure}

\section{Constructive lower bounds beyond the half-size cutoff}
\label{mj:sec:lower}
The vanishing first-order profile below $M_n/n=1/2$ should not be mistaken
for exponential suppression. A simple explicit family gives polynomial lower
bounds in every fixed proportional-bound regime (manuscript~10).

\begin{theorem}[A many-block lower bound; manuscript~10]
\label{mj:thm:lower}
Fix $0<\theta<1$ and an integer $r\ge1$ such that $(r+1)\theta>1$. There is
a constant $\underline c_{\theta,r}>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large
$n$,
\begin{equation}
 \ljP(M_n\le\lfloor\theta n\rfloor)
 \ge \underline c_{\theta,r}n^{-r/2}.
 \label{mj:eq:many-block-lower}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, $a_n^{(\lfloor\theta n\rfloor)}\ge\underline c'_{\theta,r}
4^n n^{-(r+3)/2}$ for a positive constant $\underline c'_{\theta,r}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Put $\theta_r=1/(r+1)<\theta$. Choose $\eta>0$ sufficiently small that
$\theta_r-r\eta>0$ and $\theta_r+r\eta<\theta$. Choose $r$ integers
$a_1,\ldots,a_r$ in intervals $|a_i/n-\theta_r|<\eta$, and put
\[
 b=n-r-\sum_{i=1}^ra_i.
\]
After slightly shrinking the intervals if necessary, there are at least
$c n^r$ choices, every component size is bounded between two positive
multiples of $n$, and all $a_i,b$ are at most $(\theta-\varepsilon)n$ for a
fixed $\varepsilon>0$.

Take arbitrary left decorations $\beta_i\in\Av_{a_i}(132)$ and a terminal
core $\sigma\in\Av_b(132)$. Form
\[
 \pi=R_{\beta_1}R_{\beta_2}\cdots R_{\beta_r}(\sigma).
\]
The maximum decomposition along the right child recovers this fixed-length
chain and every decoration uniquely. Restrict each component to have first
deficiency at most $\varepsilon n/3$. By \cref{mj:lem:first}, the excluded
proportion in each component is exponentially small in $n$.

For an inner right subtree, \eqref{mj:eq:first-root} says that its first
deficiency is either the deficiency of its left decoration plus one, or, at
the final step, the deficiency of the core. The downward jump introduced by
$R_{\beta_i}$ is therefore at most $a_i+1+\varepsilon n/3+1<\theta n-1$ for
large $n$. By \eqref{mj:eq:M-root}, no internal jump in a left decoration can
exceed that boundary jump. The core itself has maximum jump at most
$b-1<\theta n$. Thus all these good reconstructions obey the required
integer bound.

For each length vector, their number is
$(1-o(1))C_{a_1}\cdots C_{a_r}C_b$, uniformly on the chosen compact size
intervals. Stirling's formula gives
\[
 \frac{C_{a_1}\cdots C_{a_r}C_b}{C_n}
 \ge c''n^{-3r/2};
\]
the fixed exponential factor is $4^{-r}$ because the component sizes sum to
$n-r$. Summing over at least $c n^r$ disjoint length vectors proves
\eqref{mj:eq:many-block-lower}.
\end{proof}

For $1/3<\theta\le1/2$, this gives a lower bound of order $n^{-1}$, while
\cref{mj:thm:profile} gives $o(n^{-1/2})$. The exact order and profile
between those bounds are not proved here. For nonreciprocal $\theta$, the
smallest admissible $r$ is $\lceil1/\theta\rceil-1$; at a reciprocal endpoint
the strict condition requires a larger $r$. (For $\theta>1/2$, $r=1$ gives
the order $n^{-1/2}$ of \eqref{mj:eq:bulk-ratio}; the merge's remark.)

\section{Exact checks, exact data and sampling}
\label{mj:sec:checks}

\subsection{Exact finite checks of manuscript~10}
The standard-library program \path{code/04-macroscopic-jump-verify.py}
exhaustively checks all $82{,}499$ avoiding permutations of sizes $1$
through $11$ generated by the Catalan decomposition. For each it compares the
direct adjacent maximum with the exact root recurrence of
\cref{mj:prop:root}. It checks both endpoint counting formulas and the finite
tail envelope at every threshold in those sizes. For sizes $1$ through $8$, a
separate three-index test applied to all ordinary permutations independently
verifies the generated avoidance class. It additionally checks the exact
stopping-cost tail \eqref{mj:eq:Ttail} for $h=0,\ldots,100$, and verifies the
sampler's rotation multiplicities and full statistic histograms on all
$2{,}352$ degree words of sizes $1$ through $6$. The outcome is recorded in
\path{data/04-macroscopic-jump-verification.json}; the exact finite deficit
histograms for $n=1,\ldots,11$ are retained as integer counts in
\path{data/04-macroscopic-jump-exact_small_distributions.csv}. These tests are
finite regression evidence, not a substitute for any asymptotic proof.

\subsection{Exact finite and symbolic checks of manuscript~11}
Manuscript~11's package contains a standard-library integer implementation of
the endpoint recurrence, a separate exhaustive generator, a canonical parser
and reconstructor, and exact-count data. The verification
(\path{code/05-macroscopic-deficits-verify.py}) was executed with the
following scope:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lr}
\toprule
Check & Executed cases\\
\midrule
Avoiders examined, sizes $2$ through $9$ & $6{,}916$\\
Total counts versus exhaustive enumeration, every $1\le m<n$ & $36$\\
Cumulative endpoint states versus enumeration, sizes through $7$ & $644$\\
Canonical parsing and exact reconstruction checks & $18{,}738$\\
Positive-cost coefficients of both word identities & $60$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The first- and last-entry formulas were also checked against the same
exhaustive classes. The report is retained in
\path{data/05-macroscopic-deficits-verification.json}. These are finite checks
of the combinatorial objects and implementation. They do not establish the
universal analytic limits; the written proofs do that work.

A separate SymPy script (\path{code/05-macroscopic-deficits-verify_symbolic.py})
checked eleven groups of exact symbolic identities, including the derivative
of $\Psi$, the split-integral primitive, the moment substitution and
recurrence, the displayed moment integrals, and the word generating
functions. Its executed report is retained in
\path{data/05-macroscopic-deficits-symbolic_verification.json}; these checks
do not verify limit interchanges or analytic hypotheses.

\subsection{The exact recurrence used for manuscript~11's data}
\label{mj:sec:exact-data}
Manuscript~11 computes $a_n^{(m)}$ exactly with Part~I's endpoint counts
$T^{(m)}_{u,v}(n)$ (\cref{prop:endpoint}): the number of $m$-bounded
avoiders of length $n$ with $n-\pi_1\le u$ and $n-\pi_n\le v$. A negative
threshold gives zero, $T^{(m)}_{u,v}(1)=1$ for nonnegative thresholds, and
thresholds above $n-1$ can be capped at $n-1$. Let
$\mathcal N_s(u)=\#\{\pi\in\Av_s(132):s-\pi_1\le u\}$; its values follow by
summing \eqref{mj:eq:first-count}, and $\mathcal N_0(u)=1$ for $u\ge0$ and
zero for $u<0$. The known endpoint recurrence of Mayama--Akita is
\begin{equation}
 T_{u,v}^{(m)}(n)=
 \sum_{k=1}^{\min(m,n-1)} c_{k,u}\,
        T_{m-k,v-k}^{(m)}(n-k)
   +T_{u-1,m-1}^{(m)}(n-1),
 \label{mj:eq:DP}
\end{equation}
where $c_{1,u}=1$ and $c_{k,u}=\mathcal N_{k-1}(u-1)$ for $k\ge2$: this is
the coefficient form of Part~I's \eqref{eq:endpoint}, and $c_{k,u}$ is
Part~I's \eqref{eq:c-def}. Manuscript~11 rederives it from the maximum
bijection, as Part~I does, to make its computational certificates
self-contained; the derivation is not repeated. When $n\le m+1$, the
adjacency constraint is vacuous, and the last-entry formula gives the useful
exact shortcut (manuscript~11)
\begin{equation}
 T_{u,v}^{(m)}(n)
 =\mathcal N_{n-1}(u-1)+
   \sum_{q=1}^{\min(v,n-1)}C_{n-q-1}\mathcal N_q(u)
 \quad(n\ge2,\ n\le m+1).
 \label{mj:eq:unrestrictedDP}
\end{equation}
For \eqref{mj:eq:unrestrictedDP}, consider a final value $\ell=n-q$. When
$q\ge1$, the last-entry decomposition has a high initial block of size $q$,
which contains the first entry, and a lower block of size $n-q-1$. The whole
first deficiency is exactly the first deficiency in the high block. The count
is therefore $C_{n-q-1}\mathcal N_q(u)$, while the last threshold is
equivalent to $q\le v$. The separate case $q=0$ appends the maximum and
contributes $\mathcal N_{n-1}(u-1)$.

Memoization of \eqref{mj:eq:DP}, using \eqref{mj:eq:unrestrictedDP} whenever
possible, produces the retained counts. The implementation caps vacuous
