thresholds and memoizes smaller-size states. It uses Python
arbitrary-precision integers throughout. It is intended as a transparent
verifier and moderate-size data generator, not as a claim of a new optimal
counting algorithm. Every count and every Catalan number in the CSV files is
an exact integer. Floating point is used only for the displayed ratios and
figures.

\subsection{Moment data without a fitted constant}
For integer-valued positive $D_n$,
\begin{align*}
 \ljE D_n&=\sum_{d=1}^{n-1}\ljP(D_n\ge d),\\
 \ljE D_n^2&=\sum_{d=1}^{n-1}(2d-1)\ljP(D_n\ge d).
\end{align*}
These identities allow the moments to be computed from exact bounded counts
at every $m=1,\ldots,n-1$. The retained results (manuscript~11) are:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rcc}
\toprule
$n$ & $\ljE D_n/\sqrt n$ & $\ljE D_n^2/n^{3/2}$\\
\midrule
$20$ & $1.229950$ & $0.498514$\\
$40$ & $1.350574$ & $0.495311$\\
$80$ & $1.443085$ & $0.479054$\\
\midrule
Limit & $1.692569$ & $0.363228$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The second column of moments is a \emph{second moment}, not a variance. Its
leading constant equals that of the variance only asymptotically, because
the squared mean is of smaller order. The complete rational values are
retained as numerator and denominator integers in
\path{data/05-macroscopic-deficits-moment_counts.csv}.

The dashed line is the analytically proved constant $3/\sqrt\pi$; it was not
estimated from these three data points.}

\subsection{Uniform Catalan sampling without constructing permutations}
\label{mj:sec:sampler}
Manuscript~10's C++ sampler (\path{code/04-macroscopic-jump-simulate.cpp})
uses a binary-tree preorder word with $n$ symbols $+1$ (internal nodes) and
$n+1$ symbols $-1$ (leaves). A uniform shuffle of these symbols is rotated
immediately after the first occurrence of its global minimum partial sum. The
resulting word has nonnegative partial sums before its final $-1$ and encodes
a full ordered binary tree.

The elementary cycle lemma says each tree word has exactly $2n+1$ preimages
under this rotation map. One can see uniqueness of the valid rotation from
the integer partial sums and total sum $-1$. A nontrivial period is
impossible: it would make $-1$ an integer multiple of the sum of one shorter
period. Thus all $2n+1$ rotations are distinct. Uniformity of the shuffle,
followed by the constant preimage count, proves uniformity of the Catalan
tree. The maximum decomposition is a bijection between these trees and
$132$-avoiders.

The tree is then evaluated bottom-up using \cref{mj:prop:root}; no length-$n$
permutation array is required. Each subtree stores only its size, first
entry, last entry, and largest jump. One sample uses $O(n)$ time and $O(n)$
working memory. The implementation uses a fixed pseudorandom seed, so its
retained output is reproducible with the stated implementation. Different
standard-library implementations of \texttt{std::shuffle} may produce
different sample streams even with the same engine seed.

\begin{table}[tbp]
\centering
\caption{Manuscript~10: numerical illustrations of the moment limits, from
its shipped simulation data (\cref{mj:sec:reproduce}). Parenthesized values are one
estimated Monte Carlo standard error for the mean column, not a bound on
finite-size bias. The second-moment column is a sample estimate and is not
presented as a certified interval.}
\label{mj:tab:simulation}
\begin{tabular}{rrrr}
\toprule
$n$ & Samples & $\widehat{\ljE D_n}/\sqrt n$ &
 $\widehat{\ljE D_n^2}/n^{3/2}$\\
\midrule
64    & 300,000 & $1.41333\ (0.00250)$ & 0.48329\\
256   & 200,000 & $1.55368\ (0.00486)$ & 0.44632\\
1,024 & 100,000 & $1.62981\ (0.01027)$ & 0.41261\\
4,096 & 50,000  & $1.69621\ (0.02139)$ & 0.40232\\
16,384& 25,000  & $1.66394\ (0.04260)$ & 0.37599\\
\midrule
Proved limit & & $3/\sqrt\pi\approx1.69257$ &
 $3(4-\pi)/(4\sqrt\pi)\approx0.36323$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{What the numerics do and do not say}
The retained simulation has $675{,}000$ pseudorandom samples across five
sizes. It illustrates approach to the profile and moment constants, but
finite-size bias is visible, especially in the second moment and near the
cutoff. The theorems are established by the proofs, not by a fit to
\cref{mj:tab:simulation} or \cref{mj:tab:bulk}. The data are neither
outward-rounded interval certificates nor a claimed convergence-rate
estimate.

\subsection{Rebuilding and rerunning}
\label{mj:sec:reproduce}
Part~IV's files are
\begin{lstlisting}
code/04-macroscopic-jump-verify.py       Standard-library exact finite checks
code/04-macroscopic-jump-simulate.cpp    C++17 uniform Catalan-tree sampler
code/04-macroscopic-jump-plot_results.py
                                         Its two figures (NumPy, Matplotlib)
code/04-macroscopic-jump-build.sh        Manuscript 10's three-pass build
data/04-macroscopic-jump-*               Recorded check, exact histograms
                                         n = 1..11, 675,000 samples,
                                         delivery build audit
04-macroscopic-jump-SOURCES.md           Manuscript 10's provenance ledger
code/05-macroscopic-deficits-model.py    Exact endpoint counts, generator
code/05-macroscopic-deficits-verify.py   Exhaustive combinatorial checks
code/05-macroscopic-deficits-verify_symbolic.py
                                         SymPy identity checks
code/05-macroscopic-deficits-numerics.py Exact bulk and moment counts

                                         The four figures of this part
\end{lstlisting}
The scripts are shipped byte-identical to the deliveries and still use the
delivered names: they read and write \path{data/verification.json},
\path{data/exact_small_distributions.csv}, \path{data/simulation.csv},
\path{data/symbolic_verification.json}, \path{data/bulk_counts.csv},
\path{data/moment_counts.csv} and unprefixed files in \path{figures/}. Manuscript~11's verifier and data
generator execute \texttt{from model import}; run in place, that import would
load Part~I's different \texttt{code/model.py} and fail. The two build scripts
change to their own directory and look for an \texttt{article.tex} there, so
they fail in \texttt{code/}. The
report's README gives a recipe that restores each delivered layout in a
scratch copy; there, the manuscripts' instructions apply, and rerunning
overwrites only the copy's recorded outputs. The exact verifications need
Python 3.10 or later and no third-party package:
\begin{verbatim}
python code/verify.py                # either package
python code/numerics.py              # manuscript 11; exact counts
\end{verbatim}
Do not use Python's \texttt{-O} option, which disables the verifiers'
assertions. Manuscript~11's symbolic checks need SymPy, and both plot scripts
need NumPy and Matplotlib:
\begin{verbatim}
python code/verify_symbolic.py       # manuscript 11
python code/plot_results.py          # either package
g++ -O3 -std=c++17 code/simulate.cpp -o simulate   # manuscript 10
./simulate > data/simulation.csv
\end{verbatim}
Regenerating samples can change the numerical table unless the table is
updated too; the article and data correspond to the retained run. The
retained figures make Python unnecessary for rebuilding the article. The
included count files record exact integers; rounded display columns and plots
are not interval-arithmetic certificates.

\section{Further research questions}
\label{mj:sec:questions}
The proved results remove the unknown leading constants from all
supercritical moments and identify a positive part of the linear-bound
regime. Several neighbouring problems remain substantial. The questions below
separate statements proved above from plausible next steps. Manuscript~10
proposes ten directions and manuscript~11 ten questions; where both ask the
same question it is printed once with both texts, so thirteen remain.

\subsection{The smaller-bound regime and the hierarchy below half size}
\label{mj:sec:q-hierarchy}
\emph{Manuscript~11.} Determine an asymptotic equivalent for
$a_n^{(\lfloor\theta n\rfloor)}/C_n$ for each fixed $0<\theta<1/2$.
\emph{Manuscript~10.} For a nonreciprocal $0<\theta<1$, set
$r=\lceil1/\theta\rceil-1$. Is there a positive function $\Omega_r(\theta)$
such that
\[
 \ljP(M_n\le\theta n)\sim\Omega_r(\theta)n^{-r/2}?
\]
This is a proposed conjectural hierarchy, not a theorem. The case $r=1$ is
now proved (\cref{mj:thm:profile}), and \cref{mj:thm:lower} supplies the
lower bound in every open interval. A proof of a matching upper bound would
need control of repeated macroscopic splits and their endpoint marks.
Manuscript~11 proves only $o(n^{-1/2})$ there, and adds: a single split into
two blocks below the threshold is no longer available near the top. Several
macroscopic pieces must be created before the threshold parser can stop.
Thresholds at reciprocal integers $\theta=1/k$ are natural candidates for
changes of behaviour, but no hierarchy of exponents or constants is
established by manuscript~11. An analysis must retain the last-entry
constraints through all large pieces rather than multiply independent split
probabilities.

\subsection{Reciprocal thresholds and the critical window at \texorpdfstring{$m=n/2$}{m = n/2}}
\label{cjm:sec:mj-half}
\emph{Manuscript~10.} Determine the order of $\ljP(M_n\le n/2)$ and the
transition when $M_n\le n/2+s_n$. \emph{Manuscript~11.} Find the probability
scale and the transition function when $m=n/2+s_n$ and $s_n=o(n)$. The linear
vanishing in \eqref{mj:eq:half-edge} is a constraint on any crossover formula,
but does not determine the size of $s_n$, the width of the window, or the
probability exactly at its centre. More generally, do the $\theta=1/r$ mark
distinct polynomial or logarithmic transitions? The discarded terminal
boundary strips, negligible at a fixed bulk fraction, may become part of the
main term here. Rounding effects, parity and the terminal maximum's unit cost
must be retained at a lattice scale rather than replaced by a continuum
approximation too early.

\subsection{A uniform relative boundary-to-bulk approximation}
\emph{Manuscript~11.} Find a formula with relative error tending to zero in a
range simultaneously covering $d\to\infty$, $d=o(n)$, and $d/n$ bounded
inside $(0,1/2)$. A useful goal is an explicit error bound that exposes
dependence on both $d$ and $n-2d$. The proof here truncates the word cost
before taking the limit. A quantitative version would keep the slowly
decaying word tail, the endpoint acceptance correction, and the two
split-boundary strips in one estimate. Compact absolute uniformity is not
enough when the main profile itself tends to zero or infinity.

\subsection{A local macroscopic limit}
\label{cjm:sec:mj-local}
\emph{Both manuscripts.} For $d_n/n\to x\in(0,1/2)$ (manuscript~11), or
uniformly on compact subsets of $(0,1/2)$ with $d_n=\lfloor xn\rfloor$
(manuscript~10), is it true that
\[
 n^{3/2}\ljP(D_n=d_n)\longrightarrow\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}[x(1-x)]^{-3/2}?
\]
This is consistent with \cref{mj:thm:marked}, but it is not a consequence of
it: the vague limit controls intervals, not individual lattice masses, and
differencing two tail estimates whose errors are merely $o(n^{-1/2})$ does not
control a probability of order $n^{-3/2}$. A proof requires a local count or
substantially sharper error control. A local theorem would substantially
strengthen coefficient asymptotics and numerical certification.

\subsection{The exact total-variation constant}
\label{cjm:sec:mj-tv}
\emph{Both manuscripts.} Part~III proves a sharp order but leaves the constant
in $\ljTV(\ljLaw(D_n),\ljLaw(D))$ open (its Section~\ref{mj:sec:lj-tv}).
Determine whether $\sqrt n\,\ljTV(\ljLaw(D_n),\ljLaw(D))$ converges, and
compute the limit if it exists. The limiting boundary mass beyond $n/2$
suggests a possible contribution $3\sqrt2/\sqrt\pi$, but neither manuscript
asserts that this is the full constant (manuscript~10). The bulk measure
identifies one source of mass at large deficits. Total variation also depends
on signed finite-size corrections at small and intermediate deficits, so the
answer cannot be obtained just by integrating the unsigned bulk density; the
distinction between a weak rare-event measure and a signed correction measure
is essential (manuscript~11). Establishing it requires control of the signs
and aggregate size of discrepancies inside the finite range, which the
present weak rare-event limit does not provide.

\subsection{Centered critical and subcritical corrections}
\label{mj:sec:q-centered}
\emph{Manuscript~10.} Does
\[
 \ljE\sqrt{D_n}-\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}\log n
\]
converge, and what is its limit? Can one identify the leading correction to
$\ljE D_n^p$ for $0<p<1/2$? These questions require a quantitative matching of
the fixed-deficit law and the macroscopic measure near the singular boundary
$x=0$. (The first is the critical part of Part~III's
Section~\ref{mj:sec:lj-moments}, which Part~IV leaves open.)
[Added 30 September 2026, batch 64: both questions are answered in Part~V,
from Part~III's uniform total-variation bound and Part~IV's pointwise
profile alone. The limit exists and equals an explicit constant
$c_\star\approx-0.942121414$ (\cref{cjm:thm:critical}), and
$\ljE D_n^p=\ljE D^p+\mathfrak m(p)n^{p-1/2}+o(n^{p-1/2})$ for $0<p<1/2$, with an
explicit negative coefficient that continues $\mathfrak m_p$ through its pole
(\cref{cjm:thm:subcritical,cjm:rem:mp}). No finer matching near $x=0$ was
needed. A rate for the centered half-moment remains open
(\cref{cjm:sec:future}).]

\subsection{Second-order moment asymptotics}
\label{cjm:sec:mj-second}
\emph{Both manuscripts.} Determine the next term after $3\sqrt{n/\pi}$ in
$\ljE D_n$, the next term in the second moment, and the next terms in
$\ljE D_n^p$ for fixed $p>1/2$. The numerical tables show significant
finite-size corrections, and the finite data do not establish whether the
next mean term is constant, logarithmic, or of another order. The next
contribution can mix a boundary correction with a bulk correction. An
expansion for a fixed positive deficit fraction alone need not be integrable
down to zero. Matched subtraction of the small-$u$ singular terms of
\eqref{mj:eq:boundary-matching} is a possible route, but must come with a
uniform remainder. A correct expansion must separate finite outer
decorations, mesoscopic tails, and repeated macroscopic split events, rather
than extrapolating a few simulated sizes.

\subsection{A geometric conditional limit for the whole permutation}
\label{cjm:sec:mj-geometric}
\emph{Manuscript~10.} \Cref{mj:cor:conditional} describes two marked
statistics. Can one construct a conditioned tree or permutation limit under
$D_n>un$, with an actual canonical macroscopic split and a finite outer
decoration process? Such a result could determine the position of the
largest jump and the joint sizes of the largest ascent and descent. The
sampling representation of the limiting pair is not, by itself, such a full
geometric theorem. [Merge note: manuscript~11's parser supplies the canonical
split and its \cref{mj:thm:conditional} the limiting law of the finite outer
word jointly with the split; the question is re-scoped to the full
conditioned permutation, the position of the largest jump and the joint
largest ascent and descent, which remain open. Compare Part~III's
Section~\ref{mj:sec:lj-ascent}.]

\subsection{Conditional cost moments and mesoscopic decorations}
\emph{Manuscript~11.} Under $D_n\ge d_n$ with $d_n/n\to u\in(0,1/2)$,
determine the growth of $\ljE\,\ljcost(W_n)$ and the distribution of the
largest outer decoration. The limiting word has finite mean length $7/3$ but
infinite mean cost. Hence convergence in law from \cref{mj:thm:conditional}
does not determine these expectations. A joint theory of the word cost and a
second large split may describe how the infinite limiting expectation is
truncated at finite $n$.

\subsection{A uniform bridge to shrinking adjacency fractions}
\emph{Manuscript~10.} The current result fixes $\theta>1/2$, while the
fixed-$m$ spectral program fixes $m$ before letting $n$ grow. Determine
regimes with $m\to\infty$, $m/n\to0$. The lower-bound hierarchy suggests that
the required number of large components itself grows, so a fixed-depth
renewal expansion may cease to be uniform. (Compare Part~III's
Section~\ref{lj:sec:q-reconnect}.)

\subsection{Weighted refinements}
\emph{Manuscript~11.} Extend the rare-event profile to avoiders weighted by
descents, or record additional statistics jointly with the terminal split.
The constant three arose from three equally weighted endpoint modes. Under a
nonuniform weight those modes may no longer be equal, and the critical
operation masses will change. A correct extension should derive those weights
from the refined generating functions instead of replacing Catalan numbers by
a new sequence without rechecking the endpoint law. (Compare Part~III's
Section~\ref{mj:sec:lj-weighted}.)

\subsection{Other classes, statistics and endpoint operators}
\emph{Manuscript~10.} Identify statistics on other pattern-avoiding classes
whose root recursion has an endpoint projection like $S$. If the source is a
Catalan split measure but the finite endpoint transition has a different
matrix, which rare-event exponents and coefficients persist? The useful
target is a proved uniform-remainder theorem, not merely a formal matrix
inverse. \emph{Manuscript~11.} Identify other recursively decomposable
classes in which an extreme adjacency statistic has a tight ordinary limit
but a nontrivial $n^{1/2}$-weighted macroscopic measure. The proof suggests a
mechanism: a summable critical outer-word series, a square-root Catalan split
law, and a finite endpoint acceptance matrix. These are hypotheses to verify
in each class, not a universal theorem already proved. Symmetries preserving
absolute adjacent differences give immediate equivalent formulations, but a
genuinely new class needs a separate decomposition.

\subsection{Formalization, certified and efficient computation}
\label{cjm:sec:mj-formal}
\emph{Manuscript~10.} A Lean development could first formalize the finite
decomposition, \cref{mj:prop:root}, the endpoint counts, and the stopping-word
injection. The analytic layer would then add the uniform tail envelope, the
Riemann-sum source theorem, and the bounded-sequence contraction argument. The
last two steps deserve separate reusable interfaces. No claim is made that
these declarations already exist in the repository or that the current Python
checks constitute a formal proof. \emph{Manuscript~11.} Formalize the
threshold parser and the rare-scale tail argument, and improve the exact
endpoint implementation for large linear bounds. Natural proof-assistant
milestones are the reconstruction bijection, the word generating-function
identities, the convolution tail estimate, and the fixed-word endpoint
acceptance lemma. The dominated-convergence step should then expose its
precise $p>1/2$ hypothesis. For computation, a batched treatment of all bounds
could avoid rebuilding overlapping endpoint states. Neither a new formal
library nor an optimal complexity claim is supplied.

\section{Dependency audit, scope and conclusion}
\label{mj:sec:audit}

\subsection{The two dependency chains}
Manuscript~11's proof dependency is
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \text{Catalan maximum and endpoint formulas}\\
 \Downarrow\\
 \text{canonical threshold parser and rare-scale cost tail}\\
 \Downarrow\\
 \text{fixed-word endpoint acceptance and Catalan split integral}\\
 \Downarrow\\
 \text{bulk profile and conditional structure}\\
 \Downarrow\\
 \text{all supercritical moment constants}.
 \end{gathered}
\]
Manuscript~10's replaces the second and third lines by the exact deficit
recursion with the stopping-word envelope, and the truncated renewal equation
with its contraction proof. Neither depends on a fixed-$m$ exponential growth
approximation, an unproved component ordering, a presumed uniform spectral
gap, or the asymptotic theorems of Parts~I--III. The finite-state recurrence
is used for computations and is explicitly attributed to its prior source.

\label{mj:tab:audit}
\begin{tabular}{p{.37\linewidth}p{.56\linewidth}}
\toprule
Claim & Basis and limitation\\
\midrule
Exact root identity (10) & Direct finite comparison of all possible adjacent

Uniform tail envelope (both) & An injective stopping-word reconstruction and

Marked rare-event measure (10) & Endpoint limits, an explicit mesoscopic

Bulk profile for $0<d/n<1/2$ (both) & Two written proofs, each including

Scaled zero limit at and above $d/n=1/2$ (both) & Written monotonicity proof;

All fixed-$p>1/2$ moment constants (both) & Evaluation of that measure and a
 proved uniform integrable envelope for moment integrals.\\
Conditional word and split laws (11); three channels (10) & Written joint
 convergence proof (11); a sampling description of the limiting marked pair
 (10).\\
Deficit-size-biased uniform law (10) & A change of variable in the limiting
 weighted density; not the unweighted permutation law.\\
Sub-half-size regimes (10, 11) & Constructive polynomial lower bounds only
 (10), $o(n^{-1/2})$ (both); matching upper bounds and equivalents remain
 open.\\
Computations (both) & Exact integer finite tests and exact symbolic checks
 (11); explicitly labelled Monte Carlo illustrations (10); rounded displays of
 exact counts (11). No asymptotic claim rests on computation.\\
New Lean or Rocq declarations & None supplied or claimed.\\
Novelty, review and formal status & New relative to the inspected report's

\subsection{What is resolved}
The advances claimed are deliberately specific. The positive linear-bound
range $m/n\in(1/2,1)$ now has an explicit relative equivalent. Every fixed
moment order above the threshold $1/2$ now has a proved leading constant,
including the previously undetermined mean and variance constants. The
conditional rare event has an explicit two-block geometry and a universal
outer-word law. The entire linear-bound problem is not claimed solved,
because smaller bounds are only shown to have lower-order probability, with
polynomial lower bounds (manuscript~11's summary, extended by manuscript~10's
lower bounds).

\subsection{Conclusion}
\emph{Manuscript~10.} The main obstruction in passing from a boundary law to
exact moments is not integration but missing probability mass at macroscopic
distances. The present work identifies that mass. A two-block Catalan
source, modified by a finite endpoint renewal, produces the explicit measure
\eqref{mj:eq:marked-measure}; its deficit marginal gives the complete
first-order profile and all supercritical constants. The proof also isolates
a useful methodological lesson. A finite exact identity can remove an
apparently necessary subtree statistic. Once this identity is combined with a
uniform counting envelope, the mesoscopic remainder becomes summable and a
heuristic renewal equation becomes a convergent one. The equal three-channel
endpoint mixture and the size-biased uniform law then emerge from an
elementary explicit calculation.

\emph{Manuscript~11.} The fixed-deficit law and the macroscopic deficit
measure describe different parts of the same random statistic. The former
governs ordinary probability and low moments; the latter accounts for rare
configurations large enough to determine high moments. A canonical threshold
parser makes that second regime accessible without importing a nonuniform
spectral estimate. Its summable but heavy-tailed outer structure contributes
an endpoint-weighted mass of three, and the remaining Catalan split integral
can be evaluated exactly. This turns an order-of-magnitude moment statement
into a complete family of explicit leading constants while leaving clear,
nontrivial questions at the critical window and beyond it.

\section{Small exact examples and normalization checks}
\label{mj:sec:small}
This section was manuscript~10's Appendix~B. For $n=3$, the five avoiders are
$123$, $213$, $231$, $312$, $321$. Their deficits are respectively $2$, $1$,
$1$, $1$, $2$. For
example, $\pi=213$ is an append operation on $21$:
\[
 D(213)=\min\{D(21)+1,L(21)\}=\min\{2,1\}=1.
\]
For $\pi=231$, the nonempty right block is $1$ and the left size is one:
\[
 D(231)=\min\{F(1),2+D(1)\}=1.
\]
These examples illustrate why the append state needs the last value while a
nonempty right block removes the left block's internal maximum.

For the marked source, the total tail integral is
\[
 \int_u^{1-u}[y(1-y)]^{-3/2}\,\dd y
 =4\frac{1-2u}{\sqrt{u(1-u)}}.
\]
Multiplying by the source coefficient $1/(4\sqrt\pi)$ gives the unmarked
two-block source. The full renewal contributes a factor three, so its tail is
exactly \eqref{mj:eq:Psi}. The folding factor two and the three endpoint
channels are distinct factors; conflating them would produce an incorrect
constant.

Finally, the size-biased density integrates to one because
\[
 \frac12\int_0^{1/2}x^{-1/2}(1-x)^{-3/2}\,\dd x
 =\left[\sqrt{\frac{x}{1-x}}\right]_0^{1/2}=1.
\]
This independently checks the normalization linking the marked source, the
mean constant, and the uniform-law transformation.

Its README at that pin describes the merger of three reports. The third,

\subsection{Where the merge had to choose}
\label{mj:sec:merge}
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Placement.} Part~IV follows Part~III and precedes Part~I's
 appendices, which contain no numbered equations, tables or figures; no
 number of Parts~I--III changed. Manuscript~10's two appendices are printed as

 manuscript~11's Appendix~A (the recurrence) is \cref{mj:sec:exact-data} and
 its Appendix~B (reproduction) is replaced by \cref{mj:sec:reproduce}. Both
 title pages and abstracts are replaced by the opening note of Part~IV, the
 status box of \cref{mj:sec:source} (which prints both research-status
 boxes) and a paragraph of the report's abstract; every claim of both
 abstracts is stated in \cref{mj:sec:introduction}.
\item \emph{Base and order.} Manuscript~10 is the base. Its sections are
 printed in its order, with manuscript~11's parser and tightness sections
 beside its envelope (\cref{mj:sec:envelope}), manuscript~11's proof of the
 profile after manuscript~10's (\cref{mj:sec:words}), and manuscript~11's
 conditional structure, closed forms and exact data beside the corresponding
 material of manuscript~10.
\item \emph{Duplicates between the manuscripts.} Printed once, credited to
 both: the profile theorem (manuscript~10's Theorem~1.1 with manuscript~11's
 sequence form, Theorem~1.1 there), the deficit measure (manuscript~11's
 Theorem~1.2 as the last assertion of \cref{mj:thm:marked}), the moment
 theorem (both Theorems~1.3; one proof, with both domination arguments), the
 first-entry bound (\cref{mj:lem:first}, both constants), the small-$u$ and
 half-edge expansions, the critical pole (\cref{mj:prop:constantlimits}), and
 seven research questions. Kept as second routes: manuscript~11's uniform
 last-entry estimate (second proof of \cref{mj:lem:last}), its all-deficit
 envelope (\cref{mj:cor:all-tail}, a second proof of
 \cref{mj:prop:envelope} up to the constant), its proof of the profile
 (\cref{mj:sec:words}) and of the deficit measure
 (\cref{mj:sec:words-measure}). The constants checked equal: the mean
 constant $3/\sqrt\pi$; the variance constants $3(4-\pi)/(4\sqrt\pi)$ and
 $(3/\sqrt\pi)(1-\pi/4)$; manuscript~10's size-biased form and
 manuscript~11's moment formula (the size-biased law has total mass
 $\mathcal I(1)=1$, and manuscript~10's integral forms equal manuscript~11's
 $\mathfrak m_p$ at $p=3/2$ and $p=3$ numerically and in general by the
 substitution of \cref{mj:thm:moments}); and the small-$u$ tail, which is

\item \emph{Duplicates with Parts~I--III.} The maximum decomposition and
 positional restriction (Part~I, \cref{sec:endpoints}), the first- and

 therefore not reprinted. What the manuscripts add to them (the uniform

\clearpage
\phantomsection\part*{Part V.\quad Critical moments of the largest-jump deficit}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Part V.\texorpdfstring{\quad}{ } Critical moments of the largest-jump deficit}

\noindent\emph{Part~V was added on 30 September 2026 in batch~64 of
ProveIt's incoming-report intake, from one manuscript written against the
state of Parts~I--IV printed above. Part~IV found the macroscopic profile of
the largest-jump deficit $D_n=n-M_n$ and the leading constant of every moment
of order $p>1/2$, and asked in \cref{mj:sec:q-centered} whether
$\ljE\sqrt{D_n}-\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}\log n$ converges and what the leading
correction to $\ljE D_n^p$ is for $0<p<1/2$. Part~V answers both questions,
and with them the critical half of Part~III's
Section~\ref{mj:sec:lj-moments}, from three proved results of Parts~III
and~IV. The abstract transfer theorems behind the answer are independent of
permutations.}

\section{Provenance, conventions and the answer}
\label{cjm:sec:intro}

\subsection{One manuscript, one addition}
\label{cjm:sec:source}
Part~V is built from one AI-assisted research manuscript,
\emph{manuscript~06} of batch~64, \emph{Every Logarithmic Scale
Contributes: Critical moment crossover and endpoint corrections for the
largest jump of a $132$-avoiding permutation}, dated September 30, 2026
(27 pages, US Letter, as delivered). It arrived as the archive
\texttt{ProveIt\_Critical\_Jump\_Moments.zip} (inner directory
\texttt{Critical\_Jump\_Moments/}, main file \texttt{article.tex}) in ProveIt
commit \texttt{7747fcfdd}, and was placed in commit \texttt{3025c15df}. It
was written against ProveIt commit
\begin{center}\small\cjsha,\end{center}
at which this report's \texttt{article.tex}, \texttt{README.md} and
\path{code/05-macroscopic-deficits-model.py} had the blobs
\texttt{c673f447a2}, \texttt{9b6865915b} and \texttt{f0248e0d61}, the ones
the manuscript records. All three were unchanged at the placement commit, so
every statement the manuscript makes about this report refers to Parts~I--IV
as printed above. Its code, data, figures and two provenance notes are
shipped beside the earlier parts' files with the prefix
\path{06-critical-moments-}, continuing the report's local sequence after
Part~IV's \path{05-macroscopic-deficits-}. Its manuscript, README and PDF are
not shipped and survive in the arrival commit; its checksum ledger was
verified at placement (21 of 21 files) and retired. Its three figures were
regenerated in the write phase (\cref{cjm:sec:merge}).

The manuscript contributes, in the order printed here: the centered
critical half-moment with an exact convergent constant
(\cref{cjm:thm:critical}); the first correction to every subcritical
moment, with its sign (\cref{cjm:thm:subcritical}); a two-term crossover in
the exponent window $p=1/2+\lambda/\log n$ (\cref{cjm:thm:crossover}); the
logarithmic-scale law under square-root bias with a signed two-endpoint
correction (\cref{cjm:thm:logmeasure}); the abstract uniform transfer
theory behind all four (\cref{cjm:sec:transfer,cjm:sec:window,cjm:sec:measure}),
with tilted laws and logarithm-weighted moments (\cref{cjm:sec:tilts}), a
counterexample and a stability theorem (\cref{cjm:sec:sharpness}); a
Laplace-integral formula for the microscopic constant
(\cref{cjm:prop:h-laplace}); exact finite checks through $n=64$; ten
research questions; a derivation of the exact endpoint recurrence; and a
proof audit. Every result, proof, example, remark, limitation and question of
the manuscript is printed.

\begin{resultbox}
\textbf{Status (manuscript 06 of batch 64).} The theorems of Part~V have
written mathematical proofs. They are not Lean- or Rocq-verified, have not
been externally peer reviewed, and no publication priority is claimed. The
abstract transfer theorems are proved from explicitly stated hypotheses. The
permutation application uses three results of Parts~III and~IV as proved
inputs; it therefore has the same status as those unrefereed and unformalized
parts, and a correction to an input would affect the application but not the
abstract theorems. The printed decimals of the constants are high-precision
numerical approximations, not interval certificates; the finite data and the
figures are regression checks and illustrations, not evidence for the
asymptotic statements.
\end{resultbox}

\subsection{The question of Part~IV, and what answers it}
Part~III proves the fixed-deficit limiting law of $D_n$, the sharp order
$n^{-1/2}$ of its total-variation error and the moment threshold at $p=1/2$;
at that threshold it shows only that
$\ljE\sqrt{D_n}-\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}\log n$ is bounded
(\cref{lj:thm:moments-main}). Part~IV proves the macroscopic rare-event
profile and every supercritical moment constant, but not the centered
half-moment or corrections below the threshold: those are its question
\cref{mj:sec:q-centered}, and the README's list of what Parts~III and~IV do
not claim named both.

Part~V answers the two questions \emph{relative to the proved results of
Parts~III and~IV}. It does not require a new enumeration of large
permutations or a local limit theorem. The decisive observation is that a
uniform distribution-function error can be integrated after the microscopic
power tail has been subtracted. This makes the critical exponent accessible
without trying to insert $p=1/2$ into a divergent supercritical integral.
(Part~IV expected that the question would ``require a quantitative matching
of the fixed-deficit law and the macroscopic measure near the singular
boundary''; the matching needed turns out to be supplied by Part~III's uniform
total-variation bound together with Part~IV's pointwise profile.)

There is a second, more structural conclusion. The critical moment is the
normalizing constant of a probability measure that weights a permutation by
$\sqrt{D_n}$. Under that measure, the logarithm of the deficit explores every
scale between order one and order $n$. A general theorem identifies not
only the leading uniform law on logarithmic scales, but also its first
signed correction. That correction is concentrated at the two ends of the
scale interval.

\subsection{Inherited status}
Parts~III and~IV are AI-assisted, unrefereed research proofs, and their
probabilistic theorems are not formalized in Lean or Rocq. Part~V uses their
limiting law, total-variation estimate and bulk profile as previously proved
mathematical inputs, not as machine-checked facts. Every new transfer
statement below has a complete proof from explicitly stated hypotheses. This
separation permits an independent audit: a correction to an input from
Parts~III or~IV would affect the application, but not the abstract transfer
theorem. The manuscript's pin and its record of the inspected files are in
\cref{cjm:sec:provenance}.

\subsection{Relation to public work}
Nadler~\cite{Nadler} initiated the adjacency-constrained enumeration problem
and analyzed the case of bound two. Mayama and Akita~\cite{MayamaAkita}
developed an endpoint-state approach for arbitrary fixed bounds, including
rational generating functions. Their maximum decomposition also supplies
the finite recurrence used for the exact checks of Part~V. The limit here
takes the permutation size to infinity while the statistic itself ranges
through multiple scales; it is not an additional fixed-bound rationality
result.

The literature check used the primary arXiv records of those papers and
targeted searches for the largest-jump and critical-moment questions on
September 30, 2026. It was not an exhaustive priority investigation.
Accordingly, ``new'' in the contribution ledger (\cref{cjm:tab:ledger})
means proved here beyond the explicit scope of Parts~III and~IV, not
certified absent from all other literature.

\toprule

\midrule
Algebraic limiting law and power tail & Exact centered critical moment, including a convergent constant formula\\
$O(n^{-1/2})$ total-variation estimate & First correction for every $0<p<1/2$, with its sign\\
Macroscopic deficit-tail profile & Locally uniform moment transfer and the $1/\log n$ exponent window\\
Leading moments for fixed $p>1/2$ & Critical logarithmic-scale law and two-endpoint signed correction\\
Exact finite endpoint recurrence & Independent finite regression checks; no new formalization\\

\end{table}

\subsection{Notation of Part~V}
\label{cjm:sec:notation}
The symbols $a_n^{(m)}$, $C_n$, $\Cat$, $\Av_n(132)$, $\Pi_n$, $M_n$, $D_n$,
$D$ (Part~III's limit law), $G$, $s$, $r$ (the square roots of
\eqref{lj:eq:s-r}), $N_{N,f}$, $\Psi$, $\rho$, $u$ (a threshold fraction) and
$\mathcal N_s(u)$ keep their meanings from Parts~III and~IV. Several of the

functions is Part~IV's moment constant under another name. Part~V fixes one
name for each notion (\cref{cjm:tab:notation}). In particular:
\begin{itemize}
\item the coefficient function that the manuscript writes $\mathcal K(p)$ is
 $\mathfrak m(p)$ here, because for $p>1/2$ it \emph{is} Part~IV's moment
 constant $\mathfrak m_p$ (\cref{cjm:rem:mp}). The tempting false reading is
 that $\mathfrak m(p)$ is a leading constant for every $p$: for $0<p<1/2$ it
 is the coefficient of the \emph{correction} $n^{p-1/2}$, and it is negative,
 while the leading term is the limiting moment $\ljE D^p$. The calligraphic
 $\mathcal K$ of the manuscript would also clash with Part~III's clipping
 series $\ljK(z)$, and a Greek $\kappa$ with Part~III's quantile
 $\kappa_{1-\epsilon}$, which is why Part~IV already renamed its moment

 is the adjacency bound throughout the report and $\mathfrak m_p$ is

\item the microscopic and macroscopic constants $h$ and $j$ of the manuscript
 are $\cjmic$ and $\cjmac$, since $h$ is Part~III's cutoff
 $\lfloor n/4\rfloor$ and Part~IV's cutoff, and Part~III's $c_j=C_j/4^j$ is
 always indexed by a letter;
\item the tail coefficient $a$ of the manuscript is $\mathfrak a$, since
 $a_n^{(m)}$ counts avoiders and $a$, $b$ are block sizes (as in Part~IV and
 in \cref{cjm:app:recurrence}).
\end{itemize}
