\section{The permutation model and the three inputs}
\label{cjm:sec:inputs}

\subsection{Definitions and conventions}
Recall from Part~III that a permutation $\pi\in S_n$ avoids $132$ when there
are no indices $i<j<k$ with $\pi_i<\pi_k<\pi_j$, that $\Av_n(132)$ is this
set and has $C_n=\frac1{n+1}\binom{2n}{n}$ elements, and that $\Pi_n$ is
uniform on $\Av_n(132)$. Put
\begin{equation}
 M_n=\max_{1\le i<n}|\Pi_n(i+1)-\Pi_n(i)|,
 \qquad D_n=n-M_n.
 \label{cjm:eq:statistic}
\end{equation}
For $n=1$ the empty maximum is zero, so $D_1=1$, as in Part~IV. Thus
$1\le D_n\le n$. Since $a_n^{(m)}$ counts avoiders whose adjacent
differences are all at most $m$, Part~III's \eqref{lj:eq:bridge} reads
\begin{equation}
 \frac{a_n^{(m)}}{C_n}=\ljP(M_n\le m)=\ljP(D_n\ge n-m).
 \label{cjm:eq:count-tail}
\end{equation}
All logarithms are natural. For a positive real $x$ and complex $p$, $x^p$
means $\exp(p\log x)$.

For a positive random variable $Y$ define its strict survival function by
$\mathcal S_Y(x)=\ljP(Y>x)$. Abbreviate
\[
 \mathcal S_n(x)=\ljP(D_n>x),\qquad \mathcal S(x)=\ljP(D>x),\qquad
 \mathfrak a=\frac{3}{\sqrt\pi}.
\]
Here $D$ is the positive-integer random variable of the next input. The
strict/non-strict distinction matters for exact lattice formulas but not
for the Lebesgue integrals used in the proofs. The manuscript also writes
$A_0=\mathfrak a/2=\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}$ for the critical logarithmic
coefficient; Part~V writes the fraction out.

The three inputs below are printed as the manuscript states them, although
each is a result of Parts~III or~IV: they are the entire interface between
the abstract theory and the permutations, and printing them here makes that
interface auditable in one place.

\subsection{Input I: the microscopic law}
Part~III (\cref{lj:thm:main}, \eqref{lj:eq:main-pgf}) gives the probability
generating function
\begin{equation}
 G(z)=\ljE z^D
 =\frac{(1-s)(4+8s+3s^2-s^2r)}{4(1+2s)(1+s)^2},
 \quad s=\sqrt{1-z},\quad r=\sqrt{\frac{1+s}{2}},
 \label{cjm:eq:pgf}
\end{equation}
with the branches positive on $0\le z<1$. In particular,
$\ljP(D=1)=7/48$. Its tail satisfies (\cref{lj:thm:tail-main},
\eqref{lj:eq:tail-leading})
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal S(x)=\mathfrak a x^{-1/2}+O(x^{-3/2}),\qquad x\ge1.
 \label{cjm:eq:limiting-tail}
\end{equation}
The integer version stated in Part~III implies the real version:
$\mathcal S(x)=\mathcal S(\lfloor x\rfloor)$, and changing $x$ by at most one
changes $x^{-1/2}$ by $O(x^{-3/2})$.

The leading coefficient is consistent with a direct algebraic expansion:
\begin{equation}
 1-G(z)=3s+
 \left(-\frac{23}{4}+\frac{\sqrt2}{8}\right)s^2+
 \left(\frac{43}{4}-\frac{9\sqrt2}{16}\right)s^3+O(s^4).
 \label{cjm:eq:pgf-expansion}
\end{equation}
The manuscript verifies this identity symbolically. This finite algebraic
check does not replace Part~III's limiting-law and tail proofs.
[Merge note: this is Part~III's expansion in the proof of
\cref{lj:thm:tail-main}, $3(1-z)^{-1/2}-a_2-a_3(1-z)^{1/2}+O(1-z)$ for
$\sum_k\ljP(D>k)z^k=(1-G)/s^2$ (\eqref{lj:eq:tail-gf}), with
$a_2=23/4-\sqrt2/8$ and $a_3=-43/4+9\sqrt2/16$; the two agree.]

\subsection{Input II: a uniform finite-size error}
The total-variation theorem of Part~III (\eqref{lj:eq:main-tv}, with the
explicit bound \eqref{lj:eq:explicit-tv}) implies
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{x\ge0}|\mathcal S_n(x)-\mathcal S(x)|\le K n^{-1/2}
 \label{cjm:eq:kolmogorov}
\end{equation}
for an absolute constant $K$. No value of the best constant is required.
Indeed a half-line is one of the measurable sets controlled by total
variation. The proofs below use only \eqref{cjm:eq:kolmogorov}; the full
strength of total variation is unnecessary.

\subsection{Input III: the macroscopic profile}
Part~IV's \cref{mj:thm:profile} gives
\begin{equation}
 \sqrt n\,\mathcal S_n(nu)\longrightarrow\Psi(u),\qquad 0<u\le1,
 \label{cjm:eq:bulk-limit}
\end{equation}
where, as in \eqref{mj:eq:Psi},
\begin{equation}
 \Psi(u)=
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle \mathfrak a\frac{1-2u}{\sqrt{u(1-u)}},&0<u<1/2,\\[4pt]
 0,&1/2\le u\le1.
 \end{cases}
 \label{cjm:eq:bulk-profile}
\end{equation}
Part~V needs only pointwise convergence, not a convergence rate or a local
limit. Part~IV supplies a stronger uniformity away from zero.

The formal leading density on $(0,1/2)$ is
\begin{equation}
 -\Psi'(u)=\rho(u)=\frac{\mathfrak a}{2[u(1-u)]^{3/2}}.
 \label{cjm:eq:bulk-density}
\end{equation}
This density has infinite total mass at zero. It is a rare-event limit
measure, not a probability density on the entire interval. It is the
deficit density of Part~IV (\cref{mj:thm:marked}).

\section{Main results for the largest-jump deficit}
\label{cjm:sec:main}

\subsection{The centered square-root moment}
Define the microscopic finite part
\begin{equation}
 \cjmic=1+\frac12\int_1^\infty x^{-1/2}
       \left(\mathcal S(x)-\mathfrak a x^{-1/2}\right)\,\dd x
 \label{cjm:eq:h}
\end{equation}
and the elementary macroscopic correction
\begin{equation}
 \cjmac=\mathfrak a\left(\log2-\log(1+\sqrt2)+\sqrt2-2\right).
 \label{cjm:eq:j}
\end{equation}
The integral in \eqref{cjm:eq:h} is absolutely convergent by
\eqref{cjm:eq:limiting-tail}.

\begin{theorem}[Resolution of the centered half-moment question]
\label{cjm:thm:critical}
Using the three inputs of \cref{cjm:sec:inputs},
\begin{equation}
 \ljE\sqrt{D_n}=\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}\log n+c_\star+o(1),\qquad
 c_\star=\cjmic+\cjmac.
 \label{cjm:eq:critical}
\end{equation}
The exact definitions \eqref{cjm:eq:h}--\eqref{cjm:eq:j}, together with the
algebraic function \eqref{cjm:eq:pgf}, specify $c_\star$ without a limiting
finite-size fit. Numerical quadrature gives
\[
 c_\star\approx-0.942121413999402.
\]
\end{theorem}

This answers the first question of \cref{mj:sec:q-centered} and the critical
question of Part~III's Section~\ref{mj:sec:lj-moments}, and sharpens Part~III's
\eqref{lj:eq:critical-moment} from $O(1)$ to $c_\star+o(1)$.

\subsection{Every subcritical moment has an explicit first correction}
For $\Re p>-1/2$, $p\ne1/2$, define the regularized expression
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak m(p)=\frac{\mathfrak a}2\left\{
 \frac{2^{1/2-p}}{p-1/2}+
 \int_0^{1/2}u^{p-3/2}\bigl((1-u)^{-3/2}-1\bigr)\,\dd u
 \right\}.
 \label{cjm:eq:regularized-beta}
\end{equation}
The integral converges in this domain because its bracket is $O(u)$ at
zero. Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak m(p)=\frac{\mathfrak a}2 B_{1/2}(p-1/2,-1/2),
 \label{cjm:eq:beta-shorthand}
\end{equation}
where the incomplete beta function in \eqref{cjm:eq:beta-shorthand} is
\emph{analytically continued} when $p<1/2$. The convergent expression
\eqref{cjm:eq:regularized-beta}, not a divergent unregularized integral, is
the definition used here.

\begin{theorem}[Subcritical correction]
\label{cjm:thm:subcritical}
For each fixed real $0<p<1/2$,
\begin{equation}
 \ljE D_n^p=\ljE D^p+\mathfrak m(p)n^{p-1/2}
                +o(n^{p-1/2}).
 \label{cjm:eq:subcritical}
\end{equation}
Moreover $\mathfrak m(p)<0$ throughout this interval. Consequently
$\ljE D_n^p<\ljE D^p$ for all sufficiently large $n$, for each fixed such
$p$. This last conclusion is eventual one-sided convergence, not
monotonicity in $n$.
\end{theorem}

This answers the second question of \cref{mj:sec:q-centered}, and sharpens
Part~III's \eqref{lj:eq:small-moments} from $O(n^{p-1/2})$ to an exact first
term with its sign.

For comparison, the supercritical leading coefficient of Part~IV is the
same function: for fixed $p>1/2$, $\ljE D_n^p\sim\mathfrak m(p)n^{p-1/2}$,
that is, $\mathfrak m(p)=\mathfrak m_p$ (\cref{mj:thm:moments};
\cref{cjm:rem:mp} proves the identity). Thus one regularized analytic
function governs both sides of the moment threshold, although its role is
an error coefficient below the threshold and a leading coefficient above
it. Its pole at the threshold must be canceled before one takes a limit.

\subsection{The moving critical window}
Let
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal M(\lambda)=\int_0^1e^{\lambda t}\,\dd t
 =\begin{cases}(e^\lambda-1)/\lambda,&\lambda\ne0,\\1,&\lambda=0,
 \end{cases}
 \label{cjm:eq:Phi}
\end{equation}
which is an entire function. Set
\begin{equation}
 B_0=\cjmic-\mathfrak a,\qquad B_1=\mathfrak a+\cjmac.
 \label{cjm:eq:endpoint-constants}
\end{equation}

\begin{theorem}[Two-term moment crossover]
\label{cjm:thm:crossover}
For $p_n(\lambda)=1/2+\lambda/\log n$,
\begin{equation}
 \ljE D_n^{p_n(\lambda)}
 = \frac3{2\sqrt\pi}\log n\,\mathcal M(\lambda)+B_0+B_1e^\lambda+o(1),
 \label{cjm:eq:crossover}
\end{equation}
locally uniformly for complex $\lambda$. In particular, the error is
uniform on each fixed bounded real interval. The endpoint constants have
the numerical values
\begin{align*}
 B_0&\approx-1.324620212145336,\\
 B_1&\approx\phantom{-}0.382498798145934.
\end{align*}
At $\lambda=0$, their sum is $c_\star$.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{The logarithmic-scale law and its signed correction}
Write $\vartheta_n=\log D_n/\log n$ for $n\ge2$. Define a finite measure on
$[0,1]$ by
\begin{equation}
 \eta_n(\varphi)=\ljE\bigl[\sqrt{D_n}\,\varphi(\vartheta_n)\bigr].
 \label{cjm:eq:eta}
\end{equation}
For a Lipschitz function $\varphi$, let
$\|\varphi\|_{\cjBL}=\|\varphi\|_\infty+\cjLip(\varphi)$.

\begin{theorem}[Two endpoint corrections]
\label{cjm:thm:logmeasure}
Uniformly over complex-valued $\varphi$ with $\|\varphi\|_{\cjBL}\le1$,
\begin{equation}
 \eta_n(\varphi)=\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}\log n\int_0^1\varphi(t)\,\dd t
                +B_0\varphi(0)+B_1\varphi(1)+o(1).
 \label{cjm:eq:measure-expansion}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, in the dual bounded-Lipschitz norm,
\begin{equation}
 \eta_n-\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}\log n\,\cjLeb
       \longrightarrow B_0\delta_0+B_1\delta_1.
 \label{cjm:eq:signed-limit}
\end{equation}
This is convergence of a \emph{signed residual}, not a decomposition of a
positive measure into three positive pieces.
\end{theorem}

Under the probability measure that weights $\Pi_n$ proportionally to
$\sqrt{D_n}$, $\vartheta_n$ therefore converges weakly to
$\mathrm{Unif}[0,1]$. For real $\lambda$, weighting instead by
$D_n^{p_n(\lambda)}$ produces the limiting density
\begin{equation}
 \frac{e^{\lambda t}}{\mathcal M(\lambda)},\qquad 0\le t\le1.
 \label{cjm:eq:tilted-density}
\end{equation}
The normalization and the first correction to this probability law are
proved in \cref{cjm:sec:tilts}. \Cref{cjm:thm:logmeasure} is the critical
analogue of Part~IV's power-biased law \eqref{mj:eq:power-biased} and its
size-biased limit (\cref{mj:thm:uniform}), which live on the scale $D_n/n$
for $p>1/2$; at $p=1/2$ the natural scale is logarithmic.

\section{A general uniform moment-transfer theorem}
\label{cjm:sec:transfer}
This section contains the common argument behind all the application
results. It is independent of permutations and of the rest of the report.

\subsection{Hypotheses and regularized functions}
Let $X,X_n$ be real random variables with $X\ge1$ and $1\le X_n\le n$.
Write $\mathcal S(x)=\ljP(X>x)$ and $\mathcal S_n(x)=\ljP(X_n>x)$. Suppose that
constants $\alpha,\mathfrak a,\beta>0$ satisfy the following three
conditions:
\begin{align}
 \mathcal S(x)&=\mathfrak a x^{-\alpha}+\varrho(x),&
 |\varrho(x)|&\le K x^{-\alpha-\beta}\quad(x\ge1),
 \label{cjm:eq:A1}\\
 \sup_{x\ge0}|\mathcal S_n(x)-\mathcal S(x)|&\le K n^{-\alpha},
 \label{cjm:eq:A2}\\
 n^\alpha \mathcal S_n(nu)&\longrightarrow\Psi(u)
 &&(0<u\le1).
 \label{cjm:eq:A3}
\end{align}
Constants denoted by $K$ may be enlarged. Set
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak b(u)=\Psi(u)-\mathfrak a u^{-\alpha},\qquad 0<u\le1,
 \label{cjm:eq:b-general}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{align}
 \mathcal H(p)&=1+p\int_1^\infty x^{p-1}\varrho(x)\,\dd x,
              &&\Re p<\alpha+\beta,
 \label{cjm:eq:H-general}\\
 \mathcal J(p)&=p\int_0^1u^{p-1}\mathfrak b(u)\,\dd u,
              &&\Re p>0.
 \label{cjm:eq:J-general}
\end{align}
In this section $\Psi$ is any function satisfying \eqref{cjm:eq:A3}; in the
application it is Part~IV's profile \eqref{cjm:eq:bulk-profile}.

\begin{lemma}[The cancellation is uniformly bounded]
\label{cjm:lem:bounded}
The function $\mathfrak b$ is bounded on $(0,1]$. The integrals defining
$\mathcal H$ and $\mathcal J$ converge absolutely and define holomorphic
functions in their indicated half-planes.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For fixed $u>0$, \eqref{cjm:eq:A1} implies
$n^\alpha\mathcal S(nu)\to\mathfrak a u^{-\alpha}$. Subtracting this limit
from \eqref{cjm:eq:A3} and applying \eqref{cjm:eq:A2} gives
$|\mathfrak b(u)|\le K$. For $p$ in a compact subset of
$\Re p<\alpha+\beta$, the integrand for $\mathcal H$ is bounded by a
constant times $x^{-1-\varepsilon}$ for some $\varepsilon>0$. For $p$ in a
compact subset of $\Re p>0$, the integrand for $\mathcal J$ is bounded by a
constant times $u^{-1+\varepsilon}$. Additional powers of $|\log x|$ or
$|\log u|$ remain integrable, so differentiation under the integral is
justified locally. This proves holomorphy.
\end{proof}

Define a scaled tail discrepancy
\begin{equation}
 g_n(u)=n^\alpha\bigl(\mathcal S_n(nu)-\mathcal S(nu)\bigr),\qquad 0<u\le1.
 \label{cjm:eq:g}
\end{equation}
It is bounded by $K$, vanishes when $u<1/n$, and converges pointwise to
$\mathfrak b(u)$. The vanishing follows because both survival functions are
one on $0\le x<1$.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform integral convergence]
\label{cjm:lem:Jconv}
Put $\mathcal J_n(p)=p\int_0^1u^{p-1}g_n(u)\,\dd u$. On every compact subset
of $\Re p>0$, $\mathcal J_n\to\mathcal J$ uniformly.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $\sigma=\inf\Re p>0$ and $p^\ast=\sup|p|$ on the compact set, then
\[
 \sup_p|\mathcal J_n(p)-\mathcal J(p)|
 \le p^\ast\int_0^1u^{\sigma-1}|g_n(u)-\mathfrak b(u)|\,\dd u.
\]
The integrand tends to zero pointwise and is bounded by
$2K u^{\sigma-1}$. Dominated convergence proves the assertion.
\end{proof}

\subsection{An exact identity before any asymptotics}
For $\Re p>0$, finite support and the fundamental theorem of calculus give
\begin{equation}
 \ljE X_n^p=1+p\int_1^n x^{p-1}\mathcal S_n(x)\,\dd x.
 \label{cjm:eq:tail-moment}
\end{equation}
This is also valid for complex $p$, by applying Fubini to the absolutely
integrable identity $y^p-1=p\int_1^y x^{p-1}\,\dd x$.

\begin{lemma}[Exact decomposition]
\label{cjm:lem:exact}
For $0<\Re p<\alpha+\beta$,
\begin{align}
 \ljE X_n^p={}&\mathcal H(p)+\mathfrak a p\frac{n^{p-\alpha}-1}{p-\alpha}
       +n^{p-\alpha}\mathcal J_n(p)\notag\\
       &-p\int_n^\infty x^{p-1}\varrho(x)\,\dd x.
 \label{cjm:eq:exact-decomposition}
\end{align}
At $p=\alpha$, the quotient is interpreted as $\log n$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
In \eqref{cjm:eq:tail-moment}, split
$\mathcal S_n=\mathfrak a x^{-\alpha}+\varrho+(\mathcal S_n-\mathcal S)$.
Integrating the first term yields the quotient. Complete the second integral
to infinity and subtract its tail. In the last term set $x=nu$. The resulting
lower limit $1/n$ can be replaced by zero because $g_n$ vanishes there. These
are exact operations, not formal asymptotic manipulations.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Locally uniform complex-moment transfer]
\label{cjm:thm:transfer}
Under \eqref{cjm:eq:A1}--\eqref{cjm:eq:A3}, for every compact set
$\mathcal D\subset\{p:0<\Re p<\alpha+\beta\}$,
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{p\in\mathcal D}\left|
 n^{\alpha-p}\left(
 \ljE X_n^p-\mathcal H(p)-\mathfrak a p\frac{n^{p-\alpha}-1}{p-\alpha}
 \right)-\mathcal J(p)\right|\longrightarrow0.
 \label{cjm:eq:transfer}
\end{equation}
The singularity at $p=\alpha$ is removable in the displayed expression.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{cjm:lem:exact}, the expression inside the absolute value is
\[
 \mathcal J_n(p)-\mathcal J(p)-p n^{\alpha-p}\int_n^\infty x^{p-1}\varrho(x)\,\dd x.
\]
The first term tends uniformly to zero by \cref{cjm:lem:Jconv}. For the
second, let $\tau=\sup_{p\in\mathcal D}\Re p<\alpha+\beta$ and
$p^\ast=\sup_{p\in\mathcal D}|p|$. Then its absolute value is at most
\[
 p^\ast K n^{\alpha-\Re p}
 \frac{n^{\Re p-\alpha-\beta}}{\alpha+\beta-\Re p}
 \le\frac{p^\ast K}{\alpha+\beta-\tau}n^{-\beta}.
\]
This bound is uniform. The quotient in \eqref{cjm:eq:transfer} is an entire
function of $p$, after filling its value at $p=\alpha$, which verifies the
last assertion.
\end{proof}

\subsection{What uniformity does and does not buy}
For a fixed $p$ in the strip, \cref{cjm:thm:transfer} gives an error
$o(n^{\Re p-\alpha})$ in the unscaled moment expression. At $p=\alpha$,
this is $o(1)$. At fixed real $p>\alpha$, it need not be $o(1)$.
Consequently, extracting the constant-looking term from the formula for a
fixed supercritical exponent does \emph{not} establish a second-order
constant. A quantitative rate for $\mathcal J_n(p)-\mathcal J(p)$ would be
required.

The moving window $p-\alpha=O(1/\log n)$ is different:
$n^{p-\alpha}$ stays bounded on compact windows. Precisely there, the
uniform theorem upgrades the unscaled error to $o(1)$ and yields a genuine
two-term expansion.

\section{Fixed exponents, the critical constant, and beta regularization}
\label{cjm:sec:constants}

\subsection{The general subcritical and critical formulas}
For real $0<p<\alpha$, the limiting moment exists and
\begin{equation}
 \ljE X^p=\mathcal H(p)+\frac{\mathfrak a p}{\alpha-p}.
 \label{cjm:eq:limit-moment}
\end{equation}
This follows from the same tail integration as before, now on $[1,\infty)$.
Define
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak m(p)=\frac{\mathfrak a p}{p-\alpha}+\mathcal J(p).
 \label{cjm:eq:K-general}
\end{equation}
(In the application, $\alpha=1/2$, this is the function
\eqref{cjm:eq:regularized-beta}, by \cref{cjm:lem:beta}.) Then
\cref{cjm:thm:transfer} gives
\begin{equation}
 \ljE X_n^p=\ljE X^p+\mathfrak m(p)n^{p-\alpha}
                          +o(n^{p-\alpha}),\qquad0<p<\alpha.
 \label{cjm:eq:subcritical-general}
\end{equation}
At the critical exponent itself it gives
\begin{equation}
 \ljE X_n^\alpha=\mathfrak a\alpha\log n+\mathcal H(\alpha)+\mathcal J(\alpha)+o(1).
 \label{cjm:eq:critical-general}
\end{equation}
Thus the finite constant splits into a microscopic term determined by the
entire limiting law and a macroscopic term determined by the bulk profile.
Neither term alone supplies the answer.

\subsection{Applying the theorem to the permutation inputs}
Equations \eqref{cjm:eq:limiting-tail}--\eqref{cjm:eq:bulk-limit} satisfy
the general hypotheses with
\[
 \alpha=\frac12,\qquad\beta=1,\qquad \mathfrak a=\frac3{\sqrt\pi}.
\]
In particular, the abstract uniform strip includes $0<\Re p<3/2$. Formula
\eqref{cjm:eq:h} is exactly $\mathcal H(1/2)$. It remains to evaluate
$\mathcal J(1/2)$ and identify \eqref{cjm:eq:K-general} with the
regularized beta expression.

\begin{lemma}[Evaluation of the macroscopic critical contribution]
\label{cjm:lem:j}
For the profile \eqref{cjm:eq:bulk-profile}, $\mathcal J(1/2)=\cjmac$ as
defined in \eqref{cjm:eq:j}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By direct substitution,
\begin{align}
 \mathcal J(1/2)=\frac{\mathfrak a}2\bigg\{
 &\int_0^{1/2}\left(\frac{1-2u}{u\sqrt{1-u}}-\frac1u\right)\,\dd u
   -\int_{1/2}^1\frac{\dd u}{u}\bigg\}.
 \label{cjm:eq:j-integral}
\end{align}
The first integrand is bounded at zero; its two displayed summands must
not be integrated separately there. An antiderivative of their difference
is
\[
 \mathcal F(u)=-2\operatorname{artanh}\sqrt{1-u}
                 +4\sqrt{1-u}-\log u.
\]
As $u\downarrow0$, $\mathcal F(u)\to4-\log4$. At $u=1/2$,
$\operatorname{artanh}(1/\sqrt2)=\log(1+\sqrt2)$, so
\[
 \mathcal F(1/2)=-2\log(1+\sqrt2)+2\sqrt2+\log2.
\]
Subtract $\mathcal F(0+)$ and then the last integral $\log2$ in
\eqref{cjm:eq:j-integral}. The result is
\[
 \frac{\mathfrak a}2\bigl(\log4-2\log(1+\sqrt2)+2\sqrt2-4\bigr)=\cjmac.
\]
\end{proof}
Combining \eqref{cjm:eq:critical-general} with \cref{cjm:lem:j} proves
\cref{cjm:thm:critical}.

\begin{lemma}[A single coefficient function on both sides of the threshold]
\label{cjm:lem:beta}
For $\Re p>0$, $p\ne1/2$, the expression
$\mathfrak ap/(p-1/2)+\mathcal J(p)$ equals \eqref{cjm:eq:regularized-beta}.
In particular its residue at $p=1/2$ is $\mathfrak a/2$, and its finite part
there is $\mathfrak a+\cjmac=B_1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
First take real $p>1/2$. The singular integrals are then separately
convergent, and
\[
 \frac{\mathfrak ap}{p-1/2}+\mathcal J(p)=p\int_0^{1/2}u^{p-1}\Psi(u)\,\dd u.
\]
Since $\Psi(1/2)=0$, $u^p\Psi(u)\to0$ at zero, and
$-\Psi'=\rho$, integration by parts gives
\[
 p\int_0^{1/2}u^{p-1}\Psi(u)\,\dd u
 =\frac{\mathfrak a}2\int_0^{1/2}u^{p-3/2}(1-u)^{-3/2}\,\dd u.
\]
Subtract one from $(1-u)^{-3/2}$ and integrate the subtracted power
explicitly. This gives \eqref{cjm:eq:regularized-beta}.
Both sides are holomorphic on the connected domain
$\{\Re p>0\}\setminus\{1/2\}$, so the identity extends throughout that
domain by analytic continuation. Finally,
\[
 \frac{\mathfrak ap}{p-1/2}=\frac{\mathfrak a/2}{p-1/2}+\mathfrak a,
 \qquad \mathcal J(p)=\cjmac+O(p-1/2),
\]
which gives the residue and the finite part.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Merge note: $\mathfrak m(p)$ is Part~IV's $\mathfrak m_p$ for $p>1/2$]
\label{cjm:rem:mp}
The second display of the proof of \cref{cjm:lem:beta} shows, for real
$p>1/2$,
\[
 \mathfrak m(p)=\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}\int_0^{1/2}u^{p-3/2}(1-u)^{-3/2}\,\dd u,
\]
which is Part~IV's first formula \eqref{mj:eq:kappa-beta} for
$\mathfrak m_p$. Hence $\mathfrak m(p)=\mathfrak m_p$ for every $p>1/2$, and
the manuscript's sentence that the supercritical leading coefficient ``is the
same function'' is an identity, not only a numerical coincidence (the
write phase also checked it numerically at $p=3/4,1,3/2,2,3$, to twenty
digits against \eqref{mj:eq:kappa-t}). For $1/2<p<3/2$ it is also what
\cref{cjm:thm:transfer} says directly, since there the transfer theorem
gives the limit of \cref{mj:thm:moments}:
\[
 n^{1/2-p}\ljE D_n^p\longrightarrow\mathfrak m(p).
\]
Part~IV's \cref{mj:prop:constantlimits} gives the pole
\eqref{mj:eq:p-half} as $p\downarrow1/2$; \cref{cjm:lem:beta} sharpens it
to
\[
 \mathfrak m_p=\frac{3}{2\sqrt\pi}\,\frac{1}{p-1/2}+B_1+O(p-1/2),
\]
so the finite part of Part~IV's pole is $B_1\approx0.382498798$, the
constant of \cref{cjm:thm:crossover}. Below the threshold, the coefficient
$\mathfrak m(p)$, $0<p<1/2$, of the correction in \eqref{cjm:eq:subcritical}
is the analytic continuation of Part~IV's constants through that pole.
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{cjm:thm:subcritical}]
The expansion follows from \eqref{cjm:eq:subcritical-general} and
\cref{cjm:lem:beta}. To prove the sign, observe that
\[
 \frac{1-2u}{\sqrt{1-u}}<1\quad(0<u<1/2).
\]
Indeed both sides are positive, and squaring reduces the claim to
$u(4u-3)<0$. For $1/2\le u\le1$ the bulk profile is zero. Therefore
$\mathfrak b(u)=\Psi(u)-\mathfrak a u^{-1/2}<0$ on $(0,1]$, so
$\mathcal J(p)<0$ for every real $p>0$. If $p<1/2$, the term
$\mathfrak ap/(p-1/2)$ is also negative. Hence $\mathfrak m(p)<0$. Dividing
the remainder in \eqref{cjm:eq:subcritical} by its strictly negative
leading coefficient proves eventual one-sidedness.
\end{proof}

\section{The \texorpdfstring{1/log\,\textit{n}}{1/log n} critical exponent window}
\label{cjm:sec:window}

\begin{theorem}[General two-term crossover]
\label{cjm:thm:general-window}
Under the hypotheses of \cref{cjm:thm:transfer}, let
$p_n(\lambda)=\alpha+\lambda/\log n$. Define
\[
 B_0=\mathcal H(\alpha)-\mathfrak a,\qquad B_1=\mathfrak a+\mathcal J(\alpha).
\]
Then
\begin{equation}
 \ljE X_n^{p_n(\lambda)}
 =\mathfrak a\alpha\log n\,\mathcal M(\lambda)+B_0+B_1e^\lambda+o(1)
 \label{cjm:eq:general-window}
\end{equation}
locally uniformly for complex $\lambda$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix a compact set of $\lambda$ values. For large $n$, all $p_n(\lambda)$
lie in a fixed compact subset of the strip in \cref{cjm:thm:transfer}. In
addition $n^{p_n-\alpha}=e^\lambda$ is uniformly bounded. The theorem
therefore gives, uniformly on the chosen compact set,
\[
 \ljE X_n^{p_n}=\mathcal H(p_n)+\mathfrak a p_n\log n\,\mathcal M(\lambda)
                     +e^\lambda \mathcal J(p_n)+o(1).
\]
Continuity gives $\mathcal H(p_n)=\mathcal H(\alpha)+o(1)$ and
$\mathcal J(p_n)=\mathcal J(\alpha)+o(1)$. The remaining term is exactly
\[
 \mathfrak a p_n\log n\,\mathcal M(\lambda)
 =\mathfrak a\alpha\log n\,\mathcal M(\lambda)+\mathfrak a\lambda\mathcal M(\lambda)
 =\mathfrak a\alpha\log n\,\mathcal M(\lambda)+\mathfrak a(e^\lambda-1).
\]
Collecting constants proves \eqref{cjm:eq:general-window}, including at
$\lambda=0$ by continuity.
\end{proof}
\Cref{cjm:thm:crossover} is the specialization $\alpha=1/2$ with the
constants already computed.

\subsection{Why the width is logarithmic}
The crossover width is forced by the factor $n^{p-\alpha}$:
\[
 n^{p-\alpha}=\exp((p-\alpha)\log n).
\]
It changes by a constant factor when $p-\alpha$ changes by order
$1/\log n$. At that scale, the pole cancellation between the microscopic
limiting moment and the macroscopic contribution is visible in the entire
function $\mathcal M$.

For real $\lambda$ one has $\mathcal M(\lambda)>0$. The asymptotic expression
never asks for a moment of the limiting variable $X$ above its convergence
threshold; it concerns the finite variable $X_n$ throughout. In particular,
using $\ljE X^{p_n}$ for positive $\lambda$ would be invalid.

\subsection{An exact model that checks the normalization}
\begin{example}[A truncated Pareto variable]
\label{cjm:ex:pareto}
Let $\ljP(X>x)=x^{-\alpha}$ for $x\ge1$, and put $X_n=\min(X,n)$.
Here $\mathfrak a=1$, $\varrho=0$, $\mathcal H=1$, and $\mathfrak b=0$ almost
everywhere on $(0,1)$. The value of $\mathfrak b$ at $u=1$ is immaterial to
all integrals. Thus $\mathcal J=0$, $B_0=0$, and $B_1=1$. Direct calculation
gives the exact identities
\begin{align}
 \ljE X_n^p&=1+p\frac{n^{p-\alpha}-1}{p-\alpha},\\
 \ljE X_n^{\alpha+\lambda/\log n}&=\alpha\log n\,\mathcal M(\lambda)+e^\lambda.
 \label{cjm:eq:pareto-exact}
\end{align}
The atom at $X_n=n$ contributes exactly one to the critical weighted mass.
It is the endpoint term $B_1$, not an extra piece of the logarithmic
coefficient. This elementary example checks both the factor $\alpha$ in
the leading term and the distinction between $\mathcal J(\alpha)$ and $B_1$.
\end{example}

\section{The logarithmic-scale measure and endpoint theorem}
\label{cjm:sec:measure}
The moment crossover probes the test functions $e^{\lambda t}$. A direct
argument treats the entire bounded-Lipschitz class at once and gives a
stronger conclusion.

\subsection{Statement in the general setting}
Put $\vartheta_n=\log X_n/\log n$ and define
\[
 \eta_n(\varphi)=\ljE[X_n^\alpha\varphi(\vartheta_n)].
\]
Let $\cjLeb$ be Lebesgue measure on $[0,1]$ and define, for a finite signed
or complex measure $\nu$,
\[
 \|\nu\|_{\cjBL^*}
 =\sup_{\|\varphi\|_\infty+\cjLip(\varphi)\le1}|\nu(\varphi)|.
\]

\begin{theorem}[General two-endpoint correction]
\label{cjm:thm:general-measure}
Under \eqref{cjm:eq:A1}--\eqref{cjm:eq:A3},
\begin{equation}
 \|\eta_n-\mathfrak a\alpha\log n\,\cjLeb-B_0\delta_0-B_1\delta_1\|_{\cjBL^*}
 \longrightarrow0,
 \label{cjm:eq:BL-general}
\end{equation}
where $B_0=\mathcal H(\alpha)-\mathfrak a$ and
$B_1=\mathfrak a+\mathcal J(\alpha)$. More precisely, the norm is at most
\begin{equation}
 |\mathcal J_n(\alpha)-\mathcal J(\alpha)|+
 O\bigl((\log n)^{-1}+n^{-\beta}\bigr),
 \label{cjm:eq:BL-bound}
\end{equation}
with a constant depending only on the hypotheses.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Proof by three integrals}
\begin{proof}
For a Lipschitz function $\varphi$ on
$[0,1]$, let
\[
 f(x)=x^\alpha\varphi(\log x/\log n),\qquad1\le x\le n.
\]
This is absolutely continuous, with almost-everywhere derivative
\begin{equation}
 f'(x)=x^{\alpha-1}
 \left\{\alpha\varphi(\log x/\log n)+\frac1{\log n}\varphi'(\log x/\log n)\right\}.
 \label{cjm:eq:f-derivative}
\end{equation}
The derivative is integrable, so Fubini gives
\begin{equation}
 \eta_n(\varphi)=\varphi(0)+\int_1^n f'(x)\mathcal S_n(x)\,\dd x.
 \label{cjm:eq:weighted-tail}
\end{equation}
Split the survival function into its power tail, remainder and finite-size
discrepancy, exactly as in \cref{cjm:lem:exact}.

\paragraph{The power tail.}
The part with $\mathfrak a x^{-\alpha}$ equals
\begin{align}
 \mathfrak a\int_1^n\frac{\alpha\varphi(\log x/\log n)+(\log n)^{-1}\varphi'(\log x/\log n)}{x}\,\dd x
 =\mathfrak a\alpha\log n\int_0^1\varphi(t)\,\dd t+\mathfrak a(\varphi(1)-\varphi(0)).
 \label{cjm:eq:measure-power}
\end{align}
This identity explains why two endpoint terms occur even before the
microscopic and macroscopic corrections are considered.

\paragraph{The microscopic remainder.}
Combine the initial term $\varphi(0)$ in \eqref{cjm:eq:weighted-tail} with
the integral involving $\varrho(x)$. Uniformly for
$\|\varphi\|_{\cjBL}\le1$, their sum is
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal H(\alpha)\varphi(0)+O((\log n)^{-1}+n^{-\beta}).
 \label{cjm:eq:measure-micro}
\end{equation}
Indeed $|\varphi(\log x/\log n)-\varphi(0)|\le\log x/\log n$, and
$|\varphi'|\le1$ almost everywhere. The error from replacing $\varphi$
by $\varphi(0)$ and dropping the derivative term is bounded by
\[
 \frac1{\log n}\int_1^\infty x^{\alpha-1}|\varrho(x)|(\alpha\log x+1)\,\dd x,
\]
which is finite by \eqref{cjm:eq:A1}. Completing the remaining integral to
infinity costs $O(n^{-\beta})$.

\paragraph{The finite-size discrepancy.}
After $x=nu$, this contribution is
\begin{align}
 \int_{1/n}^1u^{\alpha-1}g_n(u)
 \left\{\alpha\varphi\left(1+\frac{\log u}{\log n}\right)
 +\frac1{\log n}\varphi'\left(1+\frac{\log u}{\log n}\right)\right\}\,\dd u.
 \label{cjm:eq:measure-finite}
\end{align}
The bound $|g_n|\le K$ and the Lipschitz property show that replacing the
function by $\varphi(1)$ and deleting its derivative costs at most
\[
 \frac K{\log n}\int_0^1u^{\alpha-1}(\alpha|\log u|+1)\,\dd u=O((\log n)^{-1}).
\]
The remaining integral is exactly $\mathcal J_n(\alpha)\varphi(1)$, since
$g_n=0$ on $(0,1/n)$. Replacing $\mathcal J_n(\alpha)$ by $\mathcal J(\alpha)$
costs at most $|\mathcal J_n(\alpha)-\mathcal J(\alpha)|$.

Adding \eqref{cjm:eq:measure-power}--\eqref{cjm:eq:measure-finite}, the
coefficient of $\varphi(0)$ is $\mathcal H(\alpha)-\mathfrak a$, and that of
$\varphi(1)$ is $\mathfrak a+\mathcal J(\alpha)$. The errors are uniform on
the unit ball, proving \eqref{cjm:eq:BL-bound}. Finally \cref{cjm:lem:Jconv}
proves convergence.
\end{proof}

Applying this theorem with $\alpha=1/2$ proves \cref{cjm:thm:logmeasure}.
The same proof also shows exactly what quantitative information is missing
for a rate: it is the single weighted integral discrepancy
$\mathcal J_n(\alpha)-\mathcal J(\alpha)$, not a new pointwise local limit.

\subsection{The topology is essential}
For the permutation application, every $\eta_n$ is supported on finitely
many points $\log d/\log n$. Its critical normalization therefore has
total-variation distance one from the continuous uniform distribution,
with the convention $\ljTV(\mu,\nu)=\sup_A|\mu(A)-\nu(A)|$ (Part~III's).
Weak or bounded-Lipschitz convergence is the correct conclusion.

Moreover, $B_0<0$ in this application. There is no contradiction with
positivity of $\eta_n$: the signed residual after removing
$\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}\log n\,\cjLeb$ need not be positive. Nor does an atom in
the correction mean that the original discrete measure has a limiting atom
at that endpoint under probability normalization.

\section{Tilted laws, logarithmic moments, and interpretation}
\label{cjm:sec:tilts}

\subsection{A full first correction under power bias}
For real $\lambda$ define the probability measure $\ljP^\lambda_n$, with
expectation $\ljE^\lambda_n$, by
\begin{equation}
 \ljE^\lambda_n\varphi(\vartheta_n)
 =\frac{\ljE[X_n^{\alpha+\lambda/\log n}\varphi(\vartheta_n)]}
        {\ljE X_n^{\alpha+\lambda/\log n}}.
 \label{cjm:eq:Q}
\end{equation}
Let
\[
 \varpi_\lambda(\varphi)
 =\frac{\int_0^1e^{\lambda t}\varphi(t)\,\dd t}{\mathcal M(\lambda)}.
\]

\begin{corollary}[Tilted logarithmic-scale laws]
\label{cjm:cor:tilt}
Uniformly for real $\lambda$ in a compact interval and
$\|\varphi\|_{\cjBL}\le1$,
\begin{align}
 \ljE^\lambda_n\varphi(\vartheta_n)=\varpi_\lambda(\varphi)
 +\frac{B_0[\varphi(0)-\varpi_\lambda(\varphi)]
       +B_1e^\lambda[\varphi(1)-\varpi_\lambda(\varphi)]}
       {\mathfrak a\alpha\log n\,\mathcal M(\lambda)}
 +o((\log n)^{-1}).
 \label{cjm:eq:tilt-correction}
\end{align}
In particular $\vartheta_n$ converges weakly under $\ljP^\lambda_n$ to the
density $e^{\lambda t}/\mathcal M(\lambda)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{cjm:thm:general-measure} to
$\varphi_\lambda(t)=e^{\lambda t}\varphi(t)$. Its bounded-Lipschitz norm is
uniformly bounded for the stated range of $\lambda$ and $\varphi$. This gives
\[
 \ljE[X_n^{\alpha+\lambda/\log n}\varphi(\vartheta_n)]
 =\mathfrak a\alpha\log n\int_0^1e^{\lambda t}\varphi(t)\,\dd t
   +B_0\varphi(0)+B_1e^\lambda\varphi(1)+o(1).
\]
The denominator has the expansion \eqref{cjm:eq:general-window} and has a
positive leading coefficient uniformly bounded away from zero after
division by $\log n$. Expanding the ratio to order $1/\log n$ gives
\eqref{cjm:eq:tilt-correction}. Bounded-Lipschitz convergence implies weak
convergence on the compact interval $[0,1]$.
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering\includegraphics[width=.86\linewidth]{figures/06-critical-moments-tilted_densities.pdf}
\caption{The limiting densities $e^{\lambda t}/\mathcal M(\lambda)$
(manuscript~06's $e^{\lambda t}/\Phi(\lambda)$), for $\lambda=-2,0,2$.
At critical square-root bias, $\lambda=0$ and the density is uniform.
These curves are the exact functions in the theorem, not fitted data.}
\label{cjm:fig:tilts}
\end{figure}

At critical bias, each fixed interval $(s,t)\subset(0,1)$ receives limiting
probability $t-s$. Equivalently, for $0<s<t<1$,
\begin{equation}
 \ljE\bigl[X_n^\alpha;\ n^s<X_n<n^t\bigr]
       \sim \mathfrak a\alpha(t-s)\log n.
 \label{cjm:eq:interval-scales}
\end{equation}
This leading statement follows from weak convergence because interval
boundaries have zero limiting mass. The second-order formula
\eqref{cjm:eq:tilt-correction} is asserted for Lipschitz test functions, not
for arbitrary discontinuous indicators.

\subsection{Logarithm-weighted critical moments}
\begin{corollary}[All fixed logarithmic powers]
\label{cjm:cor:logmoments}
For each fixed integer $k\ge1$,
\begin{equation}
 \ljE[X_n^\alpha(\log X_n)^k]
 =\frac{\mathfrak a\alpha}{k+1}(\log n)^{k+1}
      +B_1(\log n)^k+o((\log n)^k).
 \label{cjm:eq:log-moments}
\end{equation}
For $k=0$ the corresponding constant is $B_0+B_1$, as in
\eqref{cjm:eq:critical-general}.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Use $\varphi(t)=t^k$ in \cref{cjm:thm:general-measure} and multiply by
$(\log n)^k$. Its integral is $1/(k+1)$, its value at zero is zero, and its
value at one is one. No differentiation of an asymptotic expansion is
needed.
\end{proof}

For the permutation model the case $k=1$ reads
\begin{equation}
 \ljE[\sqrt{D_n}\log D_n]
   =\frac{3}{4\sqrt\pi}(\log n)^2+B_1\log n+o(\log n).
 \label{cjm:eq:log-first}
\end{equation}
It also follows that the critical-biased mean scale satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \ljE^0_n\vartheta_n=\frac12+\frac{B_1-B_0}{\mathfrak a\log n}
                            +o((\log n)^{-1}).
 \label{cjm:eq:biased-mean}
\end{equation}
Here $B_1-B_0>0$. The slow positive correction helps explain the
finite-size illustrations in \cref{cjm:sec:computation}; the theorem does not
predict monotonicity at moderate sizes.

\subsection{Two different notions of typical behavior}
Under the original uniform distribution on avoiders, $D_n$ converges to a
proper finite-valued variable $D$. It is therefore tight, and
$\log D_n/\log n\to0$ in probability. Under square-root bias, the same
logarithmic ratio instead tends to a uniform distribution. These statements
refer to different sampling measures and do not conflict.

The critical bias compensates exactly for the power-law decay: a scale
increment $\dd(\log x)$ contributes asymptotically the same amount to
$\ljE X_n^\alpha$. Positive $\lambda$ in the moving exponent window favors
larger logarithmic scales; negative $\lambda$ favors smaller ones. Both
still retain a nondegenerate distribution over the entire interval for
fixed finite $\lambda$.

\section{Sharpness and stability of the transfer mechanism}
\label{cjm:sec:sharpness}

\subsection{The uniform tail error cannot be discarded}
The limiting law and the bulk profile alone do not determine the centered
critical moment. An intermediate-scale perturbation can be invisible to
both while leaving an order-one critical contribution.

\begin{proposition}[A surviving intermediate-scale correction]
\label{cjm:prop:counterexample}
Fix $\alpha>0$, $0<\theta<1$, and $c>0$. There are random variables
$Y_n\in[1,n]$ having the same distributional limit and the same pointwise
bulk profile as the truncated Pareto variables of \cref{cjm:ex:pareto}, but
\begin{equation}
 \ljE Y_n^{\alpha+\lambda/\log n}
 =\alpha\log n\,\mathcal M(\lambda)+e^\lambda
                 +c e^{\lambda\theta}+o(1)
 \label{cjm:eq:counterexample-window}
\end{equation}
locally uniformly for complex $\lambda$. Their critical logarithmic
weighted residual converges in $\cjBL^*$ to
$\delta_1+c\delta_\theta$, not just the two-endpoint measure.
They do not satisfy \eqref{cjm:eq:A2} with order $n^{-\alpha}$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For sufficiently large $n$, put $\varepsilon_n=c n^{-\alpha\theta}\le1$.
With probability $1-\varepsilon_n$, let $Y_n$ have the law of
$X_n=\min(X,n)$ from \cref{cjm:ex:pareto}; with probability
$\varepsilon_n$, set $Y_n=n^\theta$. Define the finitely many smaller
$n$ arbitrarily in $[1,n]$.

Because $\varepsilon_n\to0$, $Y_n$ has the same limiting law as $X_n$.
For fixed $0<u<1$, the added atom lies below $nu$ for large $n$, so
\[
 n^\alpha\ljP(Y_n>nu)=(1-\varepsilon_n)u^{-\alpha}
 \longrightarrow u^{-\alpha}.
\]
At $u=1$ both survival functions vanish. Thus the bulk profile is also
unchanged. On the other hand, the new atom contributes
\[
 \varepsilon_n(n^\theta)^{\alpha+\lambda/\log n}
                  =c e^{\lambda\theta}.
\]
Removing the fraction $\varepsilon_n$ of the original law changes its
moment by $O(\varepsilon_n\log n)=o(1)$ uniformly on compact $\lambda$
sets, by \eqref{cjm:eq:pareto-exact}. This proves
\eqref{cjm:eq:counterexample-window}.

For the weighted-measure assertion, the critical contribution of the new
atom is exactly $c\varphi(\theta)$, and deleting the corresponding
fraction of the Pareto weighted measure costs
$O(\varepsilon_n\log n)\|\varphi\|_\infty=o(1)$ uniformly. Finally,
at $x=n^\theta/2$, for large $n$, the survival discrepancy from the
untruncated Pareto law equals
\[
 \varepsilon_n(1-x^{-\alpha})\sim c n^{-\alpha\theta},
\]
which is not $O(n^{-\alpha})$.
\end{proof}

This example explains the role of Part~III's total-variation theorem.
It excludes persistent critical weighted mass at intermediate logarithmic
scales. The bulk profile by itself would not exclude such mass and would
not resolve the centered half-moment question.

\subsection{Robustness under smaller perturbations}
\begin{proposition}[Stability at the relevant error scale]
\label{cjm:prop:stability}
Suppose $X_n$ satisfies the general hypotheses, $1\le Y_n\le n$, and
\[
 \delta_n:=\sup_x|\ljP(Y_n>x)-\ljP(X_n>x)|=o(n^{-\alpha}).
\]
Then the uniform transfer expansion, critical crossover, and signed
bounded-Lipschitz endpoint correction hold for $Y_n$ with the same
functions $\mathcal H,\mathcal J$ and the same constants $B_0,B_1$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $p$ in a compact subset $\mathcal D$ of $\Re p>0$, tail integration
bounds
\[
 |\ljE Y_n^p-\ljE X_n^p|
 \le |p|\delta_n\int_1^n x^{\Re p-1}\,\dd x
 \le K_{\mathcal D}\delta_n n^{\Re p}.
\]
After multiplication by $n^{\alpha-\Re p}$ this is $o(1)$ uniformly.
For a test function in the unit bounded-Lipschitz ball, use
\eqref{cjm:eq:f-derivative} to bound the corresponding weighted expectation
difference by $K\delta_n n^\alpha=o(1)$. These estimates transfer all the
assertions directly.
\end{proof}

The sufficient condition in this proposition is an error bound, not a
claim that every natural modification of a permutation statistic satisfies
it. Verifying the condition for a particular modification remains a
separate problem.

\section{Computing the constant without fitting finite permutations}
\label{cjm:sec:quadrature}

\subsection{A convergent discrete formula}
Because $D$ is positive-integer-valued, \eqref{cjm:eq:h} also gives
\begin{equation}
 \cjmic=1+\sum_{k=1}^\infty\left[
  (\sqrt{k+1}-\sqrt k)\ljP(D>k)
       -\frac{\mathfrak a}2\log\frac{k+1}{k}\right].
 \label{cjm:eq:h-series}
\end{equation}
The summands are $O(k^{-2})$, by \eqref{cjm:eq:limiting-tail}. Hence this is
an absolutely convergent series, with truncation error $O(N^{-1})$ after
$N$ terms. Its probabilities are rational coefficients of the algebraic
function $G$. It is an exact specification, although not the fastest
numerical route. (The summand is the one Part~III's proof of
\eqref{lj:eq:critical-moment} estimates by $\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}k^{-1}+O(k^{-2})$.)

Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
 \cjmic=\lim_{N\to\infty}
 \left(\ljE\sqrt{\min(D,N)}-\frac{\mathfrak a}2\log N\right).
 \label{cjm:eq:h-capped}
\end{equation}
This limit concerns a truncation of the \emph{limiting} variable $D$,
not a finite permutation variable $D_n$. Their centered constants differ
by the macroscopic contribution $\cjmac$.

\subsection{A regularized Laplace integral}
Set
\[
 \ell(t)=1-G(e^{-t}),\qquad t>0,
\]
and define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal R=\int_0^1\frac{\ell(t)-3\sqrt t}{t^{3/2}}\,\dd t
                 +\int_1^\infty\frac{\ell(t)}{t^{3/2}}\,\dd t.
 \label{cjm:eq:A-integral}
\end{equation}
Both integrals converge absolutely: \eqref{cjm:eq:pgf-expansion} gives
$\ell(t)=3\sqrt t+O(t)$ at zero, and $0\le\ell(t)\le1$ at infinity.

\begin{proposition}[A computable integral for the microscopic constant]
\label{cjm:prop:h-laplace}
With Euler's constant $\gamma$,
\begin{equation}
 \cjmic=\frac{\mathcal R}{2\sqrt\pi}
                  +\frac{\mathfrak a}2(\gamma+2\log2).
 \label{cjm:eq:h-laplace}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $0<p<1$, integrating by parts after the substitution $u=tx$ gives
\[
 x^p=\frac{p}{\Gamma(1-p)}
       \int_0^\infty(1-e^{-tx})t^{-p-1}\,\dd t.
\]
For $0<p<1/2$, Tonelli's theorem therefore yields
\begin{equation}
 \ljE D^p=\frac{p}{\Gamma(1-p)}
             \int_0^\infty\ell(t)t^{-p-1}\,\dd t.
 \label{cjm:eq:fractional-moment}
\end{equation}
Subtract $3\sqrt t$ on $(0,1)$. If $\mathfrak c(p)=p/\Gamma(1-p)$ and
\[
 \mathcal R(p)=\int_0^1[\ell(t)-3\sqrt t]t^{-p-1}\,\dd t
                 +\int_1^\infty\ell(t)t^{-p-1}\,\dd t,
\]
then $\mathcal R(p)$ is holomorphic for $0<\Re p<1$, with value
$\mathcal R$ at $p=1/2$, and
\[
 \ljE D^p=\mathfrak c(p)\mathcal R(p)+\frac{3\mathfrak c(p)}{1/2-p}.
\]
Compare this with \eqref{cjm:eq:limit-moment}:
\[
 \mathcal H(p)=\mathfrak c(p)\mathcal R(p)+\frac{3\mathfrak c(p)-\mathfrak ap}{1/2-p}.
\]
The numerator in the quotient vanishes at $p=1/2$. Its derivative there is
$\frac{\mathfrak a}2\psi(1/2)$, where $\psi=\Gamma'/\Gamma$, since
$\mathfrak c'(p)=\Gamma(1-p)^{-1}[1+p\psi(1-p)]$.
Consequently the quotient tends to $-\frac{\mathfrak a}2\psi(1/2)$.
The duplication formula for $\Gamma$, differentiated at $1/2$, gives
$\psi(1/2)=-\gamma-2\log2$. Finally
$\mathfrak c(1/2)=1/(2\sqrt\pi)$, proving \eqref{cjm:eq:h-laplace}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Stable evaluation near zero}
A naive evaluation of $1-G(e^{-t})-3\sqrt t$ loses digits near zero.
The verification code instead uses the algebraically equivalent form
\begin{equation}
 \ell(t)=\frac{s[12+(25+r)s+(11-r)s^2]}{4(1+2s)(1+s)^2},
 \quad s=\sqrt{-\operatorname{expm1}(-t)},\quad
 r=\sqrt{(1+s)/2}.
 \label{cjm:eq:ell-stable}
\end{equation}
Writing $t=v^2$, it subtracts $3v$ by the identity
\begin{equation}
 \ell(v^2)-3v=3(s-v)+
 \frac{s^2[(r-23)-(49+r)s-24s^2]}{4(1+2s)(1+s)^2}.
 \label{cjm:eq:ell-remainder}
\end{equation}
The factor $s-v$ is rationalized; the remaining exponential cancellation
can be removed by
\[
 e^{-x}-1+x=\frac{x^2}{2}\,{}_1F_1(1;3;-x),
\]
whose identity follows term by term from the exponential series. The
transformed integrand $2[\ell(v^2)-3v]/v^2$ has finite endpoint limit
$(1/\sqrt2-23)/2$.

For the second integral in \eqref{cjm:eq:A-integral}, the code uses
\[
 \int_1^\infty\ell(t)t^{-3/2}\,\dd t
      =2-\int_1^\infty G(e^{-t})t^{-3/2}\,\dd t.
\]
The last integrand decays exponentially. These rearrangements make the
reported numerical agreement meaningful; they are not an interval-based
error proof.

\centering
\caption{Constants evaluated from convergent integrals. The displayed
decimals are numerical approximations, not certified enclosures.}
\begin{tabular}{lr}
\toprule
Quantity & Approximation\\
\midrule
$\cjmic$ & $\phantom{-}0.367948538497933$\\
$\cjmac$ & $-1.310069952497335$\\
$c_\star=\cjmic+\cjmac$ & $-0.942121413999402$\\
$B_0=\cjmic-\mathfrak a$ & $-1.324620212145336$\\
$B_1=\mathfrak a+\cjmac$ & $\phantom{-}0.382498798145934$\\
$\mathfrak m(1/4)$ & $-2.575500874573932$\\

\section{Exact finite checks and numerical illustrations}
\label{cjm:sec:computation}

\subsection{Counting rather than sampling}
The manuscript's code computes exact integer distributions of $D_n$ through
$n=64$. It uses the endpoint-threshold recurrence derived in
\cref{cjm:app:recurrence}, not Monte Carlo sampling. Independent recursive
generation of every $132$-avoider through size ten agrees with the resulting
deficit histograms. For sizes through seven, a separate test of the defining
$132$ condition on all permutations verifies the recursive generator.

The verification run records:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lr}
\toprule
Check & Count or range\\
\midrule
Definition-based avoiders checked & $625$ (sizes $1$--$7$)\\
Generated avoiders checked against histograms & $23\,713$ (sizes $1$--$10$)\\
Endpoint-state equalities checked & $784$\\
Exact deficit distributions & sizes $1$--$64$\\
Exact PGF coefficients checked & $129$ (degrees $0$--$128$)\\
Symbolic algebraic identities & $5$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The integer totals equal the Catalan numbers at every computed size.
The PGF coefficients are exact rational numbers; multiplication by the
algebraic denominator is checked coefficient by coefficient, and the
coefficient of $z$ is exactly $7/48$. (Part~III's
\path{data/03-largest-jump-limit_probabilities.csv} records the same
probabilities $\ljP(D=k)$ for $k\le256$, computed there independently.)

\subsection{Independent numerical regressions}
The constant calculation was repeated at 40- and 70-decimal working
precision. The two computations agreed to more than 29 decimal places in
the recorded regression check. The elementary formula for $\cjmac$ was
checked against a separately evaluated convergent integral. The value of
$\cjmic$ was also compared with the symmetric removable limit of the
regularized fractional-moment expression at $p=1/2\pm10^{-6}$; its observed
difference was about $2.68\times10^{-12}$.

A different, slower check uses the first 128 exact PGF coefficients in
\eqref{cjm:eq:h-capped}. It gives $0.377930026226763$ for the truncated
microscopic constant, about $0.00998149$ above $\cjmic$, consistent with its
$O(1/N)$ truncation scale. No interval enclosure is inferred from that
comparison. The pole finite part of $\mathfrak m$ was also checked
numerically against $B_1$, and $\mathfrak m(1)=\mathfrak a$ was verified at
high precision as a normalization check. (That is Part~IV's mean constant,
$\mathfrak m_1=3/\sqrt\pi$.)

\subsection{What the finite data actually show}

for display. The last row lists the proved limits, not fitted estimates.}

$n$ & $\ljE\sqrt{D_n}-\frac3{2\sqrt\pi}\log n$ &
$n^{1/4}(\ljE D_n^{1/4}-\ljE D^{1/4})$ & $\ljE^0_n\vartheta_n$\\
\midrule
8  & $-0.1296061114$ & $-2.0053474893$ & $0.5061596109$\\
16 & $-0.2961230527$ & $-2.0986386677$ & $0.5651776479$\\
32 & $-0.4402451849$ & $-2.1871612273$ & $0.5855553734$\\
64 & $-0.5577086463$ & $-2.2648950027$ & $0.5922137801$\\

$\infty$ & $-0.9421214140$ & $-2.5755008746$ & $0.5$\\

These sizes are not close enough to identify the limiting constant by
inspection. In particular, the critical-biased mean scale is moving away
from $1/2$ in the displayed range even though the theorem proves its
convergence to $1/2$. The data test the implementations and illustrate slow
finite-size behavior. They are not evidence sufficient to prove the
asymptotic formulas, and no coefficient in a theorem was fitted to them.

figures/06-critical-moments-*.pdf            The three figures of this part
06-critical-moments-SOURCES.md               Pinned source paths, literature scope
06-critical-moments-PROOF_STATUS.md          Trust boundary and statement ledger
\end{lstlisting}
The scripts are shipped byte-identical to the delivery and still use the
delivered names: they read and write \path{data/exact_distributions.json},
\path{data/pgf_coefficients.json}, \path{data/exact_checks.json},
\path{data/symbolic_checks.json}, \path{data/constants.json},
\path{data/finite_moments.csv} and unprefixed files in \path{figures/}, and
the build script looks for an \texttt{article.tex} beside itself. The
report's README gives a recipe that restores the delivered layout in a
scratch copy; there, the manuscript's instructions apply,
\begin{verbatim}
python code/exact_counts.py --max-n 64 --pgf-terms 128
python code/verify_symbolic.py
python code/numerics.py
python code/figures.py
\end{verbatim}
and rerunning overwrites only the copy's recorded outputs. The counting
program uses the Python standard library. The symbolic, quadrature and
figure programs additionally use SymPy, mpmath, NumPy and Matplotlib; the
versions used are recorded in \path{data/06-critical-moments-requirements.txt}.
None of these computations is connected to a proof-assistant checker. Their
output certifies only that the indicated finite assertions passed in the
execution environment. The retained figures make Python unnecessary for
rebuilding the article.

\section{What has been settled, and what has not}
\label{cjm:sec:scope}
The two explicit questions answered here are convergence of the centered
critical half-moment and the first correction to every fixed positive
subcritical moment (\cref{mj:sec:q-centered}). Their proofs are consequences
of exactly the three inputs of \cref{cjm:sec:inputs}. The new
logarithmic-scale statements organize the same information into a
distributional picture, and the general theorem makes that mechanism reusable
outside the permutation problem.

Several stronger assertions would exceed the proof. There is no new local
limit for individual lattice masses, no exact leading constant in the
original total-variation error, and no description of the adjacency-bound
transition at $m=n/2$. The coefficient function $\mathfrak m(p)$ is
analytically continued below $1/2$, but the moment theorem is not extended
to $p\le0$ on that basis. A continuation of a coefficient formula does not
by itself continue an asymptotic theorem.

The complex crossover is locally uniform for fixed compact sets of
$\lambda$, not uniform as $|\lambda|\to\infty$. For fixed
supercritical $p$, the present transfer theorem does not supply an
$o(1)$-accurate second-order constant. The numerical constants are exact
only through their convergent definitions; their printed decimal
approximations have not been enclosed by interval arithmetic. Discrete
biased laws do not converge to their continuous limit in total variation,
and the negative endpoint coefficient $B_0$ has no positive-measure
interpretation. No elementary closed form, rationality or transcendence
result is claimed for the microscopic constant.

Finally, no proof of Part~V has been checked by Lean or Rocq, and neither
the advance relative to Parts~III and~IV nor a bounded literature search
constitutes external validation of publication priority. These limits do
not weaken the stated abstract implications; they identify precisely the
boundary of the contribution.

\section{Further research questions and proposed routes}
\label{cjm:sec:future}
The manuscript proposes ten questions. Four overlap with questions of
Part~IV; they are printed here with pointers to Part~IV's text, which is
not repeated.

\subsection{A rate for the centered half-moment}
The exact decomposition reduces the rate problem to
$\mathcal J_n(1/2)-\mathcal J(1/2)$, up to an $O(n^{-1})$ microscopic tail
error. Can one prove an explicit bound, or an asymptotic equivalent, for that
weighted macroscopic discrepancy? A promising route is to quantify Part~III's
stopping-word or Part~IV's maximum-decomposition argument in a weighted
integral norm rather than demand a uniform relative tail estimate at every
scale. Such a result would immediately sharpen both \eqref{cjm:eq:critical}
and \eqref{cjm:eq:BL-bound}. (This refines, at the critical exponent,
Part~IV's question on second-order moment asymptotics,
\cref{cjm:sec:mj-second}.)

\subsection{A local limit and the exact total-variation constant}
The derivative $\rho(u)$ suggests a lattice approximation for
$\ljP(D_n=d)$ when $d/n$ stays inside $(0,1/2)$. Proving it requires more
than differentiation of the existing tail limit. Exact endpoint-state
counts may identify a useful discrete difference equation. Even a local
bulk theorem would not alone determine the total-variation constant; one
must control all remaining scales and the signs of probability-mass
errors. Part~V does not identify that constant. (These are Part~IV's
questions \cref{cjm:sec:mj-local} and \cref{cjm:sec:mj-tv}, asked there
by both of its manuscripts.)

\subsection{The half-size threshold}
The leading bulk profile vanishes at $u=1/2$, corresponding to adjacency
bound $m=n/2$. What scale and limiting function describe the transition
there? The endpoint expansion in \eqref{cjm:eq:bulk-profile} is linear from
below, but gives no order on or beyond the threshold. A refined terminal
split analysis, retaining lattice offsets and two-large-block events,
seems more suitable than the present integrated method. (This is Part~IV's
question \cref{cjm:sec:mj-half}.)

\subsection{Wider moving-exponent windows}
Can \eqref{cjm:eq:crossover} be made uniform for
$|\lambda|\le L_n$ with $L_n\to\infty$? Such a theorem would match the
critical window to fixed subcritical or supercritical regimes.
The general proof exposes two issues: controlling the range of $p$ in
$\mathcal J_n(p)-\mathcal J(p)$, and ensuring that its error remains small
after multiplication by $e^\lambda$. A quantitative estimate from the first
question is an essential ingredient.

\subsection{Negative exponents and logarithms without critical bias}
For $p\le0$, the integral involving $\mathfrak b(u)$ is no longer controlled
merely by boundedness at zero. The explicit limiting profile has a better
small-$u$ expansion, but the finite discrepancies need a matching uniform
estimate. Can one prove that stronger estimate, and thereby determine
corrections to $\ljE\log D_n$ or $\ljE D_n^p$ for negative $p$? This is a
separate boundary-layer problem, not an automatic consequence of
\eqref{cjm:eq:regularized-beta}.

\subsection{Geometry of a permutation under critical bias}
The theorem identifies the scale of the deficit but not the conditioned
permutation. Under square-root bias, condition further on
$\log D_n/\log n\in(s,t)$. What happens to the location of the largest
jump, the relative ascent/descent contribution, or the sizes along the
maximum-decomposition path? A joint extension of Part~IV's marked
macroscopic measure (\cref{mj:thm:marked}) could lead to a scale-indexed
geometric limit. (This is the critical-bias counterpart of Part~IV's
question \cref{cjm:sec:mj-geometric}, which conditions on a macroscopic
deficit instead.)

\subsection{Other Catalan models and other tail exponents}
The abstract theorem permits arbitrary $\alpha>0$. Which statistics on
Catalan trees, other pattern classes, or conditioned branching structures
satisfy its three hypotheses? The crucial target is a uniform
$O(n^{-\alpha})$ survival error, not merely convergence in distribution.
The counterexample in \cref{cjm:prop:counterexample} provides a diagnostic
for proposed generalizations: hidden intermediate-scale mass must be
excluded.

\subsection{Higher-order endpoint corrections}
The first signed correction consists of two point masses. A next term
might involve derivatives of endpoint evaluations, a regular measure on
$[0,1]$, or slower scales, depending on the rate and shape of
$g_n-\mathfrak b$. Determine the appropriate test-function topology and the
next expansion before guessing its support. The direct three-integral proof
provides a natural starting point, with its $1/\log n$ terms still visible.

\subsection{Certified constants and their analytic nature}
The formulas for $\cjmic$ and $c_\star$ are absolutely convergent and
amenable to interval quadrature. A useful computational continuation would
supply a rigorous decimal enclosure, with explicit small-$t$ and large-$t$
error bounds. A more theoretical question asks whether $\cjmic$ admits a
simpler special-function expression. No elementary closed form, rationality
or transcendence claim is suggested by the present decimal computation.

\subsection{Formalization in the repository}
A manageable first Lean target is the abstract theorem for real exponents
on a fixed compact interval, followed by the critical half-moment and the
bounded-Lipschitz estimate. The proof uses tail integration, dominated
convergence, elementary power integrals, and uniform bounds; it need not
initially formalize complex holomorphy. The application then requires
formal counterparts of the three probabilistic inputs from Parts~III
and~IV. Keeping these interfaces separate would make proof status explicit
and permit independent replacement of any input argument. (Part~IV's
formalization targets are in \cref{cjm:sec:mj-formal}; no Lean or Rocq
declaration for any part of this report exists.)

\section{The exact endpoint recurrence used by the checks}
\label{cjm:app:recurrence}
This section was the manuscript's Appendix~A. It gives the finite
combinatorics used by the verification code. The recurrence is inherited
from the endpoint-state approach of Mayama--Akita~\cite{MayamaAkita} and is
Part~I's \cref{prop:endpoint}, in the coefficient form Part~IV prints as
\eqref{mj:eq:DP}; the first-entry count is Part~III's
\eqref{lj:eq:first-count}. The manuscript derives both again, by the same
maximum decomposition as Parts~I and~III, to make its checks reproducible
independently of the repository's code, and its derivation is kept here in
Part~IV's notation (\cref{cjm:tab:notation}).

\subsection{Maximum decomposition and first entries}
Every nonempty $132$-avoider has a unique decomposition
\begin{equation}
 \pi=(\sigma+b)\,n\,\tau,
 \qquad \sigma\in\Av_a(132),\quad\tau\in\Av_b(132),\quad a+b=n-1.
 \label{cjm:eq:root}
\end{equation}
All left entries must exceed all right entries: otherwise a left entry,
the maximum, and a larger right entry form a $132$ pattern. Conversely,
this condition and avoidance in the two blocks ensure avoidance globally.
It follows that the recursive generator is exhaustive and has no
duplicates, and yields the Catalan recurrence. (This is Part~I's maximum
decomposition, \cref{sec:endpoints}.)

Let $\mathcal N_n(u)$ count unrestricted size-$n$ avoiders with first
deficiency $n-\pi_1\le u$. Set $\mathcal N_n(u)=0$ for $u<0$ and
$\mathcal N_0(u)=1$ for $u\ge0$. For $1\le f\le n$, the number with first
entry $f$ is
\begin{equation}
 N_{n,f}=\frac{n-f+1}{n}\binom{n+f-2}{n-1},
 \label{cjm:eq:first-entry}
\end{equation}
Part~III's \eqref{lj:eq:first-count}. Here is the manuscript's
generating-function proof, which also checks the endpoint convention. Write
$\Cat(x)=\sum_{n\ge0}C_nx^n$, so $\Cat(x)=1+x\Cat(x)^2$, and for $j\ge0$
put
\[
 \mathcal G_j(x)=\sum_{n\ge j+1}N_{n,n-j}x^n.
\]
When the first deficiency is zero, the maximum is first and the rest is
an arbitrary avoider, so $\mathcal G_0=x\Cat(x)$. When the first deficiency is
$j\ge1$, the left block in \eqref{cjm:eq:root} is nonempty. Its first
entry has deficiency $j-1$ inside that block, while the right block is
arbitrary. Thus $\mathcal G_j=x\Cat(x)\mathcal G_{j-1}$, giving
\[
 \mathcal G_j(x)=(x\Cat(x))^{j+1}.
\]
Lagrange inversion applied to $\Cat-1=x\Cat^2$ gives, for $k\ge0$,
\[
 [x^k]\Cat(x)^{j+1}
 =\frac{j+1}{2k+j+1}\binom{2k+j+1}{k}.
\]
Substitute $k=n-j-1$ and simplify the binomial coefficient. The result
is \eqref{cjm:eq:first-entry} with $f=n-j$. (Part~III reaches
$(x\Cat)^{j+1}$ through the bivariate series \eqref{lj:eq:first-gf} and
\eqref{lj:eq:deficiency-fixed}; the manuscript's one-line recursion in $j$
is the same decomposition read one deficiency at a time.) Thus
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal N_n(u)=\sum_{j=0}^{\min(u,n-1)}
       \frac{j+1}{n}\binom{2n-j-2}{n-1},\qquad n\ge1.
 \label{cjm:eq:h-count}
\end{equation}
The program checks that each complete row sums to $C_n$.

\subsection{Two endpoint thresholds}
Let $T^{(m)}_{u,v}(n)$ count size-$n$ avoiders with every adjacent
difference at most $m$, first deficiency at most $u$, and last deficiency at
most $v$ (Part~I's $T^{(m)}_{p,q}(n)$ with finite thresholds). Negative
thresholds give zero, $T^{(m)}_{u,v}(1)=1$ for nonnegative thresholds, and
thresholds above $n-1$ are clipped to $n-1$.

For $n\ge2$ one has
\begin{align}
 T^{(m)}_{u,v}(n)={}&T^{(m)}_{u-1,m-1}(n-1)\notag\\
 &+\sum_{k=1}^{\min(m,n-1)}c_{k,u}\,
       T^{(m)}_{m-k,v-k}(n-k),
 \label{cjm:eq:threshold-recurrence}\\
 c_{k,u}&=\begin{cases}1,&k=1,\\\mathcal N_{k-1}(u-1),&k\ge2,\end{cases}
 \notag
\end{align}
which is \eqref{mj:eq:DP}. The unrestricted adjacency count is
$a_n^{(m)}=T^{(m)}_{n-1,n-1}(n)$.

\begin{proof}[Proof of \eqref{cjm:eq:threshold-recurrence}]
If the maximum is last, deleting it changes the first deficiency bound to
$u-1$. The final jump has size $n-\pi_{n-1}$, so the old last deficiency
must be at most $m-1$. The new last deficiency is zero, explaining the
first term.

Otherwise let the maximum be at position $k$, so the right block has size
$b=n-k\ge1$. The jump from the maximum to its following entry is at least
$k$, hence $k\le m$. The left block has size $k-1\le m-1$, so its internal
jumps and its jump to the maximum automatically meet the adjacency bound.
When nonempty, its first deficiency must be at most $u-1$; it contributes
$\mathcal N_{k-1}(u-1)$. If it is empty, the first entry is the maximum, and
the coefficient is one.

The jump from the maximum to the first entry $f$ of the standardized right
block has size $n-f=k+(b-f)$, so its first deficiency is at most $m-k$.
The final deficiency of the whole permutation is $k$ plus the right-block
last deficiency, so that bound is $v-k$. This gives the summand.
Uniqueness of \eqref{cjm:eq:root} makes all cases disjoint.
\end{proof}

The manuscript's code uses this recurrence for every size, without the
faster unrestricted-state shortcut \eqref{mj:eq:unrestrictedDP} of
Part~IV's \path{code/05-macroscopic-deficits-model.py}, which it inspected.
It is therefore intended as a finite verification implementation, not a
claim of optimal enumeration complexity. It explicitly clears the
memoization cache when the adjacency bound changes.

\section{Proof and computation audit}
\label{cjm:app:audit}
This section was the manuscript's Appendix~B.
\begin{longtable}{>{\raggedright}p{.29\linewidth}>{\raggedright}p{.30\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.31\linewidth}}
\toprule
Statement & Dependency & Verification status\\
\midrule
\endhead
Algebraic limiting law and tail & Part~III, \cref{lj:thm:main,lj:thm:tail-main} & Adopted theorem of Part~III; not formalized\\
Uniform survival error & Part~III's total-variation theorem, \eqref{lj:eq:main-tv} & Immediate half-line consequence; Part~III not formalized\\
Bulk profile & Part~IV, \cref{mj:thm:profile} & Adopted theorem of Part~IV; not formalized\\
Uniform moment transfer & Three abstract hypotheses & Complete proof in \cref{cjm:sec:transfer}\\
Centered half-moment & Transfer theorem and explicit profile & Complete deduction; convergent exact constant\\
Subcritical correction & Transfer theorem and regularization & Complete deduction; negative sign proved\\
Complex critical window & Locally uniform transfer & Complete proof; compact windows only\\
Signed endpoint measure & Three abstract hypotheses & Complete proof in dual bounded-Lipschitz norm\\
Tilted limits and logarithmic powers & Endpoint theorem & Complete proofs by normalization and test functions\\
Intermediate-scale counterexample & Truncated Pareto mixture & Explicit construction and proof\\
Exact finite counts & Endpoint recurrence and independent generation & Integer regression checks through the stated ranges\\
Constant decimals & Convergent quadrature formulas & Precision and alternate-form checks; not interval certified\\
Lean/Rocq endpoint & Not supplied & No formal verification claimed\\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}

An audit should check in particular the following possible failure points.
The leading tail coefficient is $\mathfrak a=3/\sqrt\pi$, but the critical
logarithm coefficient is $\mathfrak a/2$. The finite part of $\mathfrak m(p)$
at its pole is $B_1=\mathfrak a+\cjmac$, not $\cjmac$. The microscopic
constant $\cjmic$ and the finite-permutation constant $c_\star$ differ. The
negative endpoint coefficient is a signed correction, not a negative
probability. The complex moment theorem has an explicit strip, and its
fixed-supercritical error cannot be silently strengthened. Finally, the
finite numerical checks have no bearing on the validity of dominated
convergence beyond their stated regression role. The incomplete beta
expression is regularized below $p=1/2$; no divergent integral is treated as
an ordinary integral, and complex powers always use the real logarithm of a
positive base (\path{06-critical-moments-PROOF_STATUS.md}).

not establish that the abstract transfer principle, the constants, or related
conclusions have never appeared elsewhere. There is no claim of exhaustive
bibliographic coverage, external peer review, or established publication
priority; claims of progress are relative to the pinned report. No complete
report or external paper is redistributed.

\appendix
\section{Exact small-bound algebra}
\label{app:algebra}
For $m=3$, the certified generating function is $A_3(x)=P_3(x)/Q_3(x)$, where
\begin{align*}
 P_3(x)={}&1-x-x^2+x^3+4x^4-4x^6-3x^7-3x^8\\
          &-5x^9-3x^{10}+2x^{11}+2x^{12},\\
 Q_3(x)={}&(x-1)(x+1)
 (x^5-x^4-x^3+x^2-2x+1)\\
          &\hspace{12mm}\cdot(x^6+x^5+x^4+2x^3+x^2-1).
\end{align*}
For $m=4$, the denominator factors as
\begin{align*}
 Q_4(x)={}&(x-1)\,D_U(x)\,D_V(x),\\
 D_U(x)={}&x^{10}+x^8+x^7-6x^5-x^4-x^3-x^2-x+1,\\
 D_V(x)={}&x^{12}-x^{11}-x^{10}-x^9+5x^8+4x^7+4x^6\\
          &+4x^5+5x^4+3x^3+x^2-1.
\end{align*}
Both displayed full denominators have constant term one. The numerator
of $A_4$, listed from constant term upwards, has coefficient vector
\[
\begin{gathered}
(1,-1,-1,-1,2,9,5,-3,-14,-10,-13,-23,\\
 -31,-11,21,32,21,1,-3,-5,-3,-3).
\end{gathered}
\]
It has degree 21 and $Q_4$ has degree 23. Thus the usual coefficient
recurrence from $Q_4A_4=P_4$ is valid for all $n\ge23$ when written using
23 preceding terms. The complete ascending coefficient arrays, full state
certificates, and exact checks are in the JSON files.

\section{Reproduction and certificate formats}
\label{app:reproduction}
The archive is arranged as follows:
\begin{lstlisting}
article.tex, article.pdf       Main editable source and report
README.md, requirements.txt   Reproduction instructions
code/model.py                 Exact endpoint model and combinatorics
code/compute.py               Numerical proposals and exact symbolic solver
code/verify.py                Standard-library independent exact verifier
code/plot_results.py          Recreates the two separate figures
data/sequences.csv            n = 0..200, m = 1..12
data/growth_constants.csv     Components for m = 2..20
data/perron_certificates.json Rational bounds and positive integer vectors
data/symbolic_certificate_m*.json  Polynomial state certificates, m = 1..4
data/generating_functions.json     Reduced rational functions, m = 1..4
data/large_m_scalar_bounds.csv     Scalar evaluations through m = 1000000
\end{lstlisting}
[Added 28 September 2026, batch 38: Part~II's code, data and figures carry
the prefix \path{02-centered-remainder-} and are listed in
\cref{crem:sec:reproduce}.
Added 29 September 2026, batch 43: Part~III's carry the prefix
\path{03-largest-jump-} and are listed in \cref{lj:sec:reproduce}.
Added 29 September 2026, batch 52: Part~IV's carry the prefixes
\path{04-macroscopic-jump-} and \path{05-macroscopic-deficits-} and are
listed in \cref{mj:sec:reproduce}.
Added 30 September 2026, batch 64: Part~V's carry the prefix
\path{06-critical-moments-} and are listed in \cref{cjm:sec:reproduce}.]

The primary reproducibility command needs no third-party Python packages:
\begin{lstlisting}
python code/verify.py
\end{lstlisting}
To regenerate numerical and symbolic proposals, install the packages in
\texttt{requirements.txt} and run
\begin{lstlisting}
python code/compute.py --max-m 20 --symbolic-max 4 --large-scalars
python code/verify.py
python code/plot_results.py
\end{lstlisting}
The generator deliberately raises an exception if it cannot convert a
numerical proposal to a valid exact certificate. A floating-point result
alone is never accepted as a proof of a spectral inequality.

To build the report from the supplied figures:
\begin{lstlisting}
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
\end{lstlisting}
The third pass settles page references and the table of contents if needed.
The TeX source uses standard packages from a current full TeX Live
installation; no font files are distributed.

The exact recurrence uses $(m+1)^2$ endpoint states and at most $m+1$
terms per state. Computing all coefficients through length $N$ therefore
uses $O(Nm^3)$ integer multiply-add operations, after coefficient
precomputation, and $O(Nm^2)$ stored integers in the supplied straightforward
implementation. These are arithmetic-operation counts, not bit-complexity
bounds. Exact values have growing bit length. A rolling buffer can reduce
storage when the complete coefficient table is not needed.

Perron certificates store integers $p,D$ for each radius endpoint $p/D$,
and a positive integer vector in the state order generated by the verifier.
Symbolic certificates store all polynomial coefficients in ascending degree.
The constant term of each common denominator is checked to be one.
The certificate identities prove infinitely many coefficients because the
formal system has a unique solution; the finite coefficient checks are
additional consistency tests.

\section{Source status and proof audit}
\label{app:status}
The literature check was performed on September 19, 2026. The inspected
primary items are the arXiv v1 versions listed below, including Nadler's
Conjecture~2 and Mayama--Akita's Theorem~4.14 and Problem~P3. The latter
paper's displayed numerical table and statement that its component comparison
was unproved were also inspected in the PDF. Targeted searches used the
paper titles and identifiers together with strict monotonicity, growth
constants, and asymptotic refinements. No later solution was located in
that search. This establishes the specific source-based status used here;
it is not an assertion that every possible publication has been searched.

The logical dependencies of the new claims are particularly short.
The strict-growth theorem uses the exact endpoint recurrence, the two
irreducible components, their root characterization, the proper-principal
Perron lemma, and the explicit shift of thresholds. This report includes
proofs of those prerequisites. The asymptotic theorem instead uses the
maximum-decomposition majorant, the injective skew-block construction,
the first-entry Catalan formula, and the truncated-tail lemma. In particular,
no numerical fit, unproved component-ordering pattern, or formal passage
from finite checks to all $m$ is used.

The work has not been formalized in a proof assistant. The integer and
polynomial certificates are executable verification of the stated finite
algebraic claims; they should not be described as a formal verification
of the analytic limiting argument or of the all-$m$ theorem.
